*** is there a postfix mail hook that uses bash to run commands on each incoming mail? I want to run a plain gpg encrypt with a single recipient with a public key


#+begin_reasoning
The user asks about Postfix mail hooks/filters that use bash to run commands on each incoming mail, wanting to run gpg encryption with a single recipient public key. Let me think about what I know and maybe search for current info.

Key approaches:
1. Postfix `mailbox_command` (or `mailbox_command_maps`) — but that's for local delivery (LDA), and only works for local(8) delivery. Can't easily pipe to gpg for outbound... actually it can.
2. `content_filter` with `filter` transport using `pipe` — spam filter style.
3. Postfix `notify_classes`, `always_bcc`.
4. `recipient_bcc_maps` + a custom transport.
5. Best modern approach: `smtpd_milters` / non_smtpd_milters with OpenDKIM... but for GPG, better use a milter like `gpg-mailgate` (Python) or `postfix-gpg-milter`. There's the well-known **gpg-mailgate** project (Python) which encrypts incoming mail to GPG keys. Also **postfix-gpg-milter** (Go).
6. bash approach: use a `pipe` transport with a bash script; mail is fed on stdin, you gpg --encrypt --recipient, then hand off to another MTA or deliver.

Important gotchas: Postfix pipes mail without envelope info unless you pass args like `${sender} ${recipient}` — need to use `pipe` transport with flags and arguments `argv=/path/script ${sender} ${recipient}`. Also need to avoid loops (content_filter loop detection), and re-inject via sendmail with `-f` and `-i`.

Let me perhaps search for actual projects since the user asks "is there a postfix mail hook". Let me do a search to ground it, and maybe fetch a README.

Let me search for gpg-mailgate and postfix-gpg-milter.

I should be concise but useful. Let me do searches in parallel.

Actually let me be careful about tool use — I'll do a couple searches.

Let me recall the details of the bash/pipe approach precisely:

master.cf:
```
gpgscan   unix  -       n       n       -       -       pipe
  flags=Rq user=gpgmail argv=/usr/local/bin/gpg-wrap.sh ${sender} ${recipient}
```
Then in main.cf:
```
content_filter = gpgscan
```
Wait — content_filter requires the filter to re-inject. Actually with `content_filter = gpgscan:dummy` it's a simple content filter.

Hmm, but there's a subtlety: content_filter with pipe transport — you must re-inject the mail back into Postfix via sendmail, otherwise it gets dropped. And to avoid the filter looping, you send it to a different port (e.g., 10026) that has `-o content_filter=` and `-o receive_override_options=no_address_mappings`.

That's the classic "simple content filter" setup from Postfix's FILTER_README:

master.cf:
```
127.0.0.1:10025 inet n  -  n  -  -  smtpd
  -o content_filter=
  -o receive_override_options=no_header_body_checks
  -o smtpd_helo_restrictions=
  -o smtpd_client_restrictions=
  ...
```
And filter:
```
filter    unix  -       n       n       -       10      pipe
  flags=Rq user=filter argv=/usr/bin/gpg-filter.sh -f ${sender} -- ${recipient}
```
main.cf:
```
content_filter = filter:dummy
```

The script:
```bash
#!/bin/bash
# args: -f sender -- recipient...
sendmail=/usr/sbin/sendmail
shift  # -f
sender="$1"; shift
shift  # --
recipients=("$@")

tmp=$(mktemp)
cat > "$tmp"
gpg --batch --yes --trust-model always --encrypt --recipient KEYID --output "$tmp.gpg" "$tmp"
"$sendmail" -i -f "$sender" -- "${recipients[@]}" < "$tmp.gpg"
rm -f "$tmp" "$tmp.gpg"
```

Alternatively simpler: use `mailbox_command` for local delivery — that's a real "hook" that runs on each incoming mail for local mailboxes. `mailbox_command = /usr/local/bin/gpg-deliver.sh` with `DEFAULT` for original recipient. Postfix runs it with the mail on stdin and args: `mailbox_command $SHELL $EXTENSION $DOMAIN $USER $LOCAL $RECIPIENT $DETAIL $MAILBOX`... Let me recall mailbox_command receives arguments: `${SHELL} ${HOME} ${USER} ${EXTENSION} ${DOMAIN} ${LOCAL} ${RECIPIENT} ${DETAIL} ${MAILBOX}`... Actually documented: "The command is executed with the following environment: SHELL, HOME, USER, LOGNAME, EXTENSION, DOMAIN, RECIPIENT, LOCAL, etc." and arguments: `$SHELL $EXTENSION $DOMAIN $USER $LOCAL $RECIPIENT $DETAIL $MAILBOX`.

Hmm, precise: Postfix local(8) mailbox_command: "The command is run with a standard input... arguments: the command is executed as `command $SHELL $EXTENSION $DOMAIN $USER $LOCAL $RECIPIENT $DETAIL $MAILBOX`". Let me not overstate—I can say "it's documented in local(8)".

Better/clean: **milter** approach. gpg-mailgate is the classic (Python, though). postfix-gpg-milter is Go and provides an SMTP milter that looks up keys by recipient (via gpg keyring or keyserver/WKD).

Also relevant: **Simple approach** if you only want to archive-copy encrypted: `always_bcc` + a pipe.

Let me verify gpg-mailgate still exists. I'll search.

Also worth noting: Postfix's `mailbox_command` only applies to local(8) delivery, not virtual(8) mailboxes. So if using virtual mailboxes, pipe transport or milter is needed.

Let me run searches.

  "contentType": "text/html",
  "title": "jnphilipp's log : Auto encrypt all Incoming Email with postfix",
  "retrievalMethod": "request",
  "truncated": false,
  "content": "Auto encrypt all Incoming Email with postfix\n\n                    Monday, 30. December 2019\n                in\n                    projects\n                        ::\n                    #admin,                     #coding,                     #gpgmail,                     #pgp\n\n        I am running my own mail server for a while now. Since the beginning I was thinking about how to store the mails encrypted, so that no one can read the mails with access to the server. The solution I came up with is relative easy to setup and is based upon OpenPGP/GnuPGP.\nThe basic idea is to take incoming mail before it is stored and encrypt it. I'm running postfix, which has the option to filter queued mails with external content filters. A content filter gets a mail via stdin, does whatever it needs to do and either rejects a mail or put it back into the mail queue.\nI wrote a relativ simple Python script that takes a mail from stdin, processes it and then writes it back to stdout. The script can either decrypt, encrypt, sign or sign and encrypt a mail. It also tries to protect the mail headers following the memoryhole specs and supports Thunderbirds/Enigmails encrypted subject feature. The drawback is that Enigmail only supports the encrypted header from the memoryhole specs and other mail clients don't support them at all.  For the content_filter in postfix I wrote a Bash script, that will resend the encrypted mail to put it back into the mail queue. The scripts can be found on GitHub.\nSetup\n\nInstall gpgmail\n\nAdd a new user:\nadduser --shell /bin/false --home /home/gpgmail --disabled-password --disabled-login --gecos \"\" gpgmail\n\nCreate .gnupg folder and change permissions:\nmkdir /home/gpgmail/.gnupg\nchown gpgmail:gpgmail /home/gpgmail/.gnupg/\nchmod 700 /home/gpgmail/.gnupg/\n\nIf mails should not just get encrypted but also signed, create a new key pair:\nsudo -u gpgmail /usr/bin/gpg --homedir=/home/gpgmail/.gnupg --expert --full-gen-key\n\nImport public keys and chnage trust:\nsudo -u gpgmail /usr/bin/gpg --homedir=/home/gpgmail/.gnupg --import /home/gpgmail/pubkey.asc\nsudo -u gpgmail /usr/bin/gpg --homedir=/home/gpgmail/.gnupg --edit-key <KEY> trust save\nsudo -u gpgmail /usr/bin/gpg --homedir=/home/gpgmail/.gnupg --edit-key <KEY> trust quit\n\nEdit /etc/postfix/master.cf\n:::unixconfig\nsmtp          inet  n       -       y       -       -       smtpd -o content_filter=gpgmail-pipe\nsmtps         inet  n       -       y       -       -       smtpd -o content_filter=gpgmail-pipe\nsubmission    inet  n       -       y       -       -       smtpd -o content_filter=gpgmail-pipe\ngpgmail-pipe  unix  -       n       n       -       -       pipe\n  flags=Rq user=gpgmail argv=/usr/bin/gpgmail-postfix sign-encrypt gnupghome=/home/gpgmail/.gnupg key=<KEY_ID> passphrase=<PASSPHRASE> encrypt-subject -oi -f ${sender} ${recipient}\n\nRestart postfix.\n\nSources\n\nhttps://www.grepular.com/Automatically_Encrypting_all_Incoming_Email\nhttp://kacangbawang.com/encrypting-stored-email-with-postfix/\nhttps://blog.jak-linux.org/2019/06/13/encrypted-email-storage/"
}

{
  "url": "https://www.grepular.com/Automatically_Encrypting_all_Incoming_Email",
  "finalUrl": "https://www.grepular.com/Automatically_Encrypting_all_Incoming_Email",
  "contentType": "text/html; charset=utf-8",
  "title": "Automatically Encrypting all Incoming Email - grepular.com",
  "retrievalMethod": "request",
  "truncated": false,
  "content": "Automatically Encrypting all Incoming EmailPublished 15 years ago3 minute read\nWritten in 2011, 15 years ago. Technology moves fast, so some details may be out of date.TL;DR: gpgit, my Perl script that Exim runs as a transport filter, PGP encrypts every incoming email with my public key before it reaches my IMAP server.\nPart 1 of 2 in the series Automatically encrypting incoming emailAutomatically Encrypting all Incoming Email 15 years agoAutomatically Encrypting all Incoming Email, Part 2 15 years ago\n\nSecuring your email so nobody else can read it is very important. Even if you\nhave, \"nothing to hide,\" the security of your email is paramount; just about\nevery website out there with a login can reset passwords via email. If somebody\ncan read your email, they can effectively take over your complete online identity.\nI host my own email. I have an IMAP server, and I access my email from my laptop, PC and\nphone. If any one of these four hosts is compromised, then my email, and by\nextension my online identity, is also compromised. There is an obvious solution\nto this problem in\nPGP, but most of the emails I receive aren't even PGP signed, let\nalone encrypted. So I've started encrypting them myself as soon as they arrive.\nThe MTA I am using is\nExim, although the encryption methodology I am using\nprobably translates to other servers quite well too. I built a small Perl\napplication which takes an email on STDIN and spits out the encrypted version on\nSTDOUT. You can download it from my Github repository\nhere. I integrated it with Exim using\nthe\ntransport_filter\noption. I simply updated my transport which does local delivery with the following:\ntransport_filter = /etc/exim4/scripts/gpgit.pl my.email.address@example.com\n\nThe user that the delivery runs as needs to have GnuPG set\nup and must have the public key for my.email.address@example.com (in this case)\ninstalled. The email address in the arguments exists purely to identify which\npublic key to encrypt as. Because we're only encrypting, and not signing, the\nhost running the MTA doesn't need the private key installed at all.\nThe script uses inline encryption when the message contains a single text/plain\npart. If the message contains more than one part or something other than\ntext/plain it uses PGP/MIME instead. The reason I have done it this way is\nbecause I'm using K-9 on my Android phone\nwith APG, and K-9 doesn't yet support PGP/MIME. If a message is already\nencrypted, it is not re-encrypted.\nSo what does this get me? If somebody steals my laptop or phone, they can't\naccess my email from my IMAP clients local store because it's all encrypted and\nmy private PGP key is password protected. If somebody guesses my IMAP password,\nor uses an exploit to gain access to my account, they can't read my email\nbecause what they retrieve is encrypted. If somebody at my hosting provider or a\ngovernment wants to access my email, simply taking my server wont help them.\nThere are obvious caveats to this solution though. If somebody gets root on my\nserver or access to the network, they can sniff the emails on the way in before\nthey're encrypted. This wont help them access historical email which was\nencrypted before they started though. Also, if somebody installs a trojan on my\nlaptop it's game over; they can grab my private PGP key, and use a keylogger to\nget the password for the key. Another thing to note is that if the email is\nwritten to disk unencrypted (eg in a spool directory) before being encrypted,\nthen it's likely someone with sufficient skills would be able to undelete it.\nThe best protection for this is to either use a ram disk for the spool, or disk encryption.\nOf course, another important thing to note is that PGP only encrypts message\nbodies. It wont protect you from an adversary finding out who sent the message,\nwhat the subject line was, and what date/time it was sent. This method also\ndoesn't encrypt your Sent Items folder. My personal solution to this is: Delete\nemail, regularly and without mercy. Update\nI've resolved the problem of the contents of the Sent Items folder not being\nencrypted. See part 2 to\nsee how.\nUpdate 2 (2017)\nDon't use APG nowadays on Android. Use\nOpenKeyChain instead. It's a superb piece of\nsoftware. One of the most polished pieces of Android software I've used and\nintegrates perfectly with K-9 Mail. Like this article\n\n Sends a private notification to Mike. No external services used.Cancel\nSendWritten by Mike LoveFull stack dev + web + infra guy, interested and experienced in network privacy, security and performance. Based in Derbyshire, UK. Want to hire me? Get in touchFollow along, read more or surprise me:\n RSS    Atom    Mastodon    BlueskyOr support my writing & open‑source projects:\n\n Like\n Sends a private notification to Mike. No external services used.Cancel\nSend    PayPal    Buy Me a Coffee\n Bitcoin\nBitcoin Address\n\nCopy\nClose"
}

{
  "url": "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/vdukhovni/postfix/master/postfix/README_FILES/FILTER_README",
  "finalUrl": "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/vdukhovni/postfix/master/postfix/README_FILES/FILTER_README",
  "contentType": "text/plain; charset=utf-8",
  "title": "",
  "retrievalMethod": "request",
  "truncated": false,
  "content": "P\bPo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx A\bAf\bft\bte\ber\br-\b-Q\bQu\bue\beu\bue\be C\bCo\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt F\bFi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br\n\n-------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n\nI\bIn\bnt\btr\bro\bod\bdu\buc\bct\bti\bio\bon\bn\n\nThis document requires Postfix version 2.1 or later.\n\nNormally, Postfix receives mail, stores it in the mail queue and then delivers\nit. With the external content filter described here, mail is filtered AFTER it\nis queued. This approach decouples mail receiving processes from mail filtering\nprocesses, and gives you maximal control over how many filtering processes you\nare willing to run in parallel.\n\nThe after-queue content filter is meant to be used as follows:\n\n    Network or  -> Postfix -> C\bCo\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt -> Postfix ->  Network or\n    local users     queue     f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br      queue     local mailbox\n\nThis document describes implementations that use a single Postfix instance for\neverything: receiving, filtering and delivering mail. Applications that use two\nseparate Postfix instances will be covered by a later version of this document.\n\nThe after-queue content filter is not to be confused with the approaches\ndescribed in the SMTPD_PROXY_README or MILTER_README documents, where incoming\nSMTP mail is filtered BEFORE it is stored into the Postfix queue.\n\nThis document describes two approaches to content filter all email, as well as\nseveral options to filter mail selectively:\n\n  * Principles of operation\n  * Simple content filter\n\n      o Simple content filter example\n      o Simple content filter performance\n      o Simple content filter limitations\n      o Turning off the simple content filter\n\n  * Advanced content filter\n\n      o Advanced content filter example\n      o Advanced content filter performance\n      o Turning off the advanced content filter\n\n  * Selective content filtering\n\n      o Filtering mail from outside users only\n      o Different filters for different domains\n      o FILTER actions in access or header/body tables\n\nP\bPr\bri\bin\bnc\bci\bip\bpl\ble\bes\bs o\bof\bf o\bop\bpe\ber\bra\bat\bti\bio\bon\bn\n\nAn after-queue content filter receives unfiltered mail from Postfix (as\ndescribed further below) and can do one of the following:\n\n 1. Re-inject the mail back into Postfix, perhaps after changing content and/or\n    destination.\n\n 2. Discard or quarantine the mail.\n\n 3. Reject the mail (by sending a suitable status code back to Postfix).\n    Postfix will send the mail back to the sender address.\n\nNOTE: in this time of mail worms and forged spam, it is a VERY BAD IDEA to send\nviruses back to the sender address, because the sender address is almost\ncertainly not the originator. It is better to discard known viruses, and to\nquarantine material that is suspect so that a human can decide what to do with\nit.\n\nS\bSi\bim\bmp\bpl\ble\be c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br e\bex\bxa\bam\bmp\bpl\ble\be\n\nThe first example is simple to set up, but has major limitations that will be\naddressed in a second example. Postfix receives unfiltered mail from the\nnetwork with the smtpd(8) server, and delivers unfiltered mail to a content\nfilter with the Postfix pipe(8) delivery agent. The content filter injects\nfiltered mail back into Postfix with the Postfix sendmail(1) command, so that\nPostfix can deliver it to the final destination.\n\nThis means that mail submitted via the Postfix sendmail(1) command cannot be\ncontent filtered.\n\nIn the figure below, names followed by a number represent Postfix commands or\ndaemon programs. See the OVERVIEW document for an introduction to the Postfix\narchitecture.\n\n    Unfiltered -> smtpd(8)                   qmgr(8)     local(8) -> Filtered\n                            >- cleanup(8) -> Postfix  -< smtp(8)  -> Filtered\n                  pickup(8)                   queue      pipe(8)\n\n                        ^                                     |\n                        |                                     v\n\n                  maildrop      Postfix      Postfix     Content\n                    queue   <-  postdrop  <- sendmail <-  filter\n                                  (1)          (1)\n\nThe content filter can be a simple shell script like this:\n\n     1 #!/bin/sh\n     2\n     3 # Simple shell-based filter. It is meant to be invoked as follows:\n     4 #       /path/to/script -f sender recipients...\n     5\n     6 # Localize these. The -G option does nothing before Postfix 2.3.\n     7 INSPECT_DIR=/var/spool/filter\n     8 SENDMAIL=\"/usr/sbin/sendmail -G -i\" # NEVER NEVER NEVER use \"-t\" here.\n     9\n    10 # Exit codes from <sysexits.h>\n    11 EX_TEMPFAIL=75\n    12 EX_UNAVAILABLE=69\n    13\n    14 # Clean up when done or when aborting.\n    15 trap \"rm -f in.$$\" 0 1 2 3 15\n    16\n    17 # Start processing.\n    18 cd $INSPECT_DIR || {\n    19     echo $INSPECT_DIR does not exist; exit $EX_TEMPFAIL; }\n    20\n    21 cat >in.$$ || {\n    22     echo Cannot save mail to file; exit $EX_TEMPFAIL; }\n    23\n    24 # Specify your content filter here.\n    25 # filter <in.$$ || {\n    26 #   echo Message content rejected; exit $EX_UNAVAILABLE; }\n    27\n    28 $SENDMAIL \"$@\" <in.$$\n    29\n    30 exit $?\n\nNotes:\n\n  * Line 8: The -G option says the filter output is not a local mail\n    submission: don't do silly things like appending the local domain name to\n    addresses in message headers. This option does nothing before Postfix\n    version 2.3.\n\n  * Line 8: The -i option says don't stop reading input when a line contains\n    \".\" only.\n\n  * Line 8: NEVER NEVER NEVER use the \"-t\" command-line option here. It will\n    mis-deliver mail, like sending messages from a mailing list back to the\n    mailing list.\n\n  * Line 21: The idea is to first capture the message to file and then run the\n    content through a third-party content filter program.\n\n  * Line 22: If the message cannot be captured to file, mail delivery is\n    deferred by terminating with exit status 75 (EX_TEMPFAIL). Postfix places\n    the message in the deferred mail queue and tries again later.\n\n  * Line 25: You will need to specify a real content filter program here that\n    receives the content on standard input.\n\n  * Line 26: If the content filter program finds a problem, the mail is bounced\n    by terminating with exit status 69 (EX_UNAVAILABLE). Postfix will send the\n    message back to the sender as undeliverable mail.\n\n  * NOTE: in this time of mail worms and spam, it is a BAD IDEA to send known\n    viruses or spam back to the sender, because that address is likely to be\n    forged. It is safer to discard known viruses and to quarantine suspicious\n    content so that it can be inspected by a human being.\n\n  * Line 28: If the content is OK, it is given as input to the Postfix sendmail\n    command, and the exit status of the filter command is whatever exit status\n    the Postfix sendmail command produces. Postfix will deliver the message as\n    usual.\n\n  * Line 30: Postfix returns the exit status of the Postfix sendmail command.\n\nI suggest that you first run this script by hand until you are satisfied with\nthe results. Run it with a real message (headers+body) as input:\n\n    % /path/to/script -f sender -- recipient... <message-file\n\nOnce you're satisfied with the content filtering script:\n\n  * Create a dedicated local user account called \"filter\". This user handles\n    all potentially dangerous mail content - that is why it should be a\n    separate account. Do not use \"nobody\", and most certainly do not use \"root\"\n    or \"postfix\".\n\n  * Create a directory /var/spool/filter that is accessible only to the\n    \"filter\" user. This is where the content filtering script is supposed to\n    store its temporary files.\n\n  * Configure Postfix to deliver mail to the content filter with the pipe(8)\n    delivery agent (see the pipe(8) manpage for a description of the command\n    syntax below).\n\n    /etc/postfix/master.cf:\n      # =============================================================\n      # service type  private unpriv  chroot  wakeup  maxproc command\n      #               (yes)   (yes)   (yes)   (never) (100)\n      # =============================================================\n      filter    unix  -       n       n       -       10      pipe\n        flags=Rq user=filter null_sender=\n        argv=/path/to/script -f ${sender} -- ${recipient}\n\n    This runs up to 10 content filters in parallel. Instead of a limit of 10\n    concurrent processes, use whatever process limit is feasible for your\n    machine. Content inspection software can gobble up a lot of system\n    resources, so you don't want to have too much of it running at the same\n    time. The empty null_sender setting is required with Postfix 2.3 and later.\n\n  * To turn on content filtering for mail arriving via SMTP only, append \"-\n    o content_filter=filter:dummy\" to the master.cf entry that defines the\n    Postfix SMTP server:\n\n    /etc/postfix/master.cf:\n      # =============================================================\n      # service type  private unpriv  chroot  wakeup  maxproc command\n      #               (yes)   (yes)   (yes)   (never) (100)\n      # =============================================================\n      smtp      inet  ...other stuff here, do not change...   smtpd\n            -o content_filter=filter:dummy\n\n    The \"-o content_filter\" line causes Postfix to add one content filter\n    request record to each incoming mail message, with content \"filter:dummy\".\n    This record overrides the normal mail routing and causes mail to be given\n    to the content filter instead.\n\n    The content_filter configuration parameter expects a value of the form\n    transport:destination. The transport name specifies the first field of a\n    mail delivery agent definition in master.cf; the syntax of the next-hop\n    destination is described in the manual page of the corresponding delivery\n    agent.\n\n    The meaning of an empty next-hop filter destination is version dependent.\n    Postfix 2.7 and later will use the recipient domain; earlier versions will\n    use $myhostname. Specify \"default_filter_nexthop = $myhostname\" for\n    compatibility with Postfix 2.6 or earlier, or specify a non-empty next-hop\n    filter destination.\n\n    The content_filter setting has lower precedence than a FILTER action that\n    is specified in an access(5), header_checks(5) or body_checks(5) table.\n\n  * Execute \"p\bpo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx r\bre\bel\blo\boa\bad\bd\" to complete the change.\n\nS\bSi\bim\bmp\bpl\ble\be c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br p\bpe\ber\brf\bfo\bor\brm\bma\ban\bnc\bce\be\n\nWith the shell script as shown above you will lose a factor of four in Postfix\nperformance for transit mail that arrives and leaves via SMTP. You will lose\nanother factor in transit performance for each additional temporary file that\nis created and deleted in the process of content filtering. The performance\nimpact is less for mail that is submitted or delivered locally, because such\ndeliveries are already slower than SMTP transit mail.\n\nS\bSi\bim\bmp\bpl\ble\be c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br l\bli\bim\bmi\bit\bta\bat\bti\bio\bon\bns\bs\n\nThe problem with content filters like the one above is that they are not very\nrobust. The reason is that the software does not talk a well-defined protocol\nwith Postfix. If the filter shell script aborts because the shell runs into\nsome memory allocation problem, the script will not produce a nice exit status\nas defined in the file /usr/include/sysexits.h. Instead of going to the\ndeferred queue, mail will bounce. The same lack of robustness can happen when\nthe content filtering software itself runs into a resource problem.\n\nThe simple content filter method is not suitable for content filter actions\nthat are invoked via header_checks or body_checks patterns. These patterns will\nbe applied again after mail is re-injected with the Postfix sendmail command,\nresulting in a mail filtering loop. The advanced content filtering method (see\nbelow) makes it possible to turn off header_checks or body_checks patterns for\nfiltered mail.\n\nT\bTu\bur\brn\bni\bin\bng\bg o\bof\bff\bf t\bth\bhe\be s\bsi\bim\bmp\bpl\ble\be c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br\n\nTo turn off \"simple\" content filtering:\n\n  * Edit the master.cf file, remove the \"-o content_filter=filter:dummy\" text\n    from the entry that defines the Postfix SMTP server.\n\n  * Execute \"p\bpo\bos\bst\bts\bsu\bup\bpe\ber\br -\b-r\br A\bAL\bLL\bL\" to remove content filter request records from\n    existing queue files.\n\n  * Execute another \"p\bpo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx r\bre\bel\blo\boa\bad\bd\".\n\nA\bAd\bdv\bva\ban\bnc\bce\bed\bd c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br e\bex\bxa\bam\bmp\bpl\ble\be\n\nThe second example is more complex, but can give better performance, and is\nless likely to bounce mail when the machine runs into some resource problem.\nThis content filter receives unfiltered mail with SMTP on localhost port 10025,\nand sends filtered mail back into Postfix with SMTP on localhost port 10026.\n\nFor non-SMTP capable content filtering software, Bennett Todd's SMTP proxy\nimplements a nice PERL/SMTP content filtering framework. See: https://\nweb.archive.org/web/20151022025756/http://bent.latency.net/smtpprox/.\n\nIn the figure below, names followed by a number represent Postfix commands or\ndaemon programs. See the OVERVIEW document for an introduction to the Postfix\narchitecture.\n\n    Unfiltered -> smtpd(8)                   qmgr(8)     smtp(8)  -> Filtered\n                            >- cleanup(8) -> Postfix  -<\n    Unfiltered -> pickup(8)                   queue      local(8) -> Filtered\n\n                                     ^            |\n                                     |            v\n\n                                smtpd(8)     smtp(8)\n                                 10026\n\n                                     ^            |\n                                     |            v\n\n                                content filter 10025\n\nThe example given here filters all mail, including mail that arrives via SMTP\nand mail that is locally submitted via the Postfix sendmail command (local\nsubmissions enter Postfix via the pickup(8) server; to keep the figure simple\nwe omit local submission details). See examples near the end of this document\nfor how to exclude local users from filtering, or how to configure a\ndestination dependent content filter.\n\nYou can expect to lose about a factor of two in Postfix performance for mail\nthat arrives and leaves via SMTP, provided that the content filter creates no\ntemporary files. Each temporary file created by the content filter adds another\nfactor to the performance loss.\n\nA\bAd\bdv\bva\ban\bnc\bce\bed\bd c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br:\b: r\bre\beq\bqu\bue\bes\bst\bti\bin\bng\bg t\bth\bha\bat\bt a\bal\bll\bl m\bma\bai\bil\bl i\bis\bs f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\bre\bed\bd\n\nTo enable the advanced content filter method for all mail, specify in main.cf:\n\n    /etc/postfix/main.cf:\n        content_filter = scan:localhost:10025\n        receive_override_options = no_address_mappings\n\n  * The \"receive_override_options\" line disables address manipulation before\n    the content filter, so that the content filter sees the original mail\n    addresses instead of the result of virtual alias expansion, canonical\n    mapping, automatic bcc, address masquerading, etc.\n\n  * The \"content_filter\" line causes Postfix to add one content filter request\n    record to each incoming mail message, with content \"scan:localhost:10025\".\n    The content filter request records are added by the smtpd(8) and pickup(8)\n    servers (and qmqpd(8) if you decide to enable this service).\n\n  * Content filter requests are stored in queue files; this is how Postfix\n    keeps track of what mail needs filtering. When a queue file contains a\n    content filter request, the queue manager will deliver the mail to the\n    specified content filter regardless of its final destination.\n\n  * The content_filter configuration parameter expects a value of the form\n    transport:destination. The transport name specifies the first field of a\n    mail delivery agent definition in master.cf; the syntax of the next-hop\n    destination is described in the manual page of the corresponding delivery\n    agent.\n\n  * The meaning of an empty next-hop filter destination is version dependent.\n    Postfix 2.7 and later will use the recipient domain; earlier versions will\n    use $myhostname. Specify \"default_filter_nexthop = $myhostname\" for\n    compatibility with Postfix 2.6 or earlier, or specify a non-empty next-hop\n    filter destination.\n\n  * The content_filter setting has lower precedence than a FILTER action that\n    is specified in an access(5), header_checks(5) or body_checks(5) table.\n\nA\bAd\bdv\bva\ban\bnc\bce\bed\bd c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br:\b: s\bse\ben\bnd\bdi\bin\bng\bg u\bun\bnf\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\bre\bed\bd m\bma\bai\bil\bl t\bto\bo t\bth\bhe\be c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br\n\nIn this example, \"scan\" is an instance of the Postfix SMTP client with slightly\ndifferent configuration parameters. This is how one would set up the service in\nthe Postfix master.cf file:\n\n    /etc/postfix/master.cf:\n        # =============================================================\n        # service type  private unpriv  chroot  wakeup  maxproc command\n        #               (yes)   (yes)   (yes)   (never) (100)\n        # =============================================================\n        scan      unix  -       -       n       -       10      smtp\n            -o smtp_send_xforward_command=yes\n            -o disable_mime_output_conversion=yes\n            -o smtp_generic_maps=\n\n  * This runs up to 10 content filters in parallel. Instead of a limit of 10\n    concurrent processes, use whatever process limit is feasible for your\n    machine. Content inspection software can gobble up a lot of system\n    resources, so you don't want to have too much of it running at the same\n    time.\n\n  * With \"-o smtp_send_xforward_command=yes\", the scan transport will try to\n    forward the original client name and IP address through the content filter\n    to the after-filter smtpd process, so that filtered mail is logged with the\n    real client name IP address. See smtp(8) and XFORWARD_README for more\n    information.\n\n  * The \"-o disable_mime_output_conversion=yes\" is a workaround that prevents\n    the breaking of domainkeys and other digital signatures. This is needed\n    because some SMTP-based content filters don't announce 8BITMIME support,\n    even though they can handle 8-bit mail.\n\n  * The \"-o smtp_generic_maps=\" is a workaround that prevents local address\n    rewriting with generic(5) maps. Such rewriting should happen only when mail\n    is sent out to the Internet.\n\nA\bAd\bdv\bva\ban\bnc\bce\bed\bd c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br:\b: r\bru\bun\bnn\bni\bin\bng\bg t\bth\bhe\be c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br\n\nThe content filter can be set up with the Postfix spawn service, which is the\nPostfix equivalent of inetd. For example, to instantiate up to 10 content\nfiltering processes on localhost port 10025:\n\n    /etc/postfix/master.cf:\n        # ===================================================================\n        # service       type  private unpriv  chroot  wakeup  maxproc command\n        #                     (yes)   (yes)   (yes)   (never) (100)\n        # ===================================================================\n        localhost:10025 inet  n       n       n       -       10      spawn\n            user=filter argv=/path/to/filter localhost 10026\n\n  * \"filter\" is a dedicated local user account. The user will never log in, and\n    can be given a \"*\" password and non-existent shell and home directory. This\n    user handles all potentially dangerous mail content - that is why it should\n    be a separate account.\n\n  * By default, Postfix will terminate a command that runs longer than\n    command_time_limit seconds (default: 1000s). This is a safety measure that\n    prevents filters from running forever.\n\nIf you want to have your filter listening on port localhost:10025 instead of\nPostfix, then you must run your filter as a stand-alone program, and must not\nuse the Postfix spawn service.\n\nA\bAd\bdv\bva\ban\bnc\bce\bed\bd f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br:\b: i\bin\bnj\bje\bec\bct\bti\bin\bng\bg m\bma\bai\bil\bl b\bba\bac\bck\bk i\bin\bnt\bto\bo P\bPo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx\n\nThe job of the content filter is to either bounce mail with a suitable\ndiagnostic, or to feed the mail back into Postfix through a dedicated listener\non port localhost 10026.\n\nThe simplest content filter just copies SMTP commands and data between its\ninputs and outputs. If it has a problem, all it has to do is to reply to an\ninput of `.' from Postfix with `550 content rejected', and to disconnect\nwithout sending `.' on the connection that injects mail back into Postfix.\n\n    /etc/postfix/master.cf:\n        # ===================================================================\n        # service       type  private unpriv  chroot  wakeup  maxproc command\n        #                     (yes)   (yes)   (yes)   (never) (100)\n        # ===================================================================\n        localhost:10026 inet  n       -       n       -       10      smtpd\n            -o content_filter=\n            -\n    o\n    receive_override_options=no_unknown_recipient_checks,no_header_body_checks,no_milters\n            -o smtpd_helo_restrictions=\n            -o smtpd_client_restrictions=\n            -o smtpd_sender_restrictions=\n            # Postfix 2.10 and later: specify empty smtpd_relay_restrictions.\n            -o smtpd_relay_restrictions=\n            -o smtpd_recipient_restrictions=permit_mynetworks,reject\n            -o mynetworks=127.0.0.0/8\n            -o smtpd_authorized_xforward_hosts=127.0.0.0/8\n\n  * NOTE: do not use spaces around the \"=\" or \",\" characters.\n\n  * NOTE: the SMTP server must not have a smaller process limit than the\n    \"filter\" master.cf entry.\n\n  * The \"-o content_filter=\" overrides main.cf settings, and requests no\n    content filtering for mail from the content filter. This is required or\n    else mail will loop.\n\n  * The \"-o receive_override_options\" overrides main.cf settings to avoid\n    duplicating work that was already done before the content filter. These\n    options are complementary to the options that are specified in main.cf:\n\n      o We specify \"no_unknown_recipient_checks\" to disable attempts to find\n        out if a recipient is unknown.\n\n      o We specify \"no_header_body_checks\" to disable header/body checks.\n\n      o We specify \"no_milters\" to disable Milter applications (this option is\n        available only in Postfix 2.3 and later).\n\n      o We don't specify \"no_address_mappings\" here. They turn off junk mail controls that would only waste\n    time here.\n\n  * With \"-o smtpd_authorized_xforward_hosts=127.0.0.0/8\", the scan transport\n    will try to forward the original client name and IP address to the after-\n    filter smtpd process, so that filtered mail is logged with the real client\n    name and IP address. See XFORWARD_README and smtpd(8).\n\nA\bAd\bdv\bva\ban\bnc\bce\bed\bd c\bco\bon\bnt\bte\ben\bnt\bt f\bfi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br p\bpe\ber\brf\bfo\bor\brm\bma\ban\bnc\bce\be\n\nWith the \"sandwich\" approach to content filtering described here, it is\nimportant to match the filter concurrency to the available CPU, memory and I/\nO resources.       n       -       -       smtpd\n            -o smtpd_client_restrictions=permit_mynetworks,reject\n        127.0.0.1:smtp inet  n       -       n       -       -       smtpd\n            -o smtpd_client_restrictions=permit_mynetworks,reject\n\n  * One SMTP server address for mail from outside users with content filtering\n    turned on.\n\n    /etc/postfix.master.cf:\n        # =================================================================\n        # service     type  private unpriv  chroot  wakeup  maxproc command\n        #                   (yes)   (yes)   (yes)   (never) (100)\n        # =================================================================\n        1.2.3.5:smtp  inet  n       - As of Postfix 2.0 you can\nalso turn on content filtering on the fly.\n\nTo turn on content filtering with an access(5) table rule:\n\n    /etc/postfix/access:\n        whatever       FILTER foo:bar\n\nTo turn on content filtering with a header_checks(5) or body_checks(5) table\npattern:\n\n    /etc/postfix/header_checks:\n        /whatever/     FILTER foo:bar\n\nYou can do this in smtpd access maps as well as the cleanup server's header/\nbody_checks. This feature must be used with great care: you must disable all\nthe UCE features in the after-filter smtpd and cleanup daemons or else you will\nhave a content filtering loop.\n\nLimitations:\n\n  * FILTER actions from smtpd access maps and header/body_checks take\n    precedence over filters specified with the main.cf content_filter\n    parameter.\n\n  * If a message triggers more than one filter action, only the last one takes\n    effect.\n\n  * The same content filter is applied to all the recipients of a given\n    message."

#+end_tool
Yes — but Postfix has no "hook" in the WordPress sense. The usual, plain-bash way is an *after-queue content filter*: Postfix pipes each message into a script, and the script re-injects the (now encrypted) message with =sendmail=. This is the setup documented in Postfix's own =FILTER_README=, and it needs no external daemons.

** Minimal recipe (simple content filter)

*1. Dedicated user + GNUPGHOME + public key*

#+begin_src sh
useradd --system --shell /usr/sbin/nologin --home /var/spool/filter filter
install -d -m 700 -o filter -g filter /var/spool/filter/gnupg
sudo -u filter gpg --homedir /var/spool/filter/gnupg --import you@example.com.asc
#+end_src

*2. =/usr/local/bin/gpg-filter.sh=*

#+begin_src bash
#!/bin/bash
# invoked as: gpg-filter.sh -f sender -- recipients...
SENDMAIL="/usr/sbin/sendmail -G -i"    # NEVER use -t here
GPG="/usr/bin/gpg --homedir /var/spool/filter/gnupg --batch --yes --no-tty --trust-model always"
KEY="0xDEADBEEFDEADBEEF"                # your single public key
EX_TEMPFAIL=75

tmp=$(mktemp /var/spool/filter/msg.XXXXXX) || exit $EX_TEMPFAIL
trap 'rm -f "$tmp" "$tmp.gpg"' 0 1 2 3 15
cat > "$tmp" || exit $EX_TEMPFAIL

# don't double-encrypt
if grep -qiE '^Content-Type: multipart/encrypted|-----BEGIN PGP MESSAGE-----' "$tmp"; then
    $SENDMAIL "$@" < "$tmp"; exit $?
fi

if ! $GPG --encrypt --recipient "$KEY" --output "$tmp.gpg" "$tmp"; then
    exit $EX_TEMPFAIL      # 75 = defer/retry, safer than bouncing
fi

$SENDMAIL "$@" < "$tmp.gpg"
exit $?
#+end_src

Note: =--trust-model always= is needed unless you sign the key locally with =gpg --edit-key … trust=, otherwise gpg will refuse on an untrusted key.

*3. =/etc/postfix/master.cf=* — add the pipe transport, and turn filtering on only for the inbound smtpd you care about:

#+begin_src 
smtp  inet  n  -  y  -  -  smtpd
  -o content_filter=gpgfilter:dummy

gpgfilter  unix  -  n  n  -  10  pipe
  flags=Rq user=filter null_sender=
  argv=/usr/local/bin/gpg-filter.sh -f ${sender} -- ${recipient}
#+end_src

*4. =postfix reload=*, then re-queue old mail with =postsuper -r ALL=.

Test it by hand first: =sudo -u filter /usr/local/bin/gpg-filter.sh -f a@b -- you@example.com < msg.eml=.

** Things that bite people

- *Re-injection loop.* With =content_filter= on a /global/ basis you also need a second smtpd on =127.0.0.1:10026= with =-o content_filter== and =-o receive_override_options=no_unknown_recipient_checks,no_header_body_checks,no_milters= plus =-o smtpd_relay_restrictions== / =permit_mynetworks,reject=, and the filter instructs sendmail to submit there. The =-o content_filter== on the =smtp= entry above (per-service) avoids that whole dance, which is why it's the cleaner choice for "encrypt inbound only".
- *=null_sender==* is required for Postfix ≥ 2.3, otherwise bounces (empty sender) get piped and loop.
- *=flags=Rq=*: =R= adds a =Return-Path:= header, =q= quotes =$sender=/=$recipient=.
- *One gpg process per message* — real cost, but fine for low volume; set the =maxproc= (10 above) sensibly.
- Only headers of the payload get encrypted; subject/from/to stay cleartext unless you do PGP/MIME (RFC 3156) or memory-hole headers. Plain =gpg --encrypt= on the whole message is "encrypt at rest", not wire encryption.
- It only encrypts what arrives via that smtpd; mail from your own submission port / =sendmail= isn't touched.
- =mailbox_command= looks like the "hook" you want but only applies to =local(8)= delivery (not virtual mailboxes) and runs after queueing per-recipient — workable, but the content filter above is the standard pattern.

** If you'd rather not hand-roll it

- *gpg-mailgate* (Python, content filter, per-recipient key lookup from a keyring or keyserver) — the classic; several maintained forks exist.
- *postfix-gpg-milter* (Go, before-queue milter, key lookup via gpg keyring/keyserver/WKD) — cleaner than pipe+re-inject, no temp files, rejects instead of deferring. Use =smtpd_milters = inet:127.0.0.1:8893=.

For a single fixed public key with no per-recipient logic, the 30-line bash filter is genuinely the right amount of tooling.

*** hey man don't teach me bash tell me the abstract concepts behind postfix filters how they work and would a default gpg encrypt command be enough (make it be enough)


#+begin_reasoning
The user is annoyed at the bash dump. They want conceptual explanation: how Postfix filters work (architecture, hook points), and whether a plain `gpg --encrypt -r KEY` command is enough — and "make it be enough."

So I should explain abstractly, no scripts. Concepts:

1. Postfix architecture: smtpd/pickup → cleanup → qmgr → delivery agents (smtp, local, pipe...). Two classes of "hooks": before-queue (smtpd_* milters, smtpd_proxy_filter, smtpd_command_filter) and after-queue (content_filter → any transport, notably pipe).

2. Key concept: the filter is not a "hook" — Postfix's filtering is *routing*. A filter is just another delivery destination. content_filter is an instruction stored in the queue file (a "content filter request record") that says "when you get to delivering this, don't deliver to the recipient, hand it to this transport instead." Then re-injection is necessary because Postfix doesn't call back into your filter with the result — the filter must push the mail back in as if a new message.

3. Consequently: filter = a full MTA-shaped hole, not a function call. Contract: receives envelope (sender/recipients) as argv, message on stdin in re-injectable form (headers+body, no SMTP envelope), and must terminate with an exit status that means one of {accept, defer/tempfail, bounce}. Everything else is your problem: re-injection, loop prevention, envelope preservation, dedupe of already-encrypted mail, temp file atomicity, sender/recipient preservation.

4. milter is the alternative shape: before-queue, in-protocol, streaming callbacks, can modify headers/body in place and reject. More like a real hook, but requires a daemon speaking a protocol.

5. Now: is `gpg --encrypt -r KEY < mail` enough? Abstractly, gpg -e is a *pure function on a byte stream*, whereas a mail filter is a *stateful, side-effecting, envelope-aware pipeline stage*. Five gaps:
   - envelope: gpg only sees the message; the envelope (sender/recipients) is out-of-band and must be replayed; if you re-inject with -t you break it (list mail loops). Actually envelope matters for delivery, not for encryption.
   - parse/validity: gpg -e on the whole RFC822 message produces inline PGP, which mangles everything semantically: Content-Type still says text/plain or whatever, multipart boundaries, subject line is inside, MIME structure is destroyed. Clients will show a blob or break. So you need PGP/MIME or at least accept that it's "encryption at rest" and the client must be able to handle it.
   - idempotency: gpg -e is not idempotent, and re-injection creates a loop unless the filter is bypassed on the second pass — the fix is structural (separate smtpd, or content_filter= empty), not in the gpg command.
   - failure semantics: gpg exits 2 for various things; unmapped exit = bounce instead of defer. Need explicit mapping. Also gpg can happily encrypt to a wrong/expired key.
   - key selection: "single recipient public key" is actually the thing that makes gpg -e enough — no key lookup daemon needed.

6. "Make it be enough": distinguish two claims. `gpg -e -r KEY` is enough as the *transform*. It is not enough as the *filter*, because the filter is 90% envelope/loop/lifecycle bookkeeping and 10% transform. But you can make the transform carry the whole job by choosing the right shape: the pipe transport gives you envelope in argv, and then gpg -e as pure stdin→stdout is exactly the right shape — the only thing gpg can't do is preserve MIME. So:
   - if accepting inline PGP (encryption-at-rest, client decrypts whole message): gpg -e alone suffices as a byte transform, wrap it in pipe transport.
   - if you want clients to still render properly: need PGP/MIME, so either gpg's --encrypt with MIME prep, or a milter-based tool that does MIME surgery. gpg alone can't; `gpg -e` on a text/plain message body while keeping headers requires splitting head from body — trivial but it is no longer "just gpg".

Honest verdict: for a single fixed key and "I just want the bytes encrypted before they hit the disk," yes, one gpg invocation is enough — the rest is plumbing that Postfix's pipe transport already gives you. For "normal mail that looks normal in a client", no, because MIME.

Make it enough: the minimal conceptual checklist rather than code. Let me frame as: what you must add around gpg to make one command sufficient.

Also mention: an important subtlety — Postfix pipe delivers per recipient, and re-injection with envelope replay. Also mention `mailbox_command` vs `content_filter` vs `milter` shapes table.

Also: the delivery-time vs acceptance-time distinction — after-queue filtering means mail is on disk in cleartext briefly, which for "encryption at rest" is a real gap (spool + disk). milter/before-queue avoids it partly. That's an abstract point worth making since user's goal is at-rest encryption.

Format: prose with a few short sections, maybe a small table of hook shapes. No bash. Concise-ish but substantive. Emacs-org style? The previous answer used org. User is annoyed by bash-dump, so keep code out entirely. Use org headings maybe. Keep it maybe 500-700 words. Avoid over-length.

Let me write it.

Structure:
- Postfix has no hooks, it has delivery decisions
- Two families: before-queue vs after-queue (table)
- The core abstraction: a filter is a queue entry, not a callback
- Therefore the contract is MTA-shaped
- Where does gpg fit: pure function vs pipeline stage → gap list (envelope, MIME, idempotency/loop, exit codes, key selection)
- Verdict + what makes it enough

Careful about one factual point: does the pipe transport hand you the envelope in argv? Yes, ${sender} ${recipient} and flags=q. And message on stdin without envelope, with Return-Path optionally via flags=R. Envelope recipients may be split per-recipient deliveries.

Also: gpg -e exit codes: 2 is generic/bad. Unmapped nonzero exit → the pipe transport... actually what does pipe do with nonzero? A nonzero exit that's not 75/69 etc. results in bounce typically (status returned to qmgr → bounce or defer per sysexits). Yes — that's the brittleness.

One more abstract point: the filter boundary is "mail as bytes" — no API can tell you what part is the body, so any structural edit is textual and thus lossy. That's why MIME-aware filters need parsers, and why "just gpg it" is inevitably inline.

Also `-t` note abstracted: re-injection must not re-derive recipients from headers because headers can disagree with the envelope (Bcc, aliases, list expansions).

Write it.
#+end_reasoning

Right. Here's the concept layer.

*Postfix doesn't have hooks — it has delivery decisions*

Every program in the pipeline (smtpd, pickup, cleanup, qmgr, the delivery agents) is a stage that takes one message and hands it onward. There is no callback registry, no "on incoming mail" event. What people call a filter is actually /a routing instruction stored in the queue file/: "this message must not go to its recipient yet; give it to transport X instead." The queue manager, which knows nothing about mail semantics, just sees one more next-hop and does what it's told. So filtering is not an event you subscribe to, it's a destination you insert into the route.

*Two families, distinguished only by /when/ the fork happens*

- /Before queue/ (milters, =smtpd_proxy_filter=, smtpd command filters): the smtpd process talks to your program /during/ the SMTP conversation, message still in memory. You can reject, modify, or add headers in flight. Nothing hits the spool unencrypted. Cost: your program must speak a protocol (milter framing, or be an SMTP proxy) and behave like a daemon.
- /After queue/ (=content_filter=): the message is already accepted, written to disk, and only then routed to your program. Your program is just another delivery agent. Cost: cleartext exists on disk first, and there's no protocol to speak — which is precisely why this is the bash-friendly one.

*The consequence: the filter contract is MTA-shaped*

Because the filter /is/ a delivery agent, its interface is the interface of a mail transfer agent, not of a function: it gets the envelope handed to it out-of-band (sender, recipients), it gets the message as a byte stream on stdin with the envelope stripped off, and its only output channel is an exit status meaning accept / defer / reject. It has no return value. So the moment you write a filter, you've signed up to be a miniature MTA: replay the envelope, re-inject the result as a /new/ message, and guarantee the second pass doesn't re-enter you. That loop-breaking is not a detail — it's the whole reason the advanced method exists, and it's structural (a second smtpd with filtering disabled), not something any filter program can fix from the inside.

*Now: is =gpg --encrypt -r KEY= enough?*

Abstractly you're asking a /pure function on a byte stream/ to fill a /stateful, envelope-aware pipeline stage/. The transform is right; the shape doesn't line up in four places:

1. /Envelope./ gpg never sees sender/recipients; they're out-of-band. If you re-inject by letting Postfix derive recipients from headers, you break exactly the cases where headers and envelope disagree — Bcc, alias expansion, list mail. So the envelope must be carried, not recomputed.
2. /MIME./ gpg sees one flat blob with no idea what part is body. Encrypting the whole message inline leaves the outer =Content-Type= lying about its contents and leaves From/To/Subject/Date in cleartext. That's "encryption at rest," not mail a client renders. A client can only show you a blob and ask you to decrypt it. Doing it properly means PGP/MIME, which means splitting headers from body and rebuilding the structure — at which point it's no longer just gpg.
3. /Idempotency./ =gpg -e= is not a fixed point. Run it twice and you have nested ciphertext; run it on the re-injected copy and you have a loop. Nothing in gpg knows about a queue.
4. /Failure semantics./ gpg's exit statuses don't mean "defer" or "bounce." An unmapped nonzero exit is interpreted by Postfix as a permanent failure, so a temporarily unavailable keyring gets mail /bounced/ rather than retried. Also gpg happily encrypts to an expired or wrong key if you name it — there's no "is this the right recipient" check, because you removed the only thing that could do that check by hardcoding one key.

*Making it be enough*

The trick is to exploit the one place where the shapes do line up: the pipe transport hands you the envelope in its argument list and the message on stdin — exactly gpg's stdin/stdout shape. So:

/Yes/, one =gpg --encrypt --recipient KEY= invocation is sufficient /as the whole transform/ if — and only if — you accept inline PGP of the entire message, i.e. your goal is genuinely "no cleartext at rest," not "mail that still looks like mail." In that case the filter is 95% bookkeeping (carry the envelope, re-inject, refuse to touch already-encrypted messages, map gpg's failure to a deferral) and 5% gpg, and gpg is the correct 5%. For a single fixed key there is nothing else for a key-lookup layer to do — that's the part gpg-mailgate et al. exist for, and you don't need it.

/No/ if clients should render it: gpg alone can't do MIME surgery, so you'd either bolt a PGP/MIME wrapper around it (still one encrypt call, but no longer a plain one) or move to a milter that does MIME itself — and a milter also fixes the cleartext-on-spool gap, which matters if "at rest" is the actual requirement.

*** so what if my client is isync + notmuch and the decryption keys are on the client. Can I do a notmuch pre-new hook that does the decryption?


#+begin_reasoning
The user has: Postfix encrypting all incoming mail at rest with a single public key (server side, no private key on server). Client uses isync (mbsync) + notmuch. Private key is on the client. They ask: can I do a notmuch pre-new hook that does the decryption?

Key technical issues with pre-new hook:

1. `notmuch new` scans the maildir. A `pre-new` hook runs before indexing. But the fundamental problem: notmuch pre-new hook operates on files in the maildir. If you decrypt in place, you're replacing ciphertext with cleartext in the maildir — defeating the at-rest goal locally (but locally the threat model may be different... though notmuch index would then contain cleartext which is the whole point since you want to search it).

Wait — but actually the deeper issue: notmuch cannot search encrypted message bodies. notmuch indexes the raw message. So the standard tradeoff: you want local cleartext to index, but then the maildir has cleartext (or you keep both).

2. The real blocker for "pre-new hook that decrypts": 
- mbsync writes the PGP-inline encrypted message as a file. Its Content-Type is still whatever it was (e.g., text/plain), body is PGP MESSAGE armor. 
- To decrypt properly you must handle MIME: inline PGP vs PGP/MIME.
- The pre-new hook runs *before* notmuch indexes, once per `notmuch new`, and is a single bash command — it has no per-file interface. So you'd have to walk the whole maildir yourself, which means re-scanning everything every time (or tracking state).
- Worse: pre-new hook must not modify the maildir? Actually notmuch docs: "pre-new hook ... run before any new messages are indexed". If the hook modifies files, notmuch will index the modified versions. But notmuch detects new files by directory mtime and file names / message-id. If you modify an existing file's content, notmuch may not re-index it because it thinks it's already indexed (it tracks by message-id and file mtime? notmuch uses directory mtimes to decide which directories to scan, and then compares against the database by message-id + filename). So modifying file content in place → notmuch won't notice; you'd need `notmuch new --no-hooks` with... hmm, actually there's no "reindex" for changed content except `notmuch new` after touching, or `notmuch reindex` (which reindexes given query terms, works on already-indexed files).

So the cleaner approach: don't decrypt in the maildir. Instead:
- Option A: notmuch's own decryption support. notmuch supports `notmuch show --decrypt` and `notmuch search` cannot search encrypted bodies. Indexing encrypted content: notmuch 0.30+ has "notmuch indexopts"... no. Actually there's a feature: Emacs notmuch has `notmuch-crypto-process-mime` and `notmuch show --decrypt=true`. Searching encrypted messages: notmuch added `--decrypt` to `notmuch new`? Let me think. There was work on "indexing encrypted mail" — notmuch does NOT support indexing encrypted bodies by default because it would require the key at index time and store cleartext in Xapian. There's a patch/series "notmuch: decrypt during indexing" — I recall `notmuch new --decrypt` doesn't exist. Hmm. There is `notmuch reindex --decrypt=true` added in notmuch 0.32? Let me recall: notmuch 0.32 (2020) added `notmuch show --decrypt=...`? Actually notmuch 0.32 release notes: "notmuch reindex --decrypt=true" — I believe there IS support for indexing cleartext (indexing decrypted content) added around 0.32/0.33, storing it in the index, with the caveat it's stored in the clear in Xapian. Hmm, I should verify. Let me search.

Actually I recall: notmuch 0.32 added `notmuch new --decrypt=true` ... Hmm. I think the feature is "notmuch indexopts" no. Let me just search to be accurate rather than guess.

Also relevant: mbsync (isync) has `Sync` + `Create` and can pipe through a command? isync has no filter. But you can use `mbsync` with a `Pipe`? No. Alternatives: use `mbsync` to a staging maildir, decrypt there with a script invoked from a wrapper, then sync to the real maildir. Or use `mpop`/`fdm`. Or `maildrop`. Or use `offlineimap` with `filter` hooks (offlineimap removed hooks in v8). Or `mbsync` + `notmuch-insert`.

Another key point: the decrypted copy's Message-ID stays same; notmuch dedups by Message-ID. So you could keep two files — but notmuch would index whichever... it indexes both and treats as one message with two filenames; the searchable text would come from whichever file... messy.

Better architecture: decrypt at read time, not index time:
- notmuch + Emacs: `notmuch-show` decrypts on display via epg. Searching bodies of encrypted mail is impossible → that's the price.
- Or: decrypt-once into an alternate store, and index that store separately, with the maildir of cleartext protected by disk encryption / a separate user, and delete-or-keep policy.

Also: notmuch pre-new hook specifics: it runs with the environment; if it fails, notmuch exits with status 75. Only one hook. It's per-invocation, not per-file. And crucially, hooks are run for `notmuch new` only, not `notmuch insert`... actually pre-new hook is also run for `notmuch insert`? No — `notmuch insert` runs `pre-insert` hook. Hmm, notmuch has pre-new and post-new hooks; and `notmuch insert` runs `pre-insert`/`post-insert`. Let me verify.

Also important: if you decrypt in place in the maildir that mbsync syncs, mbsync will see the file changed locally and push it back to the server! That's a catastrophic loop — mbsync would upload the cleartext (or rather, it would treat local change as a modification and propagate). Actually mbsync by default syncs both directions; a modified local file → it uploads. Also the local file no longer matches server's UID/flags → could cause re-download or duplication. This is a strong argument against in-place decryption in a mbsync-managed maildir. You'd need `SyncState` considerations and mbsync would push the decrypted message up — destroying at-rest encryption on the server. Huge point.

Also gpg-agent / passphrase: pre-new hook runs non-interactively, so key must be available — either pinentry with cached passphrase, or gpg-agent with `allow-preset-passphrase`, or a key without passphrase (bad). If gpg fails to get passphrase, hook hangs (or times out) and notmuch new fails with 75.

Also: notmuch's index would store cleartext terms → Xapian DB is cleartext. If the threat model is "laptop stolen", the Xapian DB and the decrypted maildir are the exposure. So you're moving the trust boundary from "server" to "client disk", which is fine if client disk is encrypted — but then the server-side encryption was only protecting against server compromise, and now the client DB is the weak link. Worth making explicit.

Also the maildir flags / filename: mbsync uses filenames with UID; if you rewrite content in place keeping the filename, mbsync compares by filename+size? Not size — mbsync tracks UIDVALIDITY and message UIDs; local files are named `,U=123:2,S`. If you modify content, mbsync sees local file with same name and pushes. Yes.

Better alternatives:
1. mbsync to `~/Mail/encrypted/` (never opened by notmuch), decrypt into `~/Mail/local/` with a script (run before `notmuch new`), then `notmuch new --no-hooks` on that. But then mbsync-managed dir must be excluded from notmuch. And decryption script does its own state tracking (e.g., only decrypt files newer than last run, using a stamp file or `find -newer`). That's a legitimate "pre-new hook" pattern actually — but the hook itself is a shell script doing find+gpg, not "decrypt everything".
2. Use `notmuch reindex --decrypt=true` if available (searchable cleartext in index, maildir stays encrypted). Need to verify version support.
3. Read-time decryption only (Emacs notmuch show), accept no body search.

*** actually better can notmuch hooks encrypt the mail and then isync updates the server with the encrypted contents? Not headers just the text


#+begin_reasoning
The user is asking: can notmuch hooks encrypt the mail (the body text only, not headers) and then isync (mbsync) uploads the encrypted content back to the server?

Let me think carefully about this. This is a really important conceptual correction.

Key facts:
1. mbsync (isync) is a *sync* engine, not a mail delivery agent. It compares UIDVALIDITY/UID/message flags between local Maildir and IMAP server. It uploads new local messages and downloads new server messages.
2. mbsync does NOT upload modified message content for existing messages. IMAP fundamentally cannot rewrite a message in place. A message's body is immutable once APPENDed — UIDs are immutable. There's no "edit message" operation in IMAP (well, there's no such thing; you'd need APPEND new + delete old, which mbsync doesn't do).
3. So "modify the local file and have mbsync push the change up" is architecturally impossible. mbsync would either see it as... actually worse: mbsync detects local file changes?? Actually mbsync uses mtime/size to decide a local message needs uploading? No — for Maildir, mbsync tracks messages by filename/UID mapping in its own state database. If you modify the file content in place, mbsync generally does NOT notice content changes (it might notice size/mtime and... actually mbsync will detect a change and might re-upload as a *new* message, causing duplication). Let me be careful.

Actually mbsync does have logic: for local Maildir messages it stores the message's size and mtime? I believe mbsync's state file records UID<->filename mapping but not content hashes. If you modify the file, the sync will not propagate the change; worst case you get a "message changed" duplicate append. Let me verify rather than assert.

4. IMAP has no way to modify an existing message. Period. APPEND + EXPUNGE is the only route, and that changes the UID, breaks the message-id-based threading assumptions (well, Message-ID stays), and is exactly what mbsync doesn't do.

5. Also: notmuch hooks. There's a `pre-new` hook (runs before scanning), `post-new` hook (after), and `pre-insert` hook (before inserting). Notmuch hooks are *indexing-time* hooks, not delivery hooks, and they're not per-message: pre-new/post-new run once per `notmuch new` invocation, with the whole tree as a fait accompli — Maildir already written by mbsync. Hooks get no message list, no filename, no stdin. You can run a script that walks Maildir... but then you're back to hand-rolling a Maildir walker.

Wait — actually there IS a pre-insert hook which is per-message-ish (runs before notmuch insert, with the message on stdin... let me recall: "pre-insert hook is invoked before notmuch insert, with the message on stdin"? Actually notmuch-hooks says: pre-insert — "invoked by notmuch-insert before inserting a message into the database. The message is on standard input, and the hook can... " Hmm, I think it says the hook is invoked with the message on stdin? Let me check. I recall: "pre-insert: This hook is invoked by notmuch insert before inserting a message into the database. ... The message is not yet in the database... If the exit status is non-zero, notmuch insert will abort." I believe the stdin behavior — I'm not certain. Better to check the man page.

Anyway, key point: notmuch insert is not used by mbsync. mbsync writes Maildir files directly. So none of the notmuch hooks fire at delivery time at all. The hooks are a *different* pipeline (notmuch insert / notmuch new), not the mbsync delivery path.

6. Also the conceptual problem with "encrypt body only, keep headers": that's exactly PGP/MIME inline vs. multipart/encrypted. Encrypting only the text body requires rewriting MIME structure: for a simple text/plain message you can do inline PGP with Content-Type: text/plain and a PGP MESSAGE block body — that works and leaves headers intact. But for multipart/alternative (text+html), multipart/mixed with attachments — you can't just encrypt "the text" and leave attachments in cleartext, and if you do, you've broken the MIME tree. The general case requires multipart/encrypted with the original as the encrypted part, which means the payload is the *whole* MIME entity, and you need to add a part, change the top-level Content-Type, etc.

7. Crucially: signing/MIME manipulation on the client side is exactly what mu4e/notmuch "encrypt on send" does — but that's for *outgoing* mail composed in the client, where you control MIME generation. For incoming mail already on the server, the client can't mutate it.

8. Also: if the goal is "no cleartext at rest on the server," client-side encryption after download inherently fails, because by the time your client sees it, the server already had it in cleartext. The cleartext is in the server's IMAP store, in the server's spool, in DNS-adjacent hops etc. Encrypting client-side and pushing back only *adds* a ciphertext copy while leaving (or deleting) the plaintext — and the plaintext already existed. So the threat model is inverted: this pattern protects a server that *later* loses its plaintext, not one that never had it.

Hmm, but there IS a legitimate use case: you're synced with a provider you don't fully trust (Gmail), and you want the *stored* copy at the provider to end up encrypted, and you delete the plaintext there. Then the provider only has ciphertext going forward. That's "retroactive encryption of the remote store." That's actually a real thing people do (e.g., with Gmail + a local agent). It works only as APPEND-new + delete-old, done by something other than mbsync — e.g., a script using `mu`/`notmuch` + IMAP APPEND + EXPUNGE, or better, do it on the server side with a filter, or use an intermediate account.

So: answer is a conceptual "no, and here's the layer confusion."

Let me structure:

A. The layer confusion: notmuch hooks are index-time, mbsync delivery doesn't touch them at all.
B. IMAP messages are immutable; mbsync is a replicator, not an editor. mbsync reconciles *existence and flags*, not content. A modified local file is invisible or becomes a duplicate.
C. Even if you did APPEND+EXPUNGE: new UID, sync state churn, and you've now got two copies transiently; also mbsync will fight you.
D. Encrypting only the body while keeping headers: conceptually that's "inline PGP" and only expressible for single text/plain (or you must restructure MIME into multipart/encrypted, at which point you're writing a MIME engine).
E. Threat model inversion: cleartext already at server. Client-side re-encryption doesn't retroactively unsee it.
F. What actually works: three options:
   0. Server-side (the previous answer): encryption happens before the server ever stores cleartext — the only approach that satisfies "at rest".
   1. If you must do client-side + push back: it's an explicit APPEND+EXPUNGE job, not a hook. E.g. mbsync to a *separate* archive account, or a script that uses `gpg` + `mbsync`-free IMAP (python imaplib / `mu`'s tools / offlineimap? no). Mention `mbsync` can't; use something like `imapsync`? no that also can't modify. Actually the honest tool: a small script using IMAP APPEND with the encrypted blob and \Deleted on the original. Or use a provider-side filter (Gmail filters can't encrypt).
   2. Local-only encryption: don't push back at all. Encrypt the Maildir locally (e.g., notmuch + a local encrypting Maildir layer, or just full-disk/LUKS, or `maildir` encryption via `mbsync` post-hook + local archive). Keep the server copy as-is (accept it), and make the local copy the authoritative encrypted archive.
G. If the real goal is "the server never sees plaintext," the only correct place is before it reaches the server's storage — i.e., the MTA filter we discussed, or a before-queue milter on the receiving MTA. Client-side is structurally too late. That's the punchline.

Also worth noting: if you own the receiving server, the MTA filter is strictly better than anything client-side: it's one place, it's before storage, and the client just gets ciphertext it can decrypt with notmuch show --decrypt.

And: notmuch already handles encrypted mail natively — `notmuch show --decrypt=true`, `index.decrypt` in notmuch-config, `notmuch reindex --decrypt=true`. So if the server-side filter stores PGP/MIME (multipart/encrypted), notmuch indexes and renders it with zero extra plumbing. That's the "make it be enough" answer. Note: inline PGP (the "just gpg the whole message" approach from earlier) is *not* understood by notmuch/GMime as PGP/MIME — notmuch will show it as a text/plain body containing an armor block, and `--decrypt=true` won't decrypt it. That's a crucial connection back to the previous turn: the plain `gpg --encrypt` on the whole message produces something notmuch will NOT auto-decrypt. So if you're a notmuch user, you need PGP/MIME (multipart/encrypted), not inline whole-message encryption.

Hmm wait — is that right? For inline PGP: notmuch/GMime does support "inline PGP" detection in some cases? notmuch has `index.decrypt` which handles multipart/encrypted. For inline armored blocks in text/plain, notmuch does not decrypt automatically (Emacs notmuch-show has some support via `notmuch-show` ... there's `notmuch-crypto` which only handles multipart/encrypted signature/encryption parts). Actually, notmuch does have support for inline PGP in `notmuch show --format=text`? I believe notmuch only handles PGP/MIME properly; inline PGP is displayed raw. Whether GMime auto-detects inline... GMime has `g_mime_parser_options_set_...`? There's gmime "application/pgp-encrypted" handling only. I'll state it as: notmuch's native crypto handling is PGP/MIME-shaped; inline whole-message armor is not something it decrypts for you, you'd have to pipe through gpg manually. That's accurate enough and I should hedge slightly.

Also the "headers in cleartext" point: notmuch needs To/From/Subject/Date/Message-ID in cleartext to thread and search anyway. Memory-hole / protected headers were designed exactly for this. So "encrypt body, leave headers" is not a hack, it's RFC 9788 (formerly protected headers) — that is the standard shape. Good to mention: subject encryption via protected headers.

DO NOT USE --decrypt=true or\n--decrypt=nostash without considering the security of your\nindex.\nSee also index.decrypt in notmuch-config.\n\nEXAMPLES¶\nA user just received an encrypted message without indexing its\ncleartext. Short answer: no. Your idea collides with three independent walls, and any one of them is fatal.

/1. notmuch hooks aren't delivery hooks/

The complete set is =pre-new=, =post-new=, =post-insert=. All three fire on =notmuch new= / =notmuch insert=. mbsync does not call notmuch — it writes Maildir files directly, so /nothing fires when mail arrives/. The hooks are also zero-argument scripts with no message and no stdin: =pre-new= runs /before/ the scan (that's where you'd invoke mbsync, i.e. before the mail exists), =post-new= runs /after/ indexing over a corpus. There's no "here is the message, transform it" hook in notmuch at all. Even the flow you want has no referent.

/2. IMAP has no update operation/

A stored message is immutable, and its UID /is/ its identity. The only write verb IMAP has is APPEND — which creates a /different/ message with a /different/ UID — plus =\Deleted= on the old one. There is no "replace body."

mbsync is a replicator, not an editor: it reconciles /existence and flags/, keyed on its own UID↔filename state. It has no concept of "content changed." Rewrite a local file in place and it isn't an edit to propagate — at best it looks like an unrecognized new file (duplicate), at worst it trips a state mismatch and either re-downloads (silently undoing your encryption) or errors out. The upstream half of your plan doesn't exist for the tool to implement.

/3. "Body only, headers cleartext" isn't expressible there/

MIME trees are all-or-nothing. For a single =text/plain= you can get away with inline armor. The moment there's =multipart/alternative= or an attachment, encrypting "just the text" leaves the outer =Content-Type= lying about its own contents — a malformed message. The well-defined version is =multipart/encrypted= (RFC 3156) with the original entity as the payload, plus protected headers (RFC 9788) if you want the Subject hidden. That's a MIME engine, not a hook.

/The conceptual error underneath/

Your client only ever sees what the server already stored. Encrypting after download and pushing back doesn't un-transmit the plaintext, doesn't un-store it (it sat in the server's spool and its backups), and leaves you with plaintext-at-provider + ciphertext-at-provider + local copy. It protects a server that /later/ loses its plaintext — not one that never had it. If the requirement is "encrypted at rest," client-side is structurally the wrong end of the pipe. There is exactly one pipeline stage that owns "before storage," and it's the MTA. That's why the previous answer put it there.

/Which flips the earlier answer, and here's why/

notmuch already implements your client half completely: =index.decrypt=, =notmuch show --decrypt=true=, stashed session keys, =notmuch reindex --decrypt=. You need nothing on the client. But its native crypto path is /PGP/MIME-shaped/ — it handles =multipart/encrypted=. Whole-message inline armor (the naive =gpg --encrypt= from two turns ago) is just text/plain containing an armor blob to notmuch; =--decrypt=true= won't touch it, and you'd be piping individual messages through gpg by hand forever.

So "make gpg be enough" changes verdict the moment your client is notmuch: =gpg -e= is still the right /transform/, but the filter must emit =multipart/encrypted= with the original mime entity as payload (protected headers optional), not a bare armored blob. Same idea, one layer of framing higher — and that framing is the entire difference between "mail your client renders" and "an opaque attachment."

/The only legitimate version of your instinct/

If the real goal is "the copy sitting at my provider should become ciphertext going forward" — say you don't trust the provider's storage — that's not a hook, it's a one-shot migration: read, encrypt to =multipart/encrypted=, APPEND, EXPUNGE. It must be driven by something that owns the IMAP session (mbsync won't), it costs you UID churn and cross-client state, and it's best pointed at a separate archive account rather than your live mailbox. And note it still doesn't undo the years of plaintext the provider already has.

Bottom line: encrypt once, upstream, in the stage that precedes storage. Everything downstream of that — mbsync, notmuch, your MUA — is then just a reader of ciphertext, which is precisely the property you wanted.
