  "content": "P\bPo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx b\bbe\bef\bfo\bor\bre\be-\b-q\bqu\bue\beu\bue\be M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br s\bsu\bup\bpp\bpo\bor\brt\bt\n\n-------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n\nI\bIn\bnt\btr\bro\bod\bdu\buc\bct\bti\bio\bon\bn\n\nPostfix implements support for the Sendmail version 8 Milter (mail filter)\nprotocol. This protocol is used by applications that run outside the MTA to\ninspect SMTP events (CONNECT, DISCONNECT), SMTP commands (HELO, MAIL FROM,\netc.) as well as mail content (headers and body). All this happens before mail\nis queued.\n\nThe reason for adding Milter support to Postfix is that there exists a large\ncollection of applications, not only to block unwanted mail, but also to verify\nauthenticity (examples: OpenDKIM and DMARC) or to digitally sign mail (example:\nOpenDKIM). Having yet another Postfix-specific version of all that software is\na poor use of human and system resources.\n\nThe Milter protocol has evolved over time, and different Postfix versions\nimplement different feature sets. See the workarounds and limitations sections\nat the end of this document for differences between Postfix and Sendmail\nimplementations.\n\nThis document provides information on the following topics:\n\n  * How Milter applications plug into Postfix\n  * When Postfix and Milters inspect an SMTP session\n  * Building Milter applications\n  * Running Milter applications\n  * Configuring Postfix\n  * Workarounds\n  * Limitations\n\nH\bHo\bow\bw M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br a\bap\bpp\bpl\bli\bic\bca\bat\bti\bio\bon\bns\bs p\bpl\blu\bug\bg i\bin\bnt\bto\bo P\bPo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx\n\nThe Postfix Milter implementation uses two different lists of mail filters: one\nlist of filters for SMTP mail only, and one list of filters for non-SMTP mail.\nThe two lists have different capabilities, which is unfortunate. Avoiding this\nwould require major restructuring of Postfix.\n\n  * The SMTP-only filters handle mail that arrives via the Postfix smtpd(8)\n    server. They are typically used to filter unwanted mail and to sign mail\n    from authorized SMTP clients. You specify SMTP-only Milter applications\n    with the smtpd_milters parameter as described in a later section. Mail that\n    arrives via the Postfix smtpd(8) server is not filtered by the non-SMTP\n    filters that are described next.\n\n  * The non-SMTP filters handle mail that arrives via the Postfix sendmail(1)\n    command-line or via the Postfix qmqpd(8) server. They are typically used to\n    digitally sign mail only. Although non-SMTP filters can be used to filter\n    unwanted mail, they have limitations compared to the SMTP-only filters. You\n    specify non-SMTP Milter applications with the non_smtpd_milters parameter\n    as described in a later section.\n\nFor those who are familiar with the Postfix architecture, the figure below\nshows how Milter applications plug into Postfix. Names followed by a number are\nPostfix commands or server programs, while unnumbered names inside shaded areas\nrepresent Postfix queues. To avoid clutter, the path for local submission is\nsimplified (the OVERVIEW document has a more complete description of the\nPostfix architecture).\n\n               SMTP-only      non-SMTP\n                 filters       filters\n\n                     ^ |\n                     | v\n                                   ^ |\n                                   | |\n    Network ->   smtpd(8)          | |\n                                   | v\n\n                           \\\n\n    Network ->   qmqpd(8)  -> cleanup(8) -> incoming\n\n                           /\n\n                pickup(8)\n\n                      :\n\n    Local   -> sendmail(1)\n\nW\bWh\bhe\ben\bn P\bPo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx a\ban\bnd\bd M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\brs\bs i\bin\bns\bsp\bpe\bec\bct\bt a\ban\bn S\bSM\bMT\bTP\bP s\bse\bes\bss\bsi\bio\bon\bn\n\nGenerally, Postfix inspects information first, then the first configured\nMilter, the second configured Milter, and so on.\n\n  * With most SMTP commands: Postfix reviews one SMTP command, and if Postfix\n    does not reject it, Postfix passes the command to the first configured\n    Milter. If the first Milter does not reject the command, Postfix passes it\n    to the second configured Milter, and so on. This includes commands with an\n    envelope sender (MAIL FROM) or envelope recipient (RCPT TO). Postfix stores\n    the same envelope records in a queue file as when no Milters are\n    configured, including rewritten envelope addresses, expanded virtual\n    aliases, BCC addresses from sender/recipient_bcc_maps, and so on.\n\n  * With header/body content: Postfix may rewrite or reject header/body content\n    before it stores that content in the queue file; Postfix stores the same\n    header/body content as when no Milters are configured. If Postfix does not\n    reject the header/body content, Postfix passes it to the first configured\n    Milter which may modify or reject that content or may modify the stored\n    envelope. If the first Milter does not reject the header/body content,\n    Postfix passes it to the second configured Milter, and so on.\n\nDetails:\n\n  * Postfix hides its own Postfix-prepended Received: header, for compatibility\n    with Sendmail. Postfix does not hide other headers that Postfix or Milters\n    added or modified.\n\n  * When the Postfix SMTP server receives a sequence of one or more valid BDAT\n    commands, it generates one DATA command for the Milters.\n\n  * The Milter API does not support inspection of SMTP commands such as QUIT,\n    NOOP, or VRFY; the API supports only commands that are needed for email\n    delivery.\n\nB\bBu\bui\bil\bld\bdi\bin\bng\bg M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br a\bap\bpp\bpl\bli\bic\bca\bat\bti\bio\bon\bns\bs\n\nMilter applications have been written in C, Haskell, Java, Perl, Python, Rust,\nand more, but this document covers C applications only. For these, you need an\nobject library that implements the Sendmail 8 Milter protocol. Postfix\ncurrently does not provide such a library, but Sendmail does.\n\nSome systems install the Sendmail libmilter library by default. With other\nsystems, libmilter may be provided by a package (called \"sendmail-devel\" on\nsome Linux systems).\n\nOnce libmilter is installed, applications such as OpenDKIM and OpenDMARC build\nout of the box without requiring any tinkering:\n\n    $ g\bgz\bzc\bca\bat\bt o\bop\bpe\ben\bnd\bdk\bki\bim\bm-\b-x\bx.\b.y\by.\b.z\bz.\b.t\bta\bar\br.\b.g\bgz\bz |\b| t\bta\bar\br x\bxf\bf -\b-\n    $ c\bcd\bd o\bop\bpe\ben\bnd\bdk\bki\bim\bm-\b-x\bx.\b.y\by.\b.z\bz\n    $ .\b./\b/c\bco\bon\bnf\bfi\big\bgu\bur\bre\be .\b..\b..\b.o\bop\bpt\bti\bio\bon\bns\bs.\b..\b..\b.\n    $ m\bma\bak\bke\be\n    [...lots of output omitted...]\n    $ m\bma\bak\bke\be i\bin\bns\bst\bta\bal\bll\bl\n\nR\bRu\bun\bnn\bni\bin\bng\bg M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br a\bap\bpp\bpl\bli\bic\bca\bat\bti\bio\bon\bns\bs\n\nTo run a Milter application, see the documentation of the filter for options. A\ntypical command looks like this:\n\n    # /\b/s\bso\bom\bme\be/\b/w\bwh\bhe\ber\bre\be/\b/o\bop\bpe\ben\bnd\bdk\bki\bim\bm -\b-l\bl -\b-u\bu u\bus\bse\ber\bri\bid\bd -\b-p\bp i\bin\bne\bet\bt:\b:p\bpo\bor\brt\btn\bnu\bum\bmb\bbe\ber\br@\b@l\blo\boc\bca\bal\blh\bho\bos\bst\bt .\b..\b..\b.o\bot\bth\bhe\ber\br\n    o\bop\bpt\bti\bio\bon\bns\bs.\b..\b..\b.\n\nPlease specify a userid value that isn't used for other applications (not\n\"postfix\", not \"www\", etc.).\n\nC\bCo\bon\bnf\bfi\big\bgu\bur\bri\bin\bng\bg P\bPo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx\n\nLike Sendmail, Postfix has a lot of configuration options that control how it\ntalks to Milter applications. Besides global options that apply to all Milter\napplications, Postfix 3.0 and later support per-Milter timeouts, per-Milter\nerror handling, etc.\n\nInformation in this section:\n\n  * SMTP-Only Milter applications\n  * Non-SMTP Milter applications\n  * Milter error handling\n  * Milter protocol version\n  * Milter protocol timeouts\n  * Different settings for different Milter applications\n  * Different settings for different SMTP clients\n  * Sendmail macro emulation\n  * Postfix-specific macros\n  * What macros will Postfix send to Milters?\n\nS\bSM\bMT\bTP\bP-\b-O\bOn\bnl\bly\by M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br a\bap\bpp\bpl\bli\bic\bca\bat\bti\bio\bon\bns\bs\n\nThe SMTP-only Milter applications handle mail that arrives via the Postfix\nsmtpd(8) server. They are typically used to filter unwanted mail, and to sign\nmail from authorized SMTP clients. Mail that arrives via the Postfix smtpd(8)\nserver is not filtered by the non-SMTP filters that are described in the next\nsection.\n\n    NOTE for Postfix versions that have a mail_release_date before 20141018: do\n    not use the header_checks(5) IGNORE action to remove Postfix's own\n    Received: message header. This causes problems with mail signing filters.\n    Instead, keep Postfix's own Received: message header and use the\n    header_checks(5) REPLACE action to sanitize information.\n\nYou specify SMTP-only Milter applications (there can be more than one) with the\nsmtpd_milters parameter. Each Milter application is identified by the name of\nits listening socket; other Milter configuration options will be discussed in\nlater sections. Postfix sends commands to each Milter application in the order\nas configured with smtpd_milters. When a Milter application rejects a command,\nthat will override responses from other Milter applications.\n\n    /etc/postfix/main.cf:\n        # Milters for mail that arrives via the smtpd(8) server.\n        # See below for socket address syntax.\n        smtpd_milters = inet:localhost:portnumber ...other filters...\n\nThe general syntax for listening sockets is as follows:\n\n    u\bun\bni\bix\bx:\b:pathname\n        Connect to the local UNIX-domain server that is bound to the specified\n        pathname. If the smtpd(8) or cleanup(8) process runs chrooted, an\n        absolute pathname is interpreted relative to the Postfix queue\n        directory. On many systems, l\blo\boc\bca\bal\bl is a synonym for u\bun\bni\bix\bx\n\n    i\bin\bne\bet\bt:\b:host:\b:port\n        Connect to the specified TCP port on the specified local or remote\n        host. The host and port can be specified in numeric or symbolic form.\n\n        NOTE: Postfix syntax differs from Milter syntax which has the form\n        i\bin\bne\bet\bt:\b:port@\b@host.\n\nFor advanced configuration see \"Different settings for different SMTP clients\"\nand \"Different settings for different Milter applications\".\n\nN\bNo\bon\bn-\b-S\bSM\bMT\bTP\bP M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br a\bap\bpp\bpl\bli\bic\bca\bat\bti\bio\bon\bns\bs\n\nThe non-SMTP Milter applications handle mail that arrives via the Postfix\nsendmail(1) command-line or via the Postfix qmqpd(8) server. They are typically\nused to digitally sign mail. Although non-SMTP filters can be used to filter\nunwanted mail, there are limitations as discussed later in this section. Mail\nthat arrives via the Postfix smtpd(8) server is not filtered by the non-SMTP\nfilters.\n\nNOTE: Do not use the header_checks(5) IGNORE action to remove Postfix's own\nReceived: message header. This causes problems with mail signing filters.\nInstead, keep Postfix's own Received: message header and use the header_checks\n(5) REPLACE action to sanitize information.\n\nYou specify non-SMTP Milter applications with the non_smtpd_milters parameter.\nThis parameter uses the same syntax as the smtpd_milters parameter in the\nprevious section. As with the SMTP-only filters, you can specify more than one\nMilter application. Postfix sends commands to each Milter application in the\norder as configured with non_smtpd_milters. When a Milter application rejects a\ncommand, that will override responses from other Milter applications.\n\n    /etc/postfix/main.cf:\n        # Milters for non-SMTP mail.\n        # See below for socket address syntax.\n        non_smtpd_milters = inet:localhost:portnumber ...other filters...\n\nThere's one small complication when using Milter applications for non-SMTP\nmail: there is no SMTP session. To keep Milter applications happy, the Postfix\ncleanup(8) server actually has to simulate the SMTP client CONNECT and\nDISCONNECT events, and the SMTP client EHLO, MAIL FROM, RCPT TO and DATA\ncommands.\n\n  * When new mail arrives via the sendmail(1) command line, the Postfix cleanup\n    (8) server pretends that the mail arrives with ESMTP from \"localhost\" with\n    IP address \"127.0.0.1\". The result is very similar to what happens with\n    command line submissions in Sendmail version 8.12 and later, although\n    Sendmail uses a different mechanism to achieve this result.\n\n  * When new mail arrives via the qmqpd(8) server, the Postfix cleanup(8)\n    server pretends that the mail arrives with ESMTP, and uses the QMQPD client\n    hostname and IP address.\n\n  * When old mail is re-injected into the queue with \"postsuper -r\", the\n    Postfix cleanup(8) server uses the same client information that was used\n    when the mail arrived as new mail.\n\nThis generally works as expected, with only one exception: non-SMTP filters\nmust not REJECT or TEMPFAIL simulated RCPT TO commands. When a\nnon_smtpd_milters application REJECTs or TEMPFAILs a recipient, Postfix will\nreport a configuration error, and mail will stay in the queue.\n\nS\bSi\big\bgn\bni\bin\bng\bg i\bin\bnt\bte\ber\brn\bna\bal\bll\bly\by-\b-g\bge\ben\bne\ber\bra\bat\bte\bed\bd b\bbo\bou\bun\bnc\bce\be m\bme\bes\bss\bsa\bag\bge\bes\bs\n\nPostfix normally does not apply content filters to mail that is generated\ninternally such as bounces or Postmaster notifications. Filtering internally-\ngenerated bounces would result in loss of mail when a filter rejects a message,\nas the resulting double-bounce message would almost certainly also be blocked.\n\nTo sign Postfix's own bounce messages, enable filtering of internally-generated\nbounces (line 2 below), and don't reject any internally-generated bounces with\nnon_smtpd_milters, header_checks or body_checks (lines 3-5 below).\n\n    1 /etc/postfix/main.cf:\n    2     internal_mail_filter_classes = bounce\n    3     non_smtpd_milters = don't reject internally-generated bounces\n    4     header_checks = don't reject internally-generated bounces\n    5     body_checks = don't reject internally-generated bounces\n\nR\bRe\bec\bco\bog\bgn\bni\biz\bzi\bin\bng\bg m\bme\bes\bss\bsa\bag\bge\bes\bs g\bge\ben\bne\ber\bra\bat\bte\bed\bd b\bby\by t\bth\bhi\bis\bs P\bPo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx M\bMT\bTA\bA\n\nA Milter cannot reliably determine from message or envelope content whether a\ndelivery status notification was generated by this Postfix MTA, or whether it\nwas received from outside Postfix. For this purpose, Postfix >= 3.12 provides\nthe {postfix_internal_origin} macro. The value is one of the following:\n\nbounce (message generated by this Postfix MTA)\n    The message is a delivery status notification. An empty envelope sender\n    address indicates that the message is a notification for a message that was\n    not delivered or that was delayed, or that it contains a \"sendmail -v\" or\n    \"sendmail -bv\" delivery report; this is called a single bounce. A double-\n    bounce envelope sender address indicates that the message is a postmaster\n    copy of a single bounce, or a notification of an undeliverable single\n    bounce.\n\n    Bottom line: signing the message would allow a receiver to distinguish it\n    from a forged message.\n\nnotify (message generated by this Postfix MTA)\n    The message is an SMTP session transcript. The envelope sender contains a\n    double-bounce address.\n\n    Bottom line: signing the message would allow a receiver to distinguish it\n    from a forged message.\n\nverify (message generated by this Postfix MTA)\n    The message is an address verification probe, generated this Postfix MTA.\n    There is no benefit from signing the message as it will never be delivered.\n\n    Bottom line: Milters should not make any changes to such a message.\n\nnull or empty (message not generated by this Postfix MTA, or internally\nforwarded by this Postfix MTA)\n    The message was received with SMTP, QMQP, the Postfix sendmail(1) command,\n    or it was internally forwarded through an alias or dot-forward file.\n\nBy default, this macro is listed in milter_connect_macros. This allows a Milter\nto decide early whether to \"accept\" a message without further inspection.\n\nM\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br e\ber\brr\bro\bor\br h\bha\ban\bnd\bdl\bli\bin\bng\bg\n\nThe milter_default_action parameter specifies how Postfix handles Milter\napplication errors. The default action is to respond with a temporary error\nstatus, so that the client will try again later. Specify \"accept\" to accept\nmail without further Milter interaction, and \"reject\" to reject mail with a\npermanent status. The \"quarantine\" action is like \"accept\" but freezes the\nmessage in the \"hold\" queue, and is available with Postfix 2.6 or later.\n\n    /etc/postfix/main.cf:\n        # What to do in case of errors? Specify accept, reject, tempfail,\n        # or quarantine (Postfix 2.6 or later).\n        milter_default_action = tempfail\n\nSee \"Different settings for different Milter applications\" for advanced\nconfiguration options.\n\nM\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br p\bpr\bro\bot\bto\boc\bco\bol\bl v\bve\ber\brs\bsi\bio\bon\bn\n\nAs Postfix is not built with the Sendmail libmilter library, you may need to\nconfigure the Milter protocol version that Postfix should use. The default\nversion is 6 (before Postfix 2.6 the default version is 2).\n\n    /etc/postfix/main.cf:\n        # Postfix >= 2.6\n        milter_protocol = 6\n        # 2.3 <= Postfix <= 2.5\n        milter_protocol = 2\n\nIf the Postfix milter_protocol setting specifies a too low version, the\nlibmilter library will log an error message like this:\n\n    application name: st_optionneg[xxxxx]: 0xyy does not fulfill action\n    requirements 0xzz\n\nThe remedy is to increase the Postfix milter_protocol version number. See,\nhowever, the limitations section below for features that aren't supported by\nPostfix.\n\nWith Postfix 2.7 and earlier, if the Postfix milter_protocol setting specifies\na too high version, the libmilter library simply hangs up without logging a\nwarning, and you see a Postfix warning message like one of the following:\n\n    warning: milter inet:host:port: can't read packet header: Unknown error : 0\n    warning: milter inet:host:port: can't read packet header: Success\n    warning: milter inet:host:port: can't read SMFIC_DATA reply packet header:\n    No such file or directory\n\nThe remedy is to lower the Postfix milter_protocol version number. Postfix 2.8\nand later will automatically turn off protocol features that the application's\nlibmilter library does not expect.\n\nSee \"Different settings for different Milter applications\" for advanced\nconfiguration options.\n\nM\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br p\bpr\bro\bot\bto\boc\bco\bol\bl t\bti\bim\bme\beo\bou\but\bts\bs\n\nPostfix uses different time limits at different Milter protocol stages. The\ntable shows the timeout settings and the corresponding protocol stages (EOH =\nend of headers; EOM = end of message).\n\n     _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b\n    |P\bPo\bos\bst\btf\bfi\bix\bx p\bpa\bar\bra\bam\bme\bet\bte\ber\br     |T\bTi\bim\bme\be l\bli\bim\bmi\bit\bt|M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br p\bpr\bro\bot\bto\boc\bco\bol\bl s\bst\bta\bag\bge\be          |\n    |_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b |\n    |milter_connect_timeout|30s       |CONNECT                        |\n    |_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b |\n    |milter_command_timeout|30s       |HELO, MAIL, RCPT, DATA, UNKNOWN|\n    |_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b |\n    |milter_content_timeout|300s      |HEADER, EOH, BODY, EOM         |\n    |_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b |\n\nBeware: 30s may be too short for Milter applications that do lots of DNS\nlookups. However, if you increase the above timeouts too much, remote SMTP\nclients may hang up and mail may be delivered multiple times. This is an\ninherent problem with before-queue filtering.\n\nSee \"Different settings for different Milter applications\" for advanced\nconfiguration options.\n\nD\bDi\bif\bff\bfe\ber\bre\ben\bnt\bt s\bse\bet\btt\bti\bin\bng\bgs\bs f\bfo\bor\br d\bdi\bif\bff\bfe\ber\bre\ben\bnt\bt M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br a\bap\bpp\bpl\bli\bic\bca\bat\bti\bio\bon\bns\bs\n\nThe previous sections list a number of Postfix main.cf parameters that control\ntime limits and other settings for all Postfix Milter clients. This is\nsufficient for simple configurations. With more complex configurations it\nbecomes desirable to have different settings for different Milter clients. This\nis supported with Postfix 3.0 and later.\n\nThe following example shows a \"non-critical\" Milter client with a short connect\ntimeout, and with \"accept\" as default action when the service is unvailable.\n\n    1 /etc/postfix/main.cf:\n    2     smtpd_milters = { inet:host:port,\n    3         connect_timeout=10s, default_action=accept }\n\nInstead of a server endpoint, we now have a list enclosed in {}.\n\n  * Line 2: The first item in the list is the server endpoint. This supports\n    the exact same \"inet\" and \"unix\" syntax as described earlier.\n\n  * Line 3: The remainder of the list contains per-Milter settings. These\n    settings override global main.cf parameters, and have the same name as\n    those parameters, without the \"milter_\" prefix. The per-Milter settings\n    that are supported as of Postfix 3.0 are command_timeout, connect_timeout,\n    content_timeout, default_action, and protocol.\n\nInside the list, syntax is similar to what we already know from main.cf: items\nseparated by space or comma. There is one difference: y\byo\bou\bu m\bmu\bus\bst\bt e\ben\bnc\bcl\blo\bos\bse\be a\ba\ns\bse\bet\btt\bti\bin\bng\bg i\bin\bn p\bpa\bar\bre\ben\bnt\bth\bhe\bes\bse\bes\bs,\b, a\bas\bs i\bin\bn \"\b\"{\b{ n\bna\bam\bme\be =\b= v\bva\bal\blu\bue\be }\b}\"\b\",\b, i\bif\bf y\byo\bou\bu w\bwa\ban\bnt\bt t\bto\bo h\bha\bav\bve\be s\bsp\bpa\bac\bce\be o\bor\br\nc\bco\bom\bmm\bma\ba w\bwi\bit\bth\bhi\bin\bn a\ba v\bva\bal\blu\bue\be o\bor\br a\bar\bro\bou\bun\bnd\bd \"\b\"=\b=\"\b\".\n\nD\bDi\bif\bff\bfe\ber\bre\ben\bnt\bt s\bse\bet\btt\bti\bin\bng\bgs\bs f\bfo\bor\br d\bdi\bif\bff\bfe\ber\bre\ben\bnt\bt S\bSM\bMT\bTP\bP c\bcl\bli\bie\ben\bnt\bts\bs\n\nThe smtpd_milter_maps feature supports different Milter settings for different\nclient IP addresses. Lookup results override the global smtpd_milters setting,\nand have the same syntax. For example, to disable Milter settings for local\naddress ranges:\n\n/etc/postfix/main.cf:\n    smtpd_milter_maps = cidr:/etc/postfix/smtpd_milter_map\n    smtpd_milters = inet:host:port, { inet:host:port, ... }, ...\n\n/etc/postfix/smtpd_milter_map:\n    # Disable Milters for local clients.\n    127.0.0.0/8    DISABLE\n    192.168.0.0/16 DISABLE\n    ::/64          DISABLE\n    2001:db8::/32  DISABLE\n\nThis feature is available with Postfix 3.2 and later.\n\nS\bSe\ben\bnd\bdm\bma\bai\bil\bl m\bma\bac\bcr\bro\bo e\bem\bmu\bul\bla\bat\bti\bio\bon\bn\n\nPostfix emulates a limited number of Sendmail macros, as shown in the table.\nSome macro values depend on whether a recipient is rejected (rejected\nrecipients are available on request by the Milter application). Different\nmacros are available at different Milter protocol stages (EOH = end-of-header,\nEOM = end-of-message); their availability is not always the same as in\nSendmail. See the workarounds section below for solutions.\n\n     _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b\n    |S\bSe\ben\bnd\bdm\bma\bai\bil\bl m\bma\bac\bcr\bro\bo      |M\bMi\bil\blt\bte\ber\br p\bpr\bro\bot\bto\boc\bco\bol\bl s\bst\bta\bag\bge\be    |D\bDe\bes\bsc\bcr\bri\bip\bpt\bti\bio\bon\bn               |\n    |_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b|_\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b _\b |\n    |i                   |DATA, EOH, EOM           |Queue ID, also Postfix    |\n    |                    | Contents of https://www.postfix.org/MILTER_README.html:
Postfix before-queue Milter support

# Postfix before-queue Milter support

## Introduction

Postfix implements support for the Sendmail version 8 Milter (mail filter) protocol. This protocol is used by applications that run outside the MTA to inspect SMTP events (CONNECT, DISCONNECT), SMTP commands (HELO, MAIL FROM, etc.) as well as mail content (headers and body). All this happens before mail is queued.

The reason for adding Milter support to Postfix is that there exists a large collection of applications, not only to block unwanted mail, but also to verify authenticity (examples: OpenDKIM and DMARC ) or to digitally sign mail (example: OpenDKIM). Having yet another Postfix-specific version of all that software is a poor use of human and system resources.

The Milter protocol has evolved over time, and different Postfix versions implement different feature sets. See the workarounds and limitations sections at the end of this document for differences between Postfix and Sendmail implementations.

This document provides information on the following topics:

,* How Milter applications plug into Postfix
,* When Postfix and Milters inspect an SMTP session
,* Building Milter applications
,* Running Milter applications
,* Configuring Postfix
,* Workarounds
,* Limitations

## How Milter applications plug into Postfix

The Postfix Milter implementation uses two different lists of mail filters: one list of filters for SMTP mail only, and one list of filters for non-SMTP mail. The two lists have different capabilities, which is unfortunate. Avoiding this would require major restructuring of Postfix.

,* The SMTP-only filters handle mail that arrives via the Postfix smtpd(8) server. They are typically used to filter unwanted mail and to sign mail from authorized SMTP clients. You specify SMTP-only Milter applications with the smtpd\_milters parameter as described in a later section. Mail that arrives via the Postfix smtpd(8) server is not filtered by the non-SMTP filters that are described next.
,* The non-SMTP filters handle mail that arrives via the Postfix sendmail(1) command-line or via the Postfix qmqpd(8) server. They are typically used to digitally sign mail only. Although non-SMTP filters can be used to filter unwanted mail, they have limitations compared to the SMTP-only filters. You specify non-SMTP Milter applications with the non\_smtpd\_milters parameter as described in a later section.

For those who are familiar with the Postfix architecture, the figure below shows how Milter applications plug into Postfix. Names followed by a number are Postfix commands or server programs, while unnumbered names inside shaded areas represent Postfix queues. To avoid clutter, the path for local submission is simplified (the OVERVIEW document has a more complete description of the Postfix architecture).

> |  |  |
> | --- | --- |
> | SMTP-only filters | non-SMTP filters |
> | |  |  | | --- | --- | | ^ | | | v | | |  |  | | --- | --- | | ^ | | | | | | | v | |
> | Network | -> | smtpd(8) |
> | \ |
> | Network | -> | qmqpd(8) | -> | cleanup(8) | -> | incoming |
> | / |
> | pickup(8) |
> | : |
> | Local | -> | sendmail(1) |

## When Postfix and Milters inspect an SMTP session

Generally, Postfix inspects information first, then the first configured Milter, the second configured Milter, and so on.

,* With most SMTP commands: Postfix reviews one SMTP command, and if Postfix does not reject it, Postfix passes the command to the first configured Milter. If the first Milter does not reject the command, Postfix passes it to the second configured Milter, and so on. This includes commands with an envelope sender (MAIL FROM) or envelope recipient (RCPT TO). Postfix stores the same envelope records in a queue file as when no Milters are configured, including rewritten envelope addresses, expanded virtual aliases, BCC addresses from sender/recipient\_bcc\_maps, and so on.
,* With header/body content: Postfix may rewrite or reject header/body content before it stores that content in the queue file; Postfix stores the same header/body content as when no Milters are configured. If Postfix does not reject the header/body content, Postfix passes it to the first configured Milter which may modify or reject that content or may modify the stored envelope. If the first Milter does not reject the header/body content, Postfix passes it to the second configured Milter, and so on.

Details:

,* Postfix hides its own Postfix-prepended Received: header, for compatibility with Sendmail. Postfix does not hide other headers that Postfix or Milters added or modified.
,* When the Postfix SMTP server receives a sequence of one or more valid BDAT commands, it generates one DATA command for the Milters.
,* The Milter API does not support inspection of SMTP commands such as QUIT, NOOP, or VRFY; the API supports only commands that are needed for email delivery.

## Building Milter applications

Milter applications have been written in C, Haskell, Java, Perl, Python, Rust, and more, but this document covers C applications only. For these, you need an object library that implements the Sendmail 8 Milter protocol. Postfix currently does not provide such a library, but Sendmail does.

Some systems install the Sendmail libmilter library by default. With other systems, libmilter may be provided by a package (called "sendmail-devel" on some Linux systems).

Once libmilter is installed, applications such as OpenDKIM and OpenDMARC build out of the box without requiring any tinkering:

> ```
> $ gzcat opendkim-x.y.z.tar.gz | tar xf - $ cd opendkim-x.y.z $ ./configure ...options... $ make [...lots of output omitted...] $ make install
> ```

## Running Milter applications

To run a Milter application, see the documentation of the filter for options. A typical command looks like this:

> ```
> # /some/where/opendkim -l -u userid -p inet:portnumber@localhost ...other options...
> ```

Please specify a userid value that isn't used for other applications (not "postfix", not "www", etc.).

## Configuring Postfix

Like Sendmail, Postfix has a lot of configuration options that control how it talks to Milter applications. Besides global options that apply to all Milter applications, Postfix 3.0 and later support per-Milter timeouts, per-Milter error handling, etc.

Information in this section:

,* SMTP-Only Milter applications
,* Non-SMTP Milter applications
,* Milter error handling
,* Milter protocol version
,* Milter protocol timeouts
,* Different settings for different Milter applications
,* Different settings for different SMTP clients
,* Sendmail macro emulation
,* Postfix-specific macros
,* What macros will Postfix send to Milters?

### SMTP-Only Milter applications

The SMTP-only Milter applications handle mail that arrives via the Postfix smtpd(8) server. They are typically used to filter unwanted mail, and to sign mail from authorized SMTP clients. Mail that arrives via the Postfix smtpd(8) server is not filtered by the non-SMTP filters that are described in the next section.

> NOTE for Postfix versions that have a mail\_release\_date before 20141018: do not use the header\_checks(5) IGNORE action to remove Postfix's own Received: message header. This causes problems with mail signing filters. Instead, keep Postfix's own Received: message header and use the header\_checks(5) REPLACE action to sanitize information.

You specify SMTP-only Milter applications (there can be more than one) with the smtpd\_milters parameter. Each Milter application is identified by the name of its listening socket; other Milter configuration options will be discussed in later sections. Postfix sends commands to each Milter application in the order as configured with smtpd\_milters. When a Milter application rejects a command, that will override responses from other Milter applications.

> ```
> /etc/postfix/main.cf: # Milters for mail that arrives via the smtpd(8) server. # See below for socket address syntax. smtpd_milters = inet:localhost:portnumber ...other filters...
> ```

The general syntax for listening sockets is as follows:

> unix:pathname
> :   Connect to the local UNIX-domain server that is bound to the specified pathname. If the smtpd(8) or cleanup(8) process runs chrooted, an absolute pathname is interpreted relative to the Postfix queue directory. On many systems, local is a synonym for unix
>
> inet:host:port
> :   Connect to the specified TCP port on the specified local or remote host. The host and port can be specified in numeric or symbolic form.
>
>     NOTE: Postfix syntax differs from Milter syntax which has the form inet:port@host.

For advanced configuration see "Different settings for different SMTP clients" and "Different settings for different Milter applications".

### Non-SMTP Milter applications

The non-SMTP Milter applications handle mail that arrives via the Postfix sendmail(1) command-line or via the Postfix qmqpd(8) server. They are typically used to digitally sign mail. Although non-SMTP filters can be used to filter unwanted mail, there are limitations as discussed later in this section. Mail that arrives via the Postfix smtpd(8) server is not filtered by the non-SMTP filters.

NOTE: Do not use the header\_checks(5) IGNORE action to remove Postfix's own Received: message header. This causes problems with mail signing filters. Instead, keep Postfix's own Received: message header and use the header\_checks(5) REPLACE action to sanitize information.

You specify non-SMTP Milter applications with the non\_smtpd\_milters parameter. This parameter uses the same syntax as the smtpd\_milters parameter in the previous section. As with the SMTP-only filters, you can specify more than one Milter application. Postfix sends commands to each Milter application in the order as configured with non\_smtpd\_milters. When a Milter application rejects a command, that will override responses from other Milter applications.

> ```
> /etc/postfix/main.cf: # Milters for non-SMTP mail. # See below for socket address syntax. non_smtpd_milters = inet:localhost:portnumber ...other filters...
> ```

There's one small complication when using Milter applications for non-SMTP mail: there is no SMTP session. To keep Milter applications happy, the Postfix cleanup(8) server actually has to simulate the SMTP client CONNECT and DISCONNECT events, and the SMTP client EHLO, MAIL FROM, RCPT TO and DATA commands.

,* When new mail arrives via the sendmail(1) command line, the Postfix cleanup(8) server pretends that the mail arrives with ESMTP from "localhost" with IP address "127.0.0.1". The result is very similar to what happens with command line submissions in Sendmail version 8.12 and later, although Sendmail uses a different mechanism to achieve this result.
,* When new mail arrives via the qmqpd(8) server, the Postfix cleanup(8) server pretends that the mail arrives with ESMTP, and uses the QMQPD client hostname and IP address.
,* When old mail is re-injected into the queue with "postsuper -r", the Postfix cleanup(8) server uses the same client information that was used when the mail arrived as new mail.

This generally works as expected, with only one exception: non-SMTP filters must not REJECT or TEMPFAIL simulated RCPT TO commands. When a non\_smtpd\_milters application REJECTs or TEMPFAILs a recipient, Postfix will report a configuration error, and mail will stay in the queue.

#### Signing internally-generated bounce messages

Postfix normally does not apply content filters to mail that is generated internally such as bounces or Postmaster notifications. Filtering internally-generated bounces would result in loss of mail when a filter rejects a message, as the resulting double-bounce message would almost certainly also be blocked.

To sign Postfix's own bounce messages, enable filtering of internally-generated bounces (line 2 below), and don't reject any internally-generated bounces with non\_smtpd\_milters, header\_checks or body\_checks (lines 3-5 below).

> ```
> 1 /etc/postfix/main.cf: 2 internal_mail_filter_classes = bounce 3 non_smtpd_milters = don't reject internally-generated bounces 4 header_checks = don't reject internally-generated bounces 5 body_checks = don't reject internally-generated bounces
> ```

#### Recognizing messages generated by this Postfix MTA

A Milter cannot reliably determine from message or envelope content whether a delivery status notification was generated by this Postfix MTA, or whether it was received from outside Postfix. For this purpose, Postfix ≥ 3.12 provides the {postfix\_internal\_origin} macro. The value is one of the following:

bounce (message generated by this Postfix MTA)
:   The message is a delivery status notification. An empty envelope sender address indicates that the message is a notification for a message that was not delivered or that was delayed, or that it contains a "sendmail -v" or "sendmail -bv" delivery report; this is called a single bounce. A double-bounce envelope sender address indicates that the message is a postmaster copy of a single bounce, or a notification of an undeliverable single bounce.

    Bottom line: signing the message would allow a receiver to distinguish it from a forged message.

notify (message generated by this Postfix MTA)
:   The message is an SMTP session transcript. The envelope sender contains a double-bounce address.

    Bottom line: signing the message would allow a receiver to distinguish it from a forged message.

verify (message generated by this Postfix MTA)
:   The message is an address verification probe, generated this Postfix MTA. There is no benefit from signing the message as it will never be delivered.

    Bottom line: Milters should not make any changes to such a message.

null or empty (message not generated by this Postfix MTA, or internally forwarded by this Postfix MTA)
:   The message was received with SMTP, QMQP, the Postfix sendmail(1) command, or it was internally forwarded through an alias or dot-forward file.

By default, this macro is listed in milter\_connect\_macros. This allows a Milter to decide early whether to "accept" a message without further inspection.

### Milter error handling

The milter\_default\_action parameter specifies how Postfix handles Milter application errors. The default action is to respond with a temporary error status, so that the client will try again later. Specify "accept" to accept mail without further Milter interaction, and "reject" to reject mail with a permanent status. The "quarantine" action is like "accept" but freezes the message in the "hold" queue, and is available with Postfix 2.6 or later.

> ```
> /etc/postfix/main.cf: # What to do in case of errors? Specify accept, reject, tempfail, # or quarantine (Postfix 2.6 or later). milter_default_action = tempfail
> ```

See "Different settings for different Milter applications" for advanced configuration options.

### Milter protocol version

As Postfix is not built with the Sendmail libmilter library, you may need to configure the Milter protocol version that Postfix should use. The default version is 6 (before Postfix 2.6 the default version is 2).

> ```
> /etc/postfix/main.cf: # Postfix ≥ 2.6 milter_protocol = 6 # 2.3 ≤ Postfix ≤ 2.5 milter_protocol = 2
> ```

If the Postfix milter\_protocol setting specifies a too low version, the libmilter library will log an error message like this:

> ```
> application name: st_optionneg[xxxxx]: 0xyy does not fulfill action requirements 0xzz
> ```

The remedy is to increase the Postfix milter\_protocol version number. See, however, the limitations section below for features that aren't supported by Postfix.

With Postfix 2.7 and earlier, if the Postfix milter\_protocol setting specifies a too high version, the libmilter library simply hangs up without logging a warning, and you see a Postfix warning message like one of the following:

> ```
> warning: milter inet:host:port: can't read packet header: Unknown error : 0 warning: milter inet:host:port: can't read packet header: Success warning: milter inet:host:port: can't read SMFIC_DATA reply packet header: No such file or directory
> ```

The remedy is to lower the Postfix milter\_protocol version number. Postfix 2.8 and later will automatically turn off protocol features that the application's libmilter library does not expect.

See "Different settings for different Milter applications" for advanced configuration options.

### Milter protocol timeouts

Postfix uses different time limits at different Milter protocol stages. The table shows the timeout settings and the corresponding protocol stages (EOH = end of headers; EOM = end of message).

> | Postfix parameter | Time limit | Milter protocol stage |
> | --- | --- | --- |
> | milter\_connect\_timeout | 30s | CONNECT |
> | milter\_command\_timeout | 30s | HELO, MAIL, RCPT, DATA, UNKNOWN |
> | milter\_content\_timeout | 300s | HEADER, EOH, BODY, EOM |

Beware: 30s may be too short for Milter applications that do lots of DNS lookups. However, if you increase the above timeouts too much, remote SMTP clients may hang up and mail may be delivered multiple times. This is an inherent problem with before-queue filtering.

See "Different settings for different Milter applications" for advanced configuration options.

### Different settings for different Milter applications

The previous sections list a number of Postfix main.cf parameters that control time limits and other settings for all Postfix Milter clients. This is sufficient for simple configurations. With more complex configurations it becomes desirable to have different settings for different Milter clients. This is supported with Postfix 3.0 and later.

The following example shows a "non-critical" Milter client with a short connect timeout, and with "accept" as default action when the service is unvailable.

> ```
> 1 /etc/postfix/main.cf: 2 smtpd_milters = { inet:host:port, 3 connect_timeout=10s, default_action=accept }
> ```

Instead of a server endpoint, we now have a list enclosed in {}.

,* Line 2: The first item in the list is the server endpoint. This supports the exact same "inet" and "unix" syntax as described earlier.
,* Line 3: The remainder of the list contains per-Milter settings. These settings override global main.cf parameters, and have the same name as those parameters, without the "milter\_" prefix. The per-Milter settings that are supported as of Postfix 3.0 are command\_timeout, connect\_timeout, content\_timeout, default\_action, and protocol.

Inside the list, syntax is similar to what we already know from main.cf: items separated by space or comma. There is one difference: you must enclose a setting in parentheses, as in "{ name = value }", if you want to have space or comma within a value or around "=".

### Different settings for different SMTP clients

The smtpd\_milter\_maps feature supports different Milter settings for different client IP addresses. Lookup results override the global smtpd\_milters setting, and have the same syntax. For example, to disable Milter settings for local address ranges:

```
/etc/postfix/main.cf: smtpd_milter_maps = cidr:/etc/postfix/smtpd_milter_map smtpd_milters = inet:host:port, { inet:host:port, ... }, ... /etc/postfix/smtpd_milter_map: # Disable Milters for local clients. 127.0.0.0/8 DISABLE 192.168.0.0/16 DISABLE ::/64 DISABLE 2001:db8::/32 DISABLE
```

This feature is available with Postfix 3.2 and later.

### Sendmail macro emulation

Postfix emulates a limited number of Sendmail macros, as shown in the table. Some macro values depend on whether a recipient is rejected (rejected recipients are available on request by the Milter application). Different macros are available at different Milter protocol stages (EOH = end-of-header, EOM = end-of-message); their availability is not always the same as in Sendmail. See the workarounds section below for solutions.

> | Sendmail macro | Milter protocol stage | Description |
> | --- | --- | --- |
> | i | DATA, EOH, EOM | Queue ID, also Postfix queue file name |
> | j | Always | Value of myhostname |
> | \_ | Always | The validated client name and address |
> | {auth\_authen} | MAIL, DATA, EOH, EOM | SASL login name |
> | {auth\_author} | MAIL, DATA, EOH, EOM | SASL sender |
> | {auth\_type} | MAIL, DATA, EOH, EOM | SASL login method |
> | {client\_addr} | Always | Remote client IP address |
> | {client\_connections} | Always (only with smtpd\_milters) | Connection concurrency for this client (zero if the client is excluded from all smtpd\_client\_\* limits). |
> | {client\_name} | Always | Remote client hostname When address → name lookup or name → address verification fails: "unknown" |
> | {client\_port} | Always (Postfix ≥2.5) | Remote client TCP port |
> | {client\_ptr} | CONNECT, HELO, MAIL, DATA | Client name from address → name lookup When address → name lookup fails: "unknown" |
> | {cert\_issuer} | HELO, MAIL, DATA | TLS client certificate issuer |
> | {cert\_subject} | HELO, MAIL, DATA | TLS client certificate subject |
> | {cipher\_bits} | HELO, MAIL, DATA | TLS session key size |
> | {cipher} | HELO, MAIL, DATA | TLS cipher |
> | {daemon\_addr} | Always (Postfix ≥3.2) | Local server IP address |
> | {daemon\_name} | Always | value of milter\_macro\_daemon\_name |
> | {daemon\_port} | Always (Postfix ≥3.2) | Local server TCP port |
> | {mail\_addr} | MAIL | Sender address |
> | {mail\_host} | MAIL (Postfix ≥ 2.6, only with smtpd\_milters) | Sender next-hop destination |
> | {mail\_mailer} | MAIL (Postfix ≥ 2.6, only with smtpd\_milters) | Sender mail delivery transport |
> | {rcpt\_addr} | RCPT | Recipient address With rejected recipient: descriptive text |
> | {rcpt\_host} | RCPT (Postfix ≥ 2.6, only with smtpd\_milters) | Recipient next-hop destination With rejected recipient: enhanced status code |
> | {rcpt\_mailer} | RCPT (Postfix ≥ 2.6, only with smtpd\_milters) | Recipient mail delivery transport With rejected recipient: "error" |
> | {tls\_sni} | HELO, MAIL, DATA (Postfix ≥3.12) | TLS server name indication |
> | {tls\_version} | HELO, MAIL, DATA | TLS protocol version |
> | v | Always | value of milter\_macro\_v |

### Postfix-specific macros

In addition to the Sendmail macros discussed above, Postfix implements a small number of macros that expose Postfix-specific information.

> | Postfix macro | Milter protocol stage | Description |
> | --- | --- | --- |
> | {postfix\_internal\_origin} | Always (Postfix ≥ 3.12) | See Recognizing messages generated by this Postfix MTA |

### What macros will Postfix send to Milters?

Postfix sends specific sets of macros at different Milter protocol stages. The names of these macros are configured with the parameters shown in the table below (EOH = end of headers; EOM = end of message). Some lists require a minimum Milter protocol version.

As of Sendmail 8.14.0, Milter applications can specify what macros they want to receive at different Milter protocol stages. An application-specified list takes precedence over a Postfix-specified list.

> | Postfix parameter | Milter protocol version | Milter protocol stage |
> | --- | --- | --- |
> | milter\_connect\_macros | 2 or higher | CONNECT |
> | milter\_helo\_macros | 2 or higher | HELO/EHLO |
> | milter\_mail\_macros | 2 or higher | MAIL FROM |
> | milter\_rcpt\_macros | 2 or higher | RCPT TO |
> | milter\_data\_macros | 4 or higher | DATA |
> | milter\_end\_of\_header\_macros | 6 or higher | EOH |
> | milter\_end\_of\_data\_macros | 2 or higher | EOM |
> | milter\_unknown\_command\_macros | 3 or higher | unknown command |

By default, Postfix will send only macros whose values have been updated with information from main.cf or master.cf, from an SMTP session (for example; SASL login, or TLS certificates) or from a Mail delivery transaction (for example; queue ID, sender, or recipient).

To force a macro to be sent even when its value has not been updated, you may specify macro default values with the milter\_macro\_defaults parameter. Specify zero or more name=value pairs separated by comma or whitespace; you may even specify macro names that Postfix does not know about!

## Workarounds

,* To avoid breaking DKIM etc. signatures with an SMTP-based content filter, update the before-filter SMTP client in master.cf, and add a line with "-o disable\_mime\_output\_conversion=yes" (note: no spaces around the "="). For details, see the advanced content filter example.

  ```
  /etc/postfix/master.cf: # ============================================================= # service type private unpriv chroot wakeup maxproc command # (yes) (yes) (yes) (never) (100) # ============================================================= scan unix - - n - 10 smtp -o smtp_send_xforward_command=yes -o disable_mime_output_conversion=yes -o smtp_generic_maps=
  ```
,* Some Milter applications use the "{if\_addr}" macro to recognize local mail; this macro does not exist in Postfix. Workaround: use the "{daemon\_addr}" (Postfix ≥ 3.2) or "{client\_addr}" macro instead.
,* Some Milter applications log a warning that looks like this:

  > ```
  > sid-filter[36540]: WARNING: sendmail symbol 'i' not available
  > ```

  And they may insert an ugly message header with "unknown-msgid" like this:

  > ```
  > X-SenderID: Sendmail Sender-ID Filter vx.y.z host.example.com <unknown-msgid>
  > ```

  The problem is that Milter applications expect that the queue ID is known before the MTA accepts the MAIL FROM (sender) command. Postfix does not choose a queue ID, which is used as the queue file name, until after it accepts the first valid RCPT TO (recipient) command.

  If you experience the ugly header problem, see if a recent version of the Milter application fixes it. For example, current versions of dkim-filter and dk-filter already have code that looks up the Postfix queue ID at a later protocol stage, and sid-filter version 1.0.0 no longer includes the queue ID in the message header.

  To fix the ugly message header, you will need to add code that looks up the Postfix queue ID at some later point in time. The example below adds the lookup after the end-of-message.

  + Edit the filter source file (typically named xxx-filter/xxx-filter.c or similar).
  + Look up the mlfi\_eom() function and add code near the top shown as bold text below:
  > ```
  > dfc = cc->cctx_msg; assert(dfc != NULL); /* Determine the job ID for logging. */ if (dfc->mctx_jobid == 0 || strcmp(dfc->mctx_jobid, JOBIDUNKNOWN) == 0) { char *jobid = smfi_getsymval(ctx, "i"); if (jobid != 0) dfc->mctx_jobid = jobid; }
  > ```

  NOTES:

  + Different mail filters use slightly different names for variables. If the above code does not compile, look elsewhere in the mail filter source file for code that looks up the "i" macro value, and copy that code.
  + This change fixes only the ugly message header, but not the WARNING message. Fortunately, many Milters log that message only once.

## Limitations

This section lists limitations of the Postfix Milter implementation. Some limitations will be removed as the implementation is extended over time. Of course the usual limitations of before-queue filtering will always apply. See the CONTENT\_INSPECTION\_README document for a discussion.

,* The Milter protocol has evolved over time. Therefore, different Postfix versions implement different feature sets.

  | Postfix | Supported Milter requests |
  | --- | --- |
  | 2.6 | All Milter requests of Sendmail 8.14.0 (see notes below). |
  | 2.5 | All Milter requests of Sendmail 8.14.0, except: SMFIP\_RCPT\_REJ (report rejected recipients to the mail filter), SMFIR\_CHGFROM (replace sender, with optional ESMTP parameters), SMFIR\_ADDRCPT\_PAR (add recipient, with optional ESMTP parameters). |
  | 2.4 | All Milter requests of Sendmail 8.13.0. |
  | 2.3 | All Milter requests of Sendmail 8.13.0, except: SMFIR\_REPLBODY (replace message body). |
,* For Milter applications that are written in C, you need to use the Sendmail libmilter library.
,* Postfix has TWO sets of mail filters: filters that are used for SMTP mail only (specified with the smtpd\_milters parameter), and filters for non-SMTP mail (specified with the non\_smtpd\_milters parameter). The non-SMTP filters are primarily for local submissions.

  When mail is filtered by non\_smtpd\_milters, the Postfix cleanup(8) server has to simulate SMTP client requests. This works as expected, with only one exception: non\_smtpd\_milters must not REJECT or TEMPFAIL simulated RCPT TO commands. When this rule is violated, Postfix will report a configuration error, and mail will stay in the queue.
,* When you use the before-queue content filter for incoming SMTP mail (see SMTPD\_PROXY\_README), Milter applications have access only to the SMTP command information; they have no access to the message header or body, and cannot make modifications to the message or to the envelope.
,* Postfix 3.3 and later support the ESMTP parameters RET and ENVID in requests to replace the envelope sender (SMFIR\_CHGFROM). Postfix logs a warning message when a Milter application supplies other ESMTP parameters:

  ```
  warning: queue-id: cleanup_chg_from: ignoring bad ESMTP parameter "whatever" in SMFI_CHGFROM request
  ```
,* Postfix 3.0 and later support the ESMTP parameters NOTIFY and ORCPT in requests to add an envelope recipient. Postfix logs a warning message when a Milter application supplies other ESMTP parameters:

  ```
  warning: queue-id: cleanup_add_rcpt: ignoring ESMTP argument from Milter or header/body_checks: "whatever"
  ```
,* Postfix 2.6 and later ignore optional ESMTP parameters in requests to replace the sender (SMFIR\_CHGFROM) or to append a recipient (SMFIR\_ADDRCPT\_PAR). Postfix logs a warning message when a Milter application supplies such ESMTP parameters:

  ```
  warning: queue-id: cleanup_chg_from: ignoring ESMTP arguments "whatever" warning: queue-id: cleanup_add_rcpt: ignoring ESMTP arguments "whatever"
  ```
,* Postfix 2.3 does not implement requests to replace

{
  "url": "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sdgathman/pymilter/master/README.md",
  "finalUrl": "https://raw.githubusercontent.com/sdgathman/pymilter/master/README.md",
  "contentType": "text/plain; charset=utf-8",
  "title": "",
  "retrievalMethod": "request",
  "truncated": false,
  "content": "# Abstract\n\nThis is a python extension module to enable python scripts to attach to\nSendmail's libmilter API, enabling filtering of messages as they arrive.\nSince it's a script, you can do anything you want to the message - screen\nout viruses, collect statistics, add or modify headers, etc.  You can, at\nany point, tell Sendmail to reject, discard, or accept the message.\n\nAdditional python modules provide for navigating and modifying MIME parts, and\nsending DSNs or doing CBVs.\n\n# Requirements\n\nPython milter extension: https://pypi.org/project/pymilter/\nPython: http://www.python.org\nSendmail: http://www.sendmail.org\n   or\nPostfix: http://www.postfix.org/MILTER_README.html\n\n# Quick Installation\n\n 1. Build and install Sendmail, enabling libmilter (see libmilter/README).\n 2. Build and install Python, enabling threading.\n 3. Install this module: python setup.py --help\n 4. Add these two lines to sendmail.cf[a]:\n```\n O InputMailFilters=pythonfilter\n Xpythonfilter,        S=local:/home/username/pythonsock\n```\n 5. Run the sample.py example milter with: python sample.py\n Note that milters should almost certainly not run as root.\n\nThat's it.  Incoming mail will cause the milter to print some things, and\nsome email will be rejected (see the \"header\" method).  Edit and play.\nSee spfmilter.py for a functional SPF milter, or see bms.py for an complex\nmilter used in production.\n\n[a] This is for a quick test.  Your sendmail.cf in most distros will get\noverwritten whenever sendmail.mc is updated.  To make a milter permanent,\nadd something like:\n```\nINPUT_MAIL_FILTER(`pythonfilter', `S=local:/home/username/pythonsock, F=T, T=C:5m;S:20s;R:5m;E:5m')\n```\nto sendmail.mc instead.\n\n# Not-so-quick Installation\n\nFirst install Sendmail.  Make sure you read libmilter/README in the Sendmail\nsource directory, and make sure you enable libmilter before you build.  The\n8.11 series had libmilter marked as FFR (For Future Release); 8.12\nofficially supports libmilter, but it's still not built by default.\n\nInstall Python, and enable threading in Modules/Setup.\n\nInstall this miltermodule package; DistUtils Automatic Installation:\n\n$ python setup.py --help\n\nNow that everything is installed, we need to tell sendmail that we're going\nto filter incoming email.  Add lines similar to the following to\nsendmail.cf:\n```\nO InputMailFilters=pythonfilter\nXpythonfilter,        S=local:/home/username/pythonsock\n```\nThe \"O\" line tells sendmail which filters to use in what order; here we're\ntelling sendmail to use the filter named \"pythonfilter\".\n\nThe next line, the \"X\" line (for \"eXternal\"), lists that filter along with\nsome options associated with it.  In this case, we have the \"S\" option, which\nnames the socket that sendmail will use to communicate with this particular\nmilter.  This milter's socket is a unix-domain socket in the filesystem.\nSee libmilter/README for the definitive list of options.\n\nNB: The name is specified in two places: here, in sendmail's cf file, and\nin the milter itself.  Make sure the two match.\n\nNB: The above lines can be added in your .mc file with this line:\n```\nINPUT_MAIL_FILTER(`pythonfilter', `S=local:/home/username/pythonsock')\n```\nFor versions of sendmail prior to 8.12, you will need to enable\n`_FFR_MILTER` for the cf macros.  For example,\n```\nm4 -D_FFR_MILTER ../m4/cf.m4 myconfig.mc > myconfig.cf\n```\n# IPv6 Notes\n\nThe IPv6 protocol is supported if your operation system supports it\nand if sendmail was compiled with IPv6 support.  To determine if your\nsendmail supports IPv6, run \"sendmail -d0\" and check for the NETINET6\ncompilation option.  To compile sendmail with IPv6 support, add this\ndeclaration to your site.config.m4 before building it:\n```\nAPPENDDEF(`confENVDEF', `-DNETINET6=1')\n```\nIPv6 support can show up in two places; the communications socket\nbetween the milter and sendmail processes and in the host address\nargument to the connect() callback method.\n\nFor sendmail to be able to accept IPv6 SMTP sessions, you must\nconfigure the daemon to listen on an IPv6 port.  Furthermore if you\nwant to allow both IPv4 and IPv6 connections, some operating systems\nwill require that each listens to different port numbers.  For an\nIPv6-only setup, your sendmail configuration should contain a line\nsimilar to (first line is for sendmail.mc, second is sendmail.cf):\n```\nDAEMON_OPTIONS(`Name=MTA-v6, Family=inet6, Modify=C, Port=25')\nO DaemonPortOptions=Name=MTA-v6, Family=inet6, Modify=C, Port=25\n```\nTo allow sendmail and the milter process to communicate with each\nother over IPv6, you may use the \"inet6\" socket name prefix, as in:\n```\nXpythonfilter,        S=inet6:1234@fec0:0:0:7::5c\n```\nThe connect() callback method in the milter class will pass the\nIPv6-specific information in the 'hostaddr' argument as a tuple.  Note\nthat the type of this value is dependent upon the protocol family, and\nis not compatible with IPv4 connections.  Therefore you should always\ncheck the family argument before attempting to use the hostaddr\nargument.  A quick example showing this follows:\n```\n  import socket\n\n  class ipv6awareMilter(Milter.Milter):\n\n     def connect(self,hostname,family,hostaddr):\n\tif family==socket.AF_INET:\n\t   ipaddress, port = hostaddr\n\telif family==socket.AF_INET6:\n\t   ip6address, port, flowinfo, scopeid = hostaddr\n\telif family==socket.AF_UNIX:\n\t   socketpath = hostaddr\n```\nThe hostname argument is always safe to use without interpreting the\nprotocol family.  For IPv6 connections for which the hostname can not\nbe determined the hostname will appear similar to the string\n\"[IPv6:::1]\" with the corresponding hostaddr[0] being \"::1\".  Refer to\nRFC 2553 for information on interpreting and using the flowinfo and\nscopeid socket attributes, both of which are integers.\n\n# Authors\n\nJim Niemira (urmane@urmane.org) wrote the original C module and some quick\nand dirty python to use it.  Stuart D. Gathman (stuart@gathman.org) took that\nkludge and added threading and context objects to it, wrote a proper OO\nwrapper (Milter.py) that handles attachments, did lots of testing, packaged\nit with distutils, and generally transformed it from a quick hack to a\nreal, usable Python extension."
}

{
  "url": "https://github.com/sdgathman/pymilter",
  "finalUrl": "https://github.com/sdgathman/pymilter",
  "contentType": "text/html; charset=utf-8",
  "title": "GitHub - sdgathman/pymilter: Python bindings for libmilter api · GitHub",
  "retrievalMethod": "request",
  "truncated": false,
  "content": "Abstract\nThis is a python extension module to enable python scripts to attach to\nSendmail's libmilter API, enabling filtering of messages as they arrive.\nSince it's a script, you can do anything you want to the message - screen\nout viruses, collect statistics, add or modify headers, etc.  You can, at\nany point, tell Sendmail to reject, discard, or accept the message.\nAdditional python modules provide for navigating and modifying MIME parts, and\nsending DSNs or doing CBVs.\nRequirements\nPython milter extension: https://pypi.org/project/pymilter/\nPython: http://www.python.org\nSendmail: http://www.sendmail.org\nor\nPostfix: http://www.postfix.org/MILTER_README.html\nQuick Installation\n\nBuild and install Sendmail, enabling libmilter (see libmilter/README).\nBuild and install Python, enabling threading.\nInstall this module: python setup.py --help\nAdd these two lines to sendmail.cf[a]:\n\n O InputMailFilters=pythonfilter\n Xpythonfilter,        S=local:/home/username/pythonsock\n\nRun the sample.py example milter with: python sample.py\nNote that milters should almost certainly not run as root.\n\nThat's it.  Incoming mail will cause the milter to print some things, and\nsome email will be rejected (see the \"header\" method).  Edit and play.\nSee spfmilter.py for a functional SPF milter, or see bms.py for an complex\nmilter used in production.\n[a] This is for a quick test.  Your sendmail.cf in most distros will get\noverwritten whenever sendmail.mc is updated.  To make a milter permanent,\nadd something like:\nINPUT_MAIL_FILTER(`pythonfilter', `S=local:/home/username/pythonsock, F=T, T=C:5m;S:20s;R:5m;E:5m')\n\nto sendmail.mc instead.\nNot-so-quick Installation\nFirst install Sendmail.  Make sure you read libmilter/README in the Sendmail\nsource directory, and make sure you enable libmilter before you build.  The\n8.11 series had libmilter marked as FFR (For Future Release); 8.12\nofficially supports libmilter, but it's still not built by default.\nInstall Python, and enable threading in Modules/Setup.\nInstall this miltermodule package; DistUtils Automatic Installation:\n$ python setup.py --help\nNow that everything is installed, we need to tell sendmail that we're going\nto filter incoming email.  Add lines similar to the following to\nsendmail.cf:\nO InputMailFilters=pythonfilter\nXpythonfilter,        S=local:/home/username/pythonsock\n\nThe \"O\" line tells sendmail which filters to use in what order; here we're\ntelling sendmail to use the filter named \"pythonfilter\".\nThe next line, the \"X\" line (for \"eXternal\"), lists that filter along with\nsome options associated with it.  In this case, we have the \"S\" option, which\nnames the socket that sendmail will use to communicate with this particular\nmilter.  This milter's socket is a unix-domain socket in the filesystem.\nSee libmilter/README for the definitive list of options.\nNB: The name is specified in two places: here, in sendmail's cf file, and\nin the milter itself.  Make sure the two match.\nNB: The above lines can be added in your .mc file with this line:\nINPUT_MAIL_FILTER(`pythonfilter', `S=local:/home/username/pythonsock')\n\nFor versions of sendmail prior to 8.12, you will need to enable\n_FFR_MILTER for the cf macros.  For example,\nm4 -D_FFR_MILTER ../m4/cf.m4 myconfig.mc > myconfig.cf\n\nIPv6 Notes\nThe IPv6 protocol is supported if your operation system supports it\nand if sendmail was compiled with IPv6 support.  To determine if your\nsendmail supports IPv6, run \"sendmail -d0\" and check for the NETINET6\ncompilation option.  To compile sendmail with IPv6 support, add this\ndeclaration to your site.config.m4 before building it:\nAPPENDDEF(`confENVDEF', `-DNETINET6=1')\n\nIPv6 support can show up in two places; the communications socket\nbetween the milter and sendmail processes and in the host address\nargument to the connect() callback method.\nFor sendmail to be able to accept IPv6 SMTP sessions, you must\nconfigure the daemon to listen on an IPv6 port.  Furthermore if you\nwant to allow both IPv4 and IPv6 connections, some operating systems\nwill require that each listens to different port numbers.  For an\nIPv6-only setup, your sendmail configuration should contain a line\nsimilar to (first line is for sendmail.mc, second is sendmail.cf):\nDAEMON_OPTIONS(`Name=MTA-v6, Family=inet6, Modify=C, Port=25')\nO DaemonPortOptions=Name=MTA-v6, Family=inet6, Modify=C, Port=25\n\nTo allow sendmail and the milter process to communicate with each\nother over IPv6, you may use the \"inet6\" socket name prefix, as in:\nXpythonfilter,        S=inet6:1234@fec0:0:0:7::5c\n\nThe connect() callback method in the milter class will pass the\nIPv6-specific information in the 'hostaddr' argument as a tuple.  Note\nthat the type of this value is dependent upon the protocol family, and\nis not compatible with IPv4 connections.  Therefore you should always\ncheck the family argument before attempting to use the hostaddr\nargument.  A quick example showing this follows:\n  import socket\n\n  class ipv6awareMilter(Milter.Milter):\n\n     def connect(self,hostname,family,hostaddr):\n\tif family==socket.AF_INET:\n\t   ipaddress, port = hostaddr\n\telif family==socket.AF_INET6:\n\t   ip6address, port, flowinfo, scopeid = hostaddr\n\telif family==socket.AF_UNIX:\n\t   socketpath = hostaddr\n\nThe hostname argument is always safe to use without interpreting the\nprotocol family.  For IPv6 connections for which the hostname can not\nbe determined the hostname will appear similar to the string\n\"[IPv6:::1]\" with the corresponding hostaddr[0] being \"::1\".  Refer to\nRFC 2553 for information on interpreting and using the flowinfo and\nscopeid socket attributes, both of which are integers.\nAuthors\nJim Niemira (urmane@urmane.org) wrote the original C module and some quick\nand dirty python to use it.  Stuart D. Gathman (stuart@gathman.org) took that\nkludge and added threading and context objects to it, wrote a proper OO\nwrapper (Milter.py) that handles attachments, did lots of testing, packaged\nit with distutils, and generally transformed it from a quick hack to a\nreal, usable Python extension."
}

The logging callbacks are marked @noreply.\nMember Function Documentation\n\n          def Milter.Milter.abort\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nCalled when the connection is abnormally terminated.\nThe close callback is still called also.\n\nReimplemented from Milter.Base.\n\n          def Milter.Milter.close\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nCalled when the connection is closed.\n\nReimplemented from Milter.Base.\n\n          def Milter.Milter.connect\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           hostname,\n\n           family,\n\n           hostaddr\n\n          )\n\nCalled for each connection to the MTA.\nCalled by the xxfi_connect callback. The hostname provided by the local MTA is either the PTR name or the IP in the form \"[1.2.3.4]\" if no PTR is available. The format of hostaddr depends on the socket family:\n\nsocket.AF_INET\nA tuple of (IP as string in dotted quad form, integer port)\nsocket.AF_INET6\nA tuple of (IP as a string in standard representation, integer port, integer flow info, integer scope id)\nsocket.AF_UNIX\nA string with the socketname\n\nTo vary behavior based on what port the client connected to, for example skipping blacklist checks for port 587 (which must be authenticated), use getsymval('{daemon_port}') . The {daemon_port} macro must be enabled in sendmail.cf\n O Milter.macros.connect=j, _, {daemon_name}, {daemon_port}, {if_name}, {if_addr}\n  or sendmail.mc\n define(`confMILTER_MACROS_CONNECT', ``j, _, {daemon_name}, {daemon_port}, {if_name}, {if_addr}'')dnl\n  Parameters:\n\n    hostname the PTR name or bracketed IP of the SMTP client\n    family socket.AF_INET, socket.AF_INET6, or socket.AF_UNIX\n    hostaddr a tuple or string with peer IP or socketname\n\nReimplemented from Milter.Base.\n\n          def Milter.Milter.envfrom\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           f,\n\n           str\n\n          )\n\nCalled to begin each message.\nf -> string\t\tmessage sender\nstr -> tuple\tadditional ESMTP parameters\n\nReimplemented from Milter.Base.\n\n          def Milter.Milter.envrcpt\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           to,\n\n           str\n\n          )\n\nCalled when the SMTP client says RCPT TO.\nCalled by the xxfi_envrcpt callback. Returning REJECT rejects the current recipient, not the entire message. The recipient is the \"envelope\" recipient as defined by RFC 5321. For recipients defined in RFC 5322, for example To: or Cc:, see the header callback .\n\nReimplemented from Milter.Base.\n\n          def Milter.Milter.eoh\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nCalled at the blank line that terminates the header fields.\n\nReimplemented from Milter.Base.\n\n          def Milter.Milter.eom\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nCalled at the end of the message body.\nMost of the message manipulation actions can only take place from the eom callback.\n\nReimplemented from Milter.Base.\n\n          def Milter.Milter.header\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           field,\n\n           value\n\n          )\n\nCalled for each header field in the message body.\n\nReimplemented from Milter.Base.\n\n          def Milter.Milter.hello\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           hostname\n\n          )\n\nCalled when the SMTP client says HELO.\nReturning REJECT prevents progress until a valid HELO is provided; this almost always results in terminating the connection.\n\nReimplemented from Milter.Base.\n\nThe documentation for this class was generated from the following file:\nMilter/__init__.py\n\nGenerated on 11 Jul 2015 for pymilter by\n\n 1.6.1"
