Member Functions\ndef log\n Defined by subclasses to write log messages.\ndef connect\n Called for each connection to the MTA.\ndef hello\n Called when the SMTP client says HELO.\ndef envfrom\n Called when the SMTP client says MAIL FROM.\ndef envrcpt\n Called when the SMTP client says RCPT TO.\ndef data\n Called when the SMTP client says DATA.\ndef header\n Called for each header field in the message body.\ndef eoh\n Called at the blank line that terminates the header fields.\ndef body\n Called to supply the body of the message to the Milter by chunks.\ndef unknown\n Called when the SMTP client issues an unknown command.\ndef eom\n Called at the end of the message body.\ndef abort\n Called when the connection is abnormally terminated.\ndef close\n Called when the connection is closed.\ndef protocol_mask\n Return mask of SMFIP_N* protocol option bits to clear for this class The @nocallback and @noreply decorators set the milter_protocol function attribute to the protocol mask bit to pass to libmilter, causing that callback or its reply to be skipped.\ndef negotiate\n Negotiate milter protocol options.\ndef getsymval\n Return the value of an MTA macro.\ndef setreply\n Set the SMTP reply code and message.\ndef setsymlist\n Tell the MTA which macro names will be used.\ndef addheader\n Add a mail header field.\ndef chgheader\n Change the value of a mail header field.\ndef addrcpt\n Add a recipient to the message.\ndef delrcpt\n Delete a recipient from the message.\ndef replacebody\n Replace the message body.\ndef chgfrom\n Change the SMTP envelope sender address.\ndef quarantine\n Quarantine the message.\ndef progress\n Tell the MTA to wait a bit longer.\nPrivate Member Functions\ndef _setctx\n Attach this Milter to the low level milter.milterContext object.\nPrivate Attributes\n _ctx\n The low level milter.milterContext object.\n _actions\n A bitmask of actions this connection has negotiated to use.\n _protocol\n A bitmask of protocol options this connection has negotiated.\n\nDetailed Description\nA do \"nothing\" Milter base class representing an SMTP connection.\nPython milters should derive from this class unless they are using the low level milter module directly.\nMost of the methods are either \"actions\" or \"callbacks\". Callbacks are invoked by the MTA at certain points in the SMTP protocol. For instance when the HELO command is seen, the MTA calls the helo callback before returning a response code. All callbacks must return one of these constants: CONTINUE, TEMPFAIL, REJECT, ACCEPT, DISCARD, SKIP. The NOREPLY response is supplied automatically by the @noreply decorator if negotiation with the MTA is successful. @noreply and @nocallback methods should return CONTINUE for two reasons: the MTA may not support negotiation, and the class may be running in a test harness.\nOptional callbacks are disabled with the @nocallback decorator, and automatically reenabled when overridden. Disabled callbacks should still return CONTINUE for testing and MTAs that do not support negotiation.\nMember Function Documentation\n\n          def Milter.Base._setctx\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           ctx\n\n          )\n           [private]\n\nAttach this Milter to the low level milter.milterContext object.\n\n          def Milter.Base.abort\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nCalled when the connection is abnormally terminated.\nThe close callback is still called also.\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.addheader\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           field,\n\n           value,\n\n           idx = -1\n\n          )\n\nAdd a mail header field.\nCalls smfi_addheader. The Milter.ADDHDRS action flag must be set.\nMay be called from eom callback only.\nParameters:\n\n    field the header field name\n    value the header field value\n    idx header field index from the top of the message to insert at\n\nExceptions:\n\n    DisabledAction if ADDHDRS is not enabled\n\n          def Milter.Base.addrcpt\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           rcpt,\n\n           params = None\n\n          )\n\nAdd a recipient to the message.\nCalls smfi_addrcpt. If no corresponding mail header is added, this is like a Bcc. The syntax of the recipient is the same as used in the SMTP RCPT TO command (and as delivered to the envrcpt callback), for example \"self.addrcpt('<foo@example.com>')\". The Milter.ADDRCPT action flag must be set. If the optional params argument is used, then the Milter.ADDRCPT_PAR action flag must be set.\nMay be called from eom callback only.\nParameters:\n\n    rcpt the message recipient\n    params an optional list of ESMTP parameters\n\nExceptions:\n\n    DisabledAction if ADDRCPT or ADDRCPT_PAR is not enabled\n\n          def Milter.Base.body\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           blk\n\n          )\n\nCalled to supply the body of the message to the Milter by chunks.\nParameters:\n\n    blk a block of message bytes\n\n          def Milter.Base.chgfrom\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           sender,\n\n           params = None\n\n          )\n\nChange the SMTP envelope sender address.\nCalls smfi_chgfrom. The syntax of the sender is that same as used in the SMTP MAIL FROM command (and as delivered to the envfrom callback), for example self.chgfrom('<bar@example.com>'). The Milter.CHGFROM action flag must be set.\nMay be called from eom callback only.\nSince:0.9.1\nParameters:\n\n    sender the new sender address\n    params an optional list of ESMTP parameters\n\nExceptions:\n\n    DisabledAction if CHGFROM is not enabled\n\n          def Milter.Base.chgheader\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           field,\n\n           idx,\n\n           value\n\n          )\n\nChange the value of a mail header field.\nCalls smfi_chgheader. The Milter.CHGHDRS action flag must be set.\nMay be called from eom callback only.\nParameters:\n\n    field the name of the field to change\n    idx index of the field to change when there are multiple instances\n    value the new value of the field\n\nExceptions:\n\n    DisabledAction if CHGHDRS is not enabled\n\n          def Milter.Base.close\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nCalled when the connection is closed.\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.connect\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           hostname,\n\n           family,\n\n           hostaddr\n\n          )\n\nCalled for each connection to the MTA.\nCalled by the xxfi_connect callback. The hostname provided by the local MTA is either the PTR name or the IP in the form \"[1.2.3.4]\" if no PTR is available. The format of hostaddr depends on the socket family:\n\nsocket.AF_INET\nA tuple of (IP as string in dotted quad form, integer port)\nsocket.AF_INET6\nA tuple of (IP as a string in standard representation, integer port, integer flow info, integer scope id)\nsocket.AF_UNIX\nA string with the socketname\n\nTo vary behavior based on what port the client connected to, for example skipping blacklist checks for port 587 (which must be authenticated), use getsymval('{daemon_port}') . The {daemon_port} macro must be enabled in sendmail.cf\n O Milter.macros.connect=j, _, {daemon_name}, {daemon_port}, {if_name}, {if_addr}\n  or sendmail.mc\n define(`confMILTER_MACROS_CONNECT', ``j, _, {daemon_name}, {daemon_port}, {if_name}, {if_addr}'')dnl\n  Parameters:\n\n    hostname the PTR name or bracketed IP of the SMTP client\n    family socket.AF_INET, socket.AF_INET6, or socket.AF_UNIX\n    hostaddr a tuple or string with peer IP or socketname\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.data\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nCalled when the SMTP client says DATA.\nReturning REJECT rejects the message without wasting bandwidth on the unwanted message.\nSince:0.9.2\n\n          def Milter.Base.delrcpt\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           rcpt\n\n          )\n\nDelete a recipient from the message.\nCalls smfi_delrcpt. The recipient should match one passed to the envrcpt callback. The Milter.DELRCPT action flag must be set.\nMay be called from eom callback only.\nParameters:\n\n    rcpt the message recipient to delete\n\nExceptions:\n\n    DisabledAction if DELRCPT is not enabled\n\n          def Milter.Base.envfrom\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           f,\n\n           str\n\n          )\n\nCalled when the SMTP client says MAIL FROM.\nCalled by the xxfi_envfrom callback. Returning REJECT rejects the message, but not the connection. The sender is the \"envelope\" from as defined by RFC 5321. For the From: header (author) defined in RFC 5322, see the header callback .\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.envrcpt\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           to,\n\n           str\n\n          )\n\nCalled when the SMTP client says RCPT TO.\nCalled by the xxfi_envrcpt callback. Returning REJECT rejects the current recipient, not the entire message. The recipient is the \"envelope\" recipient as defined by RFC 5321. For recipients defined in RFC 5322, for example To: or Cc:, see the header callback .\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.eoh\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nCalled at the blank line that terminates the header fields.\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.eom\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nCalled at the end of the message body.\nMost of the message manipulation actions can only take place from the eom callback.\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.getsymval\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           sym\n\n          )\n\nReturn the value of an MTA macro.\nSendmail macro names are either single chars (e.g. \"j\") or multiple chars enclosed in braces (e.g. \"{auth_type}\"). Macro names are MTA dependent. See smfi_getsymval for default sendmail macros.\nParameters:\n\n    sym the macro name\n\n          def Milter.Base.header\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           field,\n\n           value\n\n          )\n\nCalled for each header field in the message body.\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.hello\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           hostname\n\n          )\n\nCalled when the SMTP client says HELO.\nReturning REJECT prevents progress until a valid HELO is provided; this almost always results in terminating the connection.\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.log\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           msg\n\n          )\n\nDefined by subclasses to write log messages.\n\nReimplemented in Milter.Milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.negotiate\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           opts\n\n          )\n\nNegotiate milter protocol options.\nCalled by the xffi_negotiate callback. This is an advanced callback, do not override unless you know what you are doing. Most negotiation can be done simply by using the supplied class and function decorators. Options are passed as a list of 4 32-bit ints which can be modified and are passed back to libmilter on return. Default negotiation sets P_NO* and P_NR* for callbacks marked @nocallback and @noreply respectively, leaves all actions enabled, and enables Milter.SKIP. The @enable_protocols class decorator can customize which protocol steps are implemented.\nParameters:\n\n    opts a modifiable list of 4 ints with negotiated options\n\nSince:0.9.2\n\n          def Milter.Base.progress\n          (\n\n           self\n           )\n\nTell the MTA to wait a bit longer.\nCalls smfi_progress. Resets timeouts in the MTA that detect a \"hung\" milter.\n\n          def Milter.Base.protocol_mask\n          (\n\n           klass\n           )\n\nReturn mask of SMFIP_N* protocol option bits to clear for this class The @nocallback and @noreply decorators set the milter_protocol function attribute to the protocol mask bit to pass to libmilter, causing that callback or its reply to be skipped.\nOverriding a method creates a new function object, so that milter_protocol defaults to 0. Libmilter passes the protocol bits that the current MTA knows how to skip. We clear the ones we don't want to skip. The negation is somewhat mind bending, but it is simple.\nSince:0.9.2\n\n          def Milter.Base.quarantine\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           reason\n\n          )\n\nQuarantine the message.\nCalls smfi_quarantine. When quarantined, a message goes into the mailq as if to be delivered, but delivery is deferred until the message is unquarantined. The Milter.QUARANTINE action flag must be set.\nMay be called from eom callback only.\nParameters:\n\n    reason a string describing the reason for quarantine\n\nExceptions:\n\n    DisabledAction if QUARANTINE is not enabled\n\n          def Milter.Base.replacebody\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           body\n\n          )\n\nReplace the message body.\nCalls smfi_replacebody. The entire message body must be replaced. Call repeatedly with blocks of data until the entire body is transferred. The Milter.MODBODY action flag must be set.\nMay be called from eom callback only.\nParameters:\n\n    body a chunk of body data\n\nExceptions:\n\n    DisabledAction if MODBODY is not enabled\n\n          def Milter.Base.setreply\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           rcode,\n\n           xcode = None,\n\n           msg = None,\n\n           ml\n\n          )\n\nSet the SMTP reply code and message.\nIf the MTA does not support setmlreply, then only the first msg line is used. Any '%' in a message line must be doubled, or libmilter will silently ignore the setreply. Beginning with 0.9.6, we test for that case and throw ValueError to avoid head scratching. What will really irritate you, however, is that if you carefully double any '%', your message will be sent - but with the '%' still doubled! See smfi_setreply for more information.\nParameters:\n\n    rcode The three-digit (RFC 821/2821) SMTP reply code as a string. rcode cannot be None, and must be a valid 4XX or 5XX reply code.\n    xcode The extended (RFC 1893/2034) reply code. If xcode is None, no extended code is used. Otherwise, xcode must conform to RFC 1893/2034.\n    msg The text part of the SMTP reply. If msg is None, an empty message is used.\n    ml Optional additional message lines.\n\n          def Milter.Base.setsymlist\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           stage,\n\n           macros\n\n          )\n\nTell the MTA which macro names will be used.\nThis information can reduce the size of messages received from sendmail, and hence could reduce bandwidth between sendmail and your milter where that is a factor. The Milter.SETSYMLIST action flag must be set. The protocol stages are M_CONNECT, M_HELO, M_ENVFROM, M_ENVRCPT, M_DATA, M_EOM, M_EOH.\nMay only be called from negotiate callback.\nSince:0.9.8, previous version was misspelled!\nParameters:\n\n    stage the protocol stage to set to macro list for, one of the M_* constants defined in Milter\n    macros space separated and/or lists of strings\n\n          def Milter.Base.unknown\n          (\n\n           self,\n\n           cmd\n\n          )\n\nCalled when the SMTP client issues an unknown command.\nParameters:\n\n    cmd the unknown command\n\nSince:0.9.2\n\nMember Data Documentation\n\n          Milter.Base._actions [private]\n\nA bitmask of actions this connection has negotiated to use.\nBy default, all actions are enabled. High throughput milters may want to disable unused actions to increase efficiency. Some optional actions may be disabled by calling milter.set_flags(), or by overriding the negotiate callback. The bits include: ADDHDRS,CHGBODY,MODBODY,ADDRCPT,ADDRCPT_PAR,DELRCPT CHGHDRS,QUARANTINE,CHGFROM,SETSYMLIST. The Milter.CURR_ACTS bitmask is all actions known when the milter module was compiled. Application code can also inspect this field to determine which actions are available. This is especially useful in generic library code designed to work in multiple milters.\nSince:0.9.2\n\n          Milter.Base._ctx [private]\n\nThe low level milter.milterContext object.\n\n          Milter.Base._protocol [private]\n\nA bitmask of protocol options this connection has negotiated.\nAn application may inspect this variable to determine which protocol steps are supported. Options of interest to applications: the SKIP result code is allowed only if the P_SKIP bit is set, rejected recipients are passed to the milter application only if the P_RCPT_REJ bit is set, and header values are sent and received with leading spaces (in the continuation lines) intact if the P_HDR_LEADSPC bit is set (so that the application can customize indenting).\nThe P_N* bits should be negotiated via the @noreply and @nocallback method decorators, and P_RCPT_REJ, P_HDR_LEADSPC should be enabled using the enable_protocols class decorator.\nThe bits include:  P_RCPT_REJ P_NR_CONN P_NR_HELO P_NR_MAIL P_NR_RCPT P_NR_DATA P_NR_UNKN P_NR_EOH P_NR_BODY P_NR_HDR P_NOCONNECT P_NOHELO P_NOMAIL P_NORCPT P_NODATA P_NOUNKNOWN P_NOEOH P_NOBODY P_NOHDRS P_HDR_LEADSPC P_SKIP  (all under the Milter namespace).\nSince:0.9.2\n\nThe documentation for this class was generated from the following file:\nMilter/__init__.py\n\nGenerated on 11 Jul 2015 for pymilter by\n\n 1.6.1"

pymilter is not another hook point — it's the /client side of a different protocol/. That distinction is the whole answer.

/What it actually is/

Postfix implements the Sendmail 8 milter protocol as a /client/: smtpd (or cleanup, for non-SMTP mail) opens a socket to your program and speaks a binary protocol with it. pymilter is Stuart Gathman's Python binding to =libmilter=, Sendmail's implementation of the /server/ side of that protocol. So what you get from it is: the wire framing, the connection state machine, the callback dispatch, the socket/listening/threading daemon, and the action verbs (=addheader=, =chgheader=, =replacebody=, =addrcpt=, =quarantine=, =setreply=). What you don't get is a hook in Postfix — you get a small daemon that Postfix dials up and asks questions during the SMTP conversation.

/The state machine is the concept/

/connect → helo → envfrom → envrcpt ... → data → header ... → eoh → body ... → eom/, one method call per event, on an object that represents one SMTP transaction. Compare that to the pipe transport, which handed you a finished blob plus argv. Here the message is assembled /in your process/, incrementally, while the sending MTA is still standing there waiting. Everything else follows from two rules:

- Most mutating actions are legal /only/ at =eom=, because that's the first moment the whole message exists.
- Your return value at each event is a /protocol/ answer — =CONTINUE=, =TEMPFAIL=, =REJECT=, =DISCARD= — not a process exit status.

That second point is the fix for the failure-semantics mismatch I flagged earlier. The "gpg failed" → "this is a permanent bounce" problem disappears, because you can say =TEMPFAIL= and mean it, and the sender retries in the SMTP session rather than Postfix inventing a bounce.

/Where it sits, and what that buys you/

Before-queue. This is the answer to the architectural hole in the earlier design: the plaintext never reaches the spool, so there's no window where cleartext exists on disk, and no re-injection step at all — no =sendmail -G -i=, no second smtpd on 10026, no loop-breaking =-o content_filter==, no temp files. Body replacement is the mechanism: Postfix ≥ 2.6 supports all Sendmail 8.14 milter requests including =SMFIR_REPLBODY= (2.3 explicitly did not), and pymilter exposes it as =self.replacebody(chunk)=, gated on negotiating the =MODBODY= action. So the shape is:

#+begin_src python
class GpgMilter(Milter.Base):
    def envrcpt(self, to, params):      # collect the envelope
        self.rcpts.append(to)
        return Milter.CONTINUE
    def eom(self):                      # only place you may mutate
        if self.already_encrypted:       # seen in header() callback
            return Milter.ACCEPT
        body = self.build_pgp_mime(...)  # this is still yours to write
        self.chgheader("Content-Type", 1, "multipart/encrypted; ...")
        self.replacebody(body)
        return Milter.CONTINUE
#+end_src

/What you now own instead/

You've traded a short-lived process for a daemon, and that's a real shift in failure modes:

- /Supervision./ It must be up. =milter_default_action= decides what happens when it isn't: =tempfail= (mail queues, nothing is lost, everything stops) or =accept= (mail flows /unencrypted/, silently defeating the entire setup). For your purpose =tempfail= is the only sane value — and that means your filter is now on the availability path of your inbound mail.
- /Concurrency./ pymilter runs each connection in its own thread, so your code must be thread-safe; the GIL is not a problem here since gpg work is subprocess I/O, but memory is (you're holding whole messages).
- /Before-queue's inherent costs./ The sender is held open during encryption; =milter_content_timeout= defaults to 300s, and if you blow through it the sending MTA may retransmit — duplicate mail. Postfix's own docs call this "an inherent problem with before-queue filtering."
- /One body, N recipients./ A milter transaction has one message and a recipient list; no request makes the body differ per recipient. With your single fixed key this is a non-issue — which is precisely why your use case is the one where the milter approach is clean.

/Does it change the "gpg alone isn't enough" verdict?/

No, and it sharpens why. pymilter hands you headers one at a time and the body in chunks — it does /not/ hand you a MIME tree. =chgheader= + =replacebody= are the right /mechanism/ for emitting =multipart/encrypted=, but the entity construction (armor, =Version:=, the =application/pgp-encrypted= control part, boundary discipline, protected headers) is still yours. pymilter ships some MIME navigation helpers, but nothing that turns =gpg -e= into RFC 3156 on your behalf. So: gpg remains the correct transform, and the MIME framing remains the actual work — the milter just moves it from "post-queue shell squinting at bytes" to "in-process Python object, still doing MIME."

/Practical notes/

- Scope it to inbound: =smtpd_milters= on the port-25 service only, or =smtpd_milter_maps= by cidr to exclude 127.0.0.0/8 and your submission port. Local =sendmail= submissions are a /different/ list (=non_smtpd_milters=) — don't accidentally encrypt your own outbound.
- Postfix's socket syntax is =inet:host:port=; milter's own is =inet:port@host=. Getting that backwards is the classic first-hour bug. A unix socket under a chrooted smtpd resolves relative to the queue directory.
- Deps are real: =libmilter-dev= from sendmail's source tree, compiled C extension via pip. pymilter is maintained but small and low-velocity; if it's unavailable, mimedefang (Perl, milter + real MIME engine) is the battle-tested equivalent, and =postfix-gpg-milter= (Go) is this exact design already implemented.
- Guard against re-encryption by sniffing =Content-Type: multipart/encrypted= in the =header= callback, not by guessing.

So the three-way picture: /pipe/content_filter/ = after-queue, batch, envelope in argv, cleartext touches disk, failure is an exit code. /milter via pymilter/ = before-queue, in-memory, envelope is an object, cleartext never lands, failure is an SMTP code — at the price of owning a daemon. /notmuch/mbsync hooks/ = none of the above; they're downstream of storage and can't affect it.

{
  "url": "https://www.husk-project.org/",
  "finalUrl": "https://www.husk-project.org/",
  "contentType": "text/html",
  "title": "husk project",
  "retrievalMethod": "request",
  "truncated": false,
  "content": "Husk\n\n      Husk is a pass-through solution for automatic OpenPGP encryption.\nHusk is an email filter (milter) for MTAs which encrypts emails while they pass through it. It facilitates Web-of-Trust technologies to use decentralized and federated certificate authorities as sources for authenticated OpenPGP certificates. It aims to reduce the amount of administrative effort of obtaining and keeping them up to date by establishing narrow, focused trust delegations. Husk can be used to encrypt emails for services which cannot encrypt on their own – like notification systems or issue trackers, or being used at the end of transport to implement zero-access encryption (for email at rest).\nHusk is written in Rust and uses Sequoia PGP for encryption and certificate handling.\nFeatures\n\nOpportunistic email encryption using OpenPGPv4 or OpenPGPv6 (configurable).\nIntegration into the email processing flow via the sendmail-milter protocol.\nDownloading of certificates from online sources on demand.\nUses the Web-of-Trust by using introducers, allowing a fine grade of trust delegation.\nPeriodically update all local certificates by querying online sources (keyservers, etc).\nOptionally tunnel all requests to online sources through the Tor-network.\nStatistics collection.\nTest how Husk will treat email addresses by probing the running process.\n\nHusk allows for a smooth entry. Without introducers and locals (see the\ndocumentation for details) Husk will\nnot modify any passing email. This allows you to define introducers and\nlocals step by step and examine their influence on your email traffic.\nHusk is not an all-or-nothing solution.\n\n    News\n\n      2026-09-16 - Milestone 7 reached\n\n      2026-08-24 - Milestone 6 reached\n\n      2026-08-03 - Milestone 5 reached\n\n      2026-06-30 - Milestone 4 reached\n\n      2026-05-26 - Milestone 3 reached\n\n      2026-01-05 - Milestone 2 reached\n\n      2025-11-05 - Project start\n\n      This project is funded through the NGI0 Commons Fund, a fund established by NLnet.\n\n        Home\n        Theory of operation\n        Documentation\n        Repository\n        Contact"
}

{
  "url": "https://www.husk-project.org/documentation/",
  "finalUrl": "https://www.husk-project.org/documentation/",
  "contentType": "text/html",
  "title": "husk project",
  "retrievalMethod": "request",
  "truncated": false,
  "content": "Husk\n\n    Documentation\n    Installation\nHusk is written in Rust, so you need a recent rust toolchain to compile\nthe source. If your distribution doesn't provide such a toolchain (or\nit is too old), you can get an up-to-date version from\nhere.\nInstall the dependencies\nFor debian/trixie the following packages are necessary:\n# apt install clang nettle-dev pkg-config libssl-dev capnproto libsqlite3-dev\n\nInstall via 'cargo install'\nHusk is a published crate, so you can use cargo install to install it.\nPlease note, that the name of the crate is husk-milter.\n$ cargo install --locked husk-milter\n\nThis will install the binary husk into your home directory in\n~/.cargo/bin.\nInstall from repository\nIf you want to enjoy the newest version of Husk, clone its repository\nand compile:\n$ git clone https://gitlab.com/husk-project/husk-milter\n$ cd husk-milter\n$ cargo build --locked --release\n\nThis will create the binary husk in ./target/release/. Copy it to\na convenient place.\nThe compilation also generates a set of files containing code for\nshell completion. The environment variable ASSET_OUT_DIR can be\nused to specify the location where these file are generated.\n$ ASSET_OUT_DIR=/tmp cargo build --locked --release\n\nA subdirectory /tmp/shell_completions will be created, containing\nthe generated files.\nConfiguration\nThe configuration file of Husk uses the toml format.\nHusk uses OpenPGP certificates for encryption. It needs a storage location\nfor these certificates. As Husk uses Sequoia PGP,\nthe name for this parameter is sequoia_home.\nsequoia_home = \"/var/lib/husk/\"\n\nHusk will use the default cryptographic policy of Sequoia PGP. If you want\nto make modifications, you can specify a file by:\npolicy = \"/path/to/file\"\n\nThe default location is /etc/husk/crypto-policy. For details, see below.\nHusk consists of\n\na milter (mail filter) which does the encryption,\nan updater which periodically updates certificates with data from online\nsources and\nA monitor process which collects statistical data from the above processes.\n\nMilter, updater and monitor have their sections in the config file: [milter],\n[updater] and [monitor].\nMilter\nThe milter is a background process (daemon) that listens on a TCP port for\nincoming requests. This port is configured under the name connection.\n[milter]\nconnection = \"localhost:3000\"\n\nThis example tells Husk to listen on 127.0.0.1 (localhost), port 3000.\nThere is a corresponding configuration for the MTA, so that the MTA\nknows where to find Husk (see below).\nHusk queries online sources for missing certificates. For WKD and DANE,\nthe URL of the online source is derived from the email. Keyservers\nhave to be configured separately.\nThese keyservers are also used by the updater.\n[milter]\n...\nkeyservers = [ \"hkps://keys.openpgp.org/\", \"hkps://mail-api.proton.me\" ]\n\nThe milter creates a file which contains information to identify the milter\nprocess - a pid file.\nThe location of the pid file for the milter can be configured by:\n[milter]\n...\npid_file = \"/path/to/file\"\n\nIf no pid_file is configured, /var/lib/husk/husk.pid is used.\nThe milter will can add a signature if it encrypts an email. For that\na signing_key has to be provided. Its storage location is configured\nby:\n[milter]\n...\nsigning_key = \"/var/lib/husk/signing_key.asc\"\n\nHow the signing key can be set or created is described below. If no\nsigning key can be found, the milter will not add a signature. The\ndefault location is /var/lib/husk/signing_key.asc.\nWhen encrypting an email, the subject of the email is moved into the\nencrypted part and the subject is replaced. The replacement can be\nconfigured by:\n[milter]\n...\nsubject_replacement = \"some string\"\n\nDefault subject_replacement is ... (three dots).\nThe milter can search for missing certificates via the\nTor network. You will need a locally running\nTor process, as the milter will use it as a sock5 proxy.\n[milter]\n...\nuse_tor_via = \"socks5://localhost:9050/\"\n\nDefault is undefined, meaning the updates are not tunneled through Tor.\nWhen the milter encrypts a message, it chooses the output format from the\ncapabilities the used certificate advertises. A certificate which signals\nOpenPGPv6 capabilities will lead to an encrypted message in OpenPGPv6\nformat.\nThis will lead to undecryptable emails, when clients only capable of OpenPGPv4\nare used (at the time of this writing for instance Thunderbird and GnuPG).\nThe milter can be forced to ignore OpenPGPv6 capabilities and produce\nOpenPGPv4 format messages.\n[milter]\n...\nrfc4880_profile = true\n\nFor the time being the default is true.\nThis is a problematic feature as it sabotages the upgrade path to OpenPGPv6.\n\nHusk queries online sources for missing certificates. If a certificate\nisn't available, Husk will repeat the query when the next email for\nthe associated recipient needs processing.\nThis can lead to many queries and as updates to online sources are rare,\nthe outcome will likely not change. To prevent (possibly) useless queries,\nHusk keeps a history of failed attempts and will refrain from querying\nif the last failure is too recent.\nThe history can be configured by defining how long an entry in the history\nis considered 'too recent' and how many entries the history will store.\n[milter]\n...\nthrottle_max_age = 3600\n\nEntries in the history older then throttle_max_age are no longer\nconsidered 'too recent', in case the milter will repeat the query.\nthrottle_max_age is measured in seconds.\n[milter]\n...\nthrottle_max_size = 4096\n\nThis option limits the number of entries in the history. If the history\noverflows, failed queries might be repeated even if they are otherwise\n'recent'.\nThe milter process can be probed for email addresses, to check how a mail\nfor an certain email address would be treated (see below). To prevent\nunauthorized access, a secret has to be exchanged together with the email\naddress.\n[milter]\n...\nsecret = \"oochu9xieghaic2uu1iejooquos4uiP1aiBe8OhchiaYephu\"\n\nThe secret has to be one word (no whitespaces) and should be random. If\nno secret is configured, probing will be rejected.\nUpdater\nThe updater periodically updates all certificates of the local certificate\nstore from online sources.\nThe updates are spaced by a random time interval, specified by the minimum of\nseconds to wait and the maximum.\n[updater]\n...\nwait_min = 5\nwait_max = 3600\n\nLike the milter, the updater can tunnel its requests through the Tor network. The\nconfiguration is similar:\n[updater]\n...\nuse_tor_via = \"socks5://localhost:9050/\"\n\nDefault is undefined, meaning the updates are not tunneled through Tor.\nLike the milter, the updater also creates a pid file. Its location can be configured\nby:\n[updater]\n...\npid_file = \"/path/to/file\"\n\nIf no pid_file is configured, /var/lib/husk/updater.pid is used.\nThe updater maintains a state file so, that it can resume its work after\na restart of the process. The location can be configured by:\n[updater]\n...\nstate_file = \"/path/to/file\"\n\nThe default location is /var/lib/husk/updater.state.\nMonitor\nThe monitor is a stopover which collects and aggregates statistical data\nfrom the milter and the updater. This data can then be read out to\nfeed it into higher level monitoring solutions.\nFor communication, has two sockets - one for receiving data from the\nmilter and the updater, and a second for reading out the aggregated data.\n[monitor]\n...\ninput = \"/var/lib/husk/monitor.socket\"\noutput = \"/var/lib/husk/monitor-readout.socket\"\n\nThese are also the default values. Make sure that the monitor process\nhas read/write permissions on those sockets. If not existing, the monitor\nprocess will try to create these entries which requires the corresponding\npermissions for the parent directory.\nThe monitor creates a pid file:\n[monitor]\n...\npid_file = \"/var/lib/husk/monitor.pid\"\n\nThis is also the default.\nWhen the monitor is read out, there is an option to reset all counters\nto zero.\n[monitor]\n...\nreset_on_read = true\n\nThis can be set to true or false. Default is true.\nLogging\nHusk uses log4rs for logging. The configuration of log4rs is merged\ninto the main configuration file under the node named logging. That\nmeans that you take a log4rs configuration and prepend logging to\neach section:\n[logging.appenders.console]\nkind = \"console\"\n[logging.appenders.console.encoder]\npattern = \"{d(%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S)(local)} {h({l})} [{t}] {m}{n}\"\n\n[logging.appenders.file]\nkind = \"file\"\npath = \"/tmp/husk.log\"\n\n[logging.appenders.file.encoder]\npattern = \"{d(%Y-%m-%d %H:%M:%S)(local)} {h({l})} [{t}] {m}{n}\"\n\n[logging.root]\nlevel = \"debug\"\nappenders = [\"file\"]\n\n[logging.loggers.\"husk\"]\nlevel = \"debug\"\n\nso that [appenders.console] becomes [logging.appenders.console]\nand so on.\nIf you do not specify any logging, Husk will default to log to the console.\nLocation\nYou can put the configuration file at any place and start Husk with\n$ husk ... --config <LOCATION> ...\n\nor use the default location /etc/husk/config.toml. In this case you\ncan skip --config.\nConfigure postfix\nThis is a description on how to add Husk to the postfix MTA. If you want to\nuse Husk with a different MTA, please refer to its documentation on how to add\na milter.\nFor postfix, you have to tell the MTA where it can find the milter port.\nThe configuration entries are smtpd_milters and non_smtpd_milters.\nsmtpd_milters = inet:localhost:3000\nnon_smtpd_milters = $smtpd_milters\n\ninet:localhost:3000 corresponds to the connection configuration parameter\nin the Husk configuration.\nThe example above configures non_smtpd_milters by using the setting of\nsmtpd_milters (via the $ operator).\nPlease keep in mind that in case you configured multiple milters, the\nsequence is important, as Husk might change the content of the mail.\nConfigure a cryptographic policy\nHusk uses the default cryptographic policy from Sequoia PGP. This default\npolicy rejects algorithms which are deprecated (like SHA1 for instance).\nYou can modify this default policy by providing a file containing the\nmodifications you want to make. The format of this file is identical to\nthe cryptographic policy used by Sequoia PGP.\nAn example to allow the usage of SHA1:\n[hash_algorithms]\nsha1.second_preimage_resistance = \"always\"\nsha1.collision_resistance = \"always\"\n\nA full documentation of the cryptographic policy can be found\nhere.\nThe modifications are only used by Husk, other applications based on\nSequoia PGP are not effected.\nUsage\nHusk has two aspects:\n\nit can run as a background process\nit can be used as a cli-tool for administrating the background\nprocess.\n\nThe CLI syntax follows the scheme NOUN (more NOUNs), VERB and then\nparameters (if sensible). Like\n$ husk introducer add --cert-file FILE --domains DOMAIN\n\nor\n\n$ husk daemon start --config config.toml\n\nHusk includes a help system, which can be used by passing --help.\n$ husk --help\n\nor\n\n$ husk introducer add --help\n\nand so on. It shows you the available subcommands and parameters.\nThe milter daemon\nThe daemon is the background process which processes mails and encrypts\nthem if possible. It has to be started separately, the MTA expects it\nto be reachable and doesn't start it on it's own.\nStart the daemon\n$ husk daemon start\n\nor use\n$ husk daemon start --config /path/to/the/configuration/file.toml\n\nIf you want to start the daemon as a background process add --background\nto the command:\n$ husk daemon start --background\n\nHusk can (and should) run as an unprivileged process (keep in mind to\nuse a port number above 1024, as ports below require a privileged\nprocess).\nStop the daemon\nStopping means terminating the process. Please note that your MTA will start\nproducing error messages if a registered milter disappears.\n$ husk daemon stop\n\nStatus of the daemon\n$ husk daemon status\n\nIf the daemon is running, daemon status will also report the pid of the process.\nUsing systemd\nIn this example the binary is installed into /usr/local/bin/husk and\n/var/lib/husk is used for certificate and key storage. The user account\nhusk is used for running the process.\n[Unit]\nDescription=Husk encrypting milter\nAfter=network.target\n\n[Service]\nType=simple\nWorkingDirectory=/var/lib/husk\nExecStart=/usr/local/bin/husk daemon start\nUser=husk\nGroup=husk\nRestart=always\n\n[Install]\nWantedBy=multi-user.target\n\nCreate a file with the above content in /etc/systemd/system/husk.service and\nuse systemctl to enable and start the process.\nIntroducers\nFor Husk to use a certificate for encryption, it has to be authenticated -\nmeaning, the user id of the certificate (usually an email address) is\nindeed the user id of the key holder of said certificate.\nThis authentication can only be done by humans, but once it's done, its result\ncan be shared.\nThis is what an introducer does - it authenticates certificates and publishes\nthe result in form of a certification which becomes part of the certificate.\nHusk allows to use introducers to benefit from their work. At the same\ntime, introducers are limited to a list of domains for which certifications\nare accepted. This is a restriction Husk imposes. The list of domains is\nspecified by the administrator, not the introducer.\nAdd an introducer\nTo add an introducer you need its certificate in a file. When adding\nyou have to specify a list of domains this introducer is allowed to\nintroduce.\n$ husk introducer add --cert-file FILE --domains example.com\n\nYou can specify a list of domains by\n... ---domains domain1 domains2 domain3\n\nFILE must contain a valid certificate, not expired and not revoked.\nIn case you already added the certificate to Husk, you can promote it\nby using its fingerprint.\n$ husk introducer add --cert FINGERPRINT --domains example.com\n\nList introducers\n$ husk introducer list\n\nRemove an introducer\n$ husk introducer remove --cert FINGERPRINT\n\nIf you remove an introducer, the certificate will stay in the certificate\nstore - only it's special powers will be removed. You can reactivate an\nintroducer by using husk introducer add --cert FINGERPRINT ..., if you\naccidentally removed one.\nCreate an introducer\nIntroducers are maintained separately from Husk. The following is an example\non how to create and use one, using the command line tool sq from Sequoia PGP.\nFor the sake of this example we have two sides: Husk as consumer and CA\nas a producer. CA stands for certificate authority. CA is an ordinary\nOpenPGP key, what makes it a certificate authority is how it is used.\nIn most cases, CA is not you, but the one who's work you want to use - see above.\nCreate a key:\n$ sq key generate --own-key --userid 'My CA'\n\nYou can create a more complex key with more user ids and capabilities,\nbut for a CA this is enough.\nThis CA can now be used to certify other keys (or more precise: certificates).\nLet B0F698D7419862EAC6F435B0D5FC683922578868 be the fingerprint of a\ncertificate.\n$ sq pki vouch add --certifier-userid 'My CA' \\\n  --cert B0F698D7419862EAC6F435B0D5FC683922578868 \\\n  --all\n\nThis example certifies all user ids of B0F698D7419862EAC6F435B0D5FC683922578868.\nYou can be more selective by using --userid or --email instead of --all.\nNow publish the certified key:\n$ sq network keyserver publish --cert B0F698D7419862EAC6F435B0D5FC683922578868\n\nRepeat the last two steps for all certificates you want to certify.\nNow let Husk know and use your CA. First copy the certificate of CA\nto where Husk is running.\n$ sq cert export --cert-userid 'My CA' > ca.pgp\n$ scp ca.pgp $HUSK_SERVER\n\nOn the $HUSK_SERVER:\n$ husk introducer add --cert-file ca.pgp --domains ...\n\nChoose the list of domains you want to trust CA to introduce carefully.\nLet's assume the B0F698D7419862EAC6F435B0D5FC683922578868 is the certificate\nof alice@example.com and you trust CA for that domain.\n$ husk introducer add --cert-file ca.pgp --domains example.com\n\nIf now Husk encounters a mail addressed to alice@example.com (and her\ncertificate isn't yet locally available), Husk will query online sources\nand check the results for a certification of CA (as CA is registered\nas an introducer for *@example.com).\nIf such a certification can be found, the right certificate is identified\nand Husk will use it for encryption (and stores it locally).\nLocals\nLocals are certificates which are added to Husk by the administrator\nand do not require to be introduced.\nIf you want to test Husk or want to use it only with a few dedicated\ncertificates, you can skip creating an introducer and use locals.\nAdd Locals\n$ husk locals add --cert-file FILE\n\nFILE must contain a valid certificate, not expired and not revoked.\nLocals can be promoted to introducers by using\nhusk introducer add --cert FINGERPRINT ....\nList locals\n$ husk locals list\n\nRemove locals\n$ husk locals remove --cert FINGERPRINT\n\nIf you remove a local, its certificate will stay in the certificate store, but\nwill be dormant.\nSigning key\nHusk can sign/encrypt mails instead of only encrypting them. For that,\nHusk needs the appropriate key.\nSigning is done when a signing key is configured, without that key Husk\nwill only encrypt a message.\nSetting the signing key\nIf you already have a key, you can import it into the keystore of Husk\nby:\n$ husk signkey import --cert-file FILE\n\nFILE must contain an OpenPGP key (public and private key material) which\nis not expired or revoked and must be able to sign messages.\nThe new key will be used instantly, a restart of the daemon is not necessary.\nCreate a signing key\nYou can tell Husk to create a signing key by:\n$ hust signkey create\n\nThis will create a key which can only sign messages. With --userid you can\nspecify a custom user id:\n$ hust signkey create --userid \"My special signing key\"\n\nWithout --userid Husk will use Husk signing key as user id.\nDisplaying the fingerprint of the current signing key\n$ husk signkey fingerprint\n\nExporting the certificate of a signing key\nYou might want to make the signing key public, so that users receiving\na signed/encrypted message from Husk can identify the signer.\n$ husk signkey print\n\nwill print the certificate (public key) to the console, so that you can\nshare it with the world.\nThe updater\nStart the updater\n$ husk updater start\n\nor use\n$ husk updater start --config /path/to/the/configuration/file.toml\n\nIf you want to start the updater as a background process, add --background\nto the command:\n$ husk updater start --background\n\nThe Husk updater must run as the same user as the milter daemon.\nStop the updater\n$ husk updater stop\n\nStatus of the updater\n$ husk updater status\n\nIf the updater is running, updater status will also report the pid of the process.\nUsing systemd\nIn this example the binary is installed into /usr/local/bin/husk and\n/var/lib/husk is used for certificate and key storage. The user account\nhusk is used for running the process.\n[Unit]\nDescription=Husk certificate updater\nAfter=network.target\n\n[Service]\nType=simple\nWorkingDirectory=/var/lib/husk\nExecStart=/usr/local/bin/husk updater start\nUser=husk\nGroup=husk\nRestart=always\n\n[Install]\nWantedBy=multi-user.target\n\nCreate a file with the above content in /etc/systemd/system/husk-updater.service and\nuse systemctl to enable and start the process.\nThe monitor\nThe monitor functions as a stopover to aggregate statistical data before it\nis read out for further processing. If the monitor process isn't running,\nstatistical data is not collected.\nStart the monitor\n$ husk monitor start\n\nor use\n$ husk monitor start --config /path/to/the/configuration/file.toml\n\nIf you want to start the monitor as a background process, add --background\nto the command:\n$ husk monitor start --background\n\nThe Husk monitor must run as the same user as the milter daemon.\nStop the updater\n$ husk monitor stop\n\nStatus of the updater\n$ husk monitor status\n\nIf the monitor is running, monitor status will also report the pid of the process.\nUsing systemd\nIn this example the binary is installed into /usr/local/bin/husk and\n/var/lib/husk is used for certificate and key storage. The user account\nhusk is used for running the process.\n[Unit]\nDescription=Husk certificate monitor process\nAfter=network.target\n\n[Service]\nType=simple\nWorkingDirectory=/var/lib/husk\nExecStart=/usr/local/bin/husk monitor start\nUser=husk\nGroup=husk\nRestart=always\n\n[Install]\nWantedBy=multi-user.target\n\nCreate a file with the above content in /etc/systemd/system/husk-monitor.service and\nuse systemctl to enable and start the process.\nRead out the data\n$ husk monitor readout\nencryption_error:1\nencrypted_rcpt:12\nunencrypted_rcpt:7\nunencryptable_mail:5\ne2e_encrypted_mail:7\ninjected_mail:9\nencrypted_mail:12\nupdater_cert_updated:1\nupdater_cert_keep:1\n\nThe meaning of the returned fields:\n\nencryption_error: Number of attempts an encryption failed due to an\nunsupported certificate.\nencrypted_rcpt: Number of recipients with a valid certificate.\nunencrypted_rcpt: Number of recipients without a valid certificate.\ne2e_encrypted_mail: Number of incoming emails which are already encrypted.\ninjected_mail: Number of times a new email had to be send because the\nlist of recipients had to be split (for instance in encryptable and\nunencryptable recipients).\nencrypted_mail: Number of time an email got encrypted.\nupdater_cert_updated: Number of times the updater found an update\nfor a certificate online.\nupdater_cert_keep: Number of certificates the updater found no update.\n\nFields with a value of zero are skipped in the output.\nYou can get a json formatted output by:\n$ husk monitor readout --json\n\nThe json output will contain all fields (even if their value is zero).\nIf the monitor is configured to reset_on_read, the counters are set to\nzero after a readout.\nProbe the milter\nUsing the monitor subcommand, the running milter process can be queried\nfor email addresses.\n$ husk monitor probe --email alice@example.com\nalice@example.com has 1 local certificates\nintroduced by 7033BAF30F9E9D1338D0A5D35E3E9BC3663623E5 - <ca@example.com>, CA\n\nor\n$ husk monitor probe --email bob@example.com\nbob@example.com has 0 local certificates\ncertificate queries for bob@example.com are currently throttled\nintroduced by 7033BAF30F9E9D1338D0A5D35E3E9BC3663623E5 - <ca@example.com>, CA\n\nIf there are no lines starting with introduced by, then the domain of\nthe requested email has no introducer. If the domain has several introducers,\nall are listed.\nThe line certificate queries... indicates that\n\nthere are no (valid) certificates that can be found online.\nthe last attempt to get a certificate for that email address is less then\nthrottle_max_age ago (see above).\n\n        Home\n        Theory of operation\n        Documentation\n        Repository\n        Contact"
}

{
  "url": "https://www.husk-project.org/theory/",
  "finalUrl": "https://www.husk-project.org/theory/",
  "contentType": "text/html",
  "title": "husk project",
  "retrievalMethod": "request",
  "truncated": false,
  "content": "Husk\n\n    Theory of operation\n    Husk is a milter (\"mail filter\"). When registered with an MTA every passing\nemail is forwarded to the milter, which can modify its content before giving it\nback to the MTA for further processing.\nFor each email passing, Husk will check the recipients of that email and when it\nfinds a valid and authenticated certificate, it will encrypt that email.\nHusk uses OpenPGP and leverages its features. Husk avoids maintaining an own\nset of metadata of its certificates.\nAuthenticated certificates\nAn authenticated certificate is a certificate with User IDs which are valid,\nmeaning that the certificate actually belongs to the User ID and is not an\nimpersonation.\nTo persist the information about authenticity, a certificate gets certified by\nanother certificate, which means that the keyholder of that other certificate\nvouches for its authenticity. This certification becomes part of the vouched\ncertificate and is published with it.\nAs the keyholder of the certified certification can use that certificate to\ncertify other certificates a chain gets created. The certificates are the nodes,\nthe certifications are the edges between these nodes. These edges have attibutes\nwhich further classify the relationship of the nodes connected by this edge:\n\nA trust depth\nA trust amount\nA regular expression\n\nA certificate is considered authenticated if there is a path from a trusted\ncertificate to that certificate. The trusted certificate can be any certificate,\nbut usually it's a dedicated \"special\" certificate (the local trust root).\nThe traversal of the path starts with a certain thrust depth - usually the\nhighest possible value. With each edge the trust depth is decreased by one and\ncompared with the trust depth of the certification - the minimum of these two\nvalues will be used from there on. The traversal ends when a trust depth of zero\nor the certificate in question is reached.\nIf a path can be established, the minimum value of all trust amounts of the\nedges is taken, for a certificate to be considered, the resulting trust amount\nhas to be higher than a predefined threshold. If there are a couple of\nindependent paths, the one with the highest trust amount is used.\nEdges on the path can have a regular expression. If a certificate is encountered\nwhich has no User ID matching this regular expression, the traversal ends. That\nway a certification can be limited to a set of domains.\nCertifications usually carry a trust depth of zero, meaning that the\ncertificate on the other end of an edge is authenticated, but certificates\nfurther down the path are not.\nCertifications carrying a trust depth of one are creating trusted introducers,\nas the certifications made by the certificate of the other end of the edge will\nbe taken into consideration.\nHusk uses the concept of trusted introducers combined with regular expressions\nto delegate the authentication, thus reducing the maintenance effort needed to\nestablish a larger set of usable certificates. Idealy an administrator only\nhas to configure a set of trusted introducers.\nProcessing of an email\nFor each recipient of an email processed Husk checks if there an authenticated\nlocally available. If not, but a trusted introducer for the domain of the\nrecipient exists, Husk queries the internet (Keyservers, WKD, DANE) for\ncertificates of that recipient - if a certificate is returned which is\ncertified by the trusted introducer, it will be stored locally and used for this\nand future emails.\nIf for all recipients an authenticated certificate is available, the email\nwill be encrypted and passed back to the MTA. If no certificate is found, the\nemail will not be modified - Husk implements opportunistic encryption.\nIf only for a subset of recipients certificates can be found, Husk will produce\na second email for all recipients where encryption is not available.\n\n        Home\n        Theory of operation\n        Documentation\n        Repository\n        Contact"
}

  "url": "https://gitlab.com/husk-project/husk-milter/-/raw/main/README.md",

  "truncated": false,
  "content": "# Husk\n\nHusk is a pass-through solution for automatic OpenPGP encryption.\n\nHusk is an email filter (milter) for MTAs which encrypts emails while they pass through it. It facilitates Web-of-Trust technologies to use decentralized and federated certificate authorities as sources for authenticated OpenPGP certificates. It aims to reduce the amount of administrative effort of obtaining and keeping them up to date by establishing narrow, focused trust delegations. Husk can be used to encrypt emails for services which cannot encrypt on their own – like notification systems or issue trackers, or being used at the end of transport to implement zero-access encryption (for email at rest).\n\nHusk is written in Rust and uses Sequoia PGP for encryption and certificate handling.\n\n## Notable Features\n\n- Opportunistic email encryption using `OpenPGPv4` or `OpenPGPv6` (configurable).\n- Integration into the email processing flow via the `sendmail-milter` protocol.\n- Downloading of certificates from online sources on demand.\n- Uses the Web-of-Trust by using `introducers`, allowing a fine grade of trust delegation.\n- Periodically update all local certificates by querying online sources (keyservers, etc).\n- Optionally tunnel all requests to online sources through the Tor-network.\n- Statistics collection.\n- Test how Husk will treat email addresses by probing the running process.\n\nHusk allows for a smooth entry. Without `introducers` and `locals` (see the\n[documentation](https://www.husk-project.org/documentation) for details) Husk will\nnot modify any passing email. This allows you to define `introducers` and\n`locals` step by step and examine their influence on your email traffic.\nHusk is not an all-or-nothing solution.\n\n## Documentation\n\nThe documentation (with install instructions) can be found [here](https://www.husk-project.org/documentation).\n\n## Issues\n\nPlease report any issues (or missing features) at the\n[issue tracker](https://gitlab.com/husk-project/husk-milter/-/work_items?sort=created_date&state=opened&first_page_size=20)\nof the project.\n\n## Website\n\nThis project has a [website](https://www.husk-project.org/) with more background\ninformation. The website also has contact information.\n\n## Funding\n\nThis project is funded through the [NGI0 Commons Fund](https://nlnet.nl/core), a fund established by [NLnet](https://nlnet.nl/).\n\n<table>\n    <tr>\n        <td>\n            <img src=\"https://nlnet.nl/logo/banner.svg\" style=\"max-width: 250px;width: 75%\" alt=\"NLnet\">\n        </td>\n        <td>\n            <img src=\"https://nlnet.nl/image/logos/NGI0Core_tag.svg\" style=\"max-width: 250px;width: 75%\" alt=\"NLnet\">\n        </td>\n    </tr>\n</table>\n\n## License\n\nHusk is licensed under AGPL 3.0 - for details read the included `license.txt` file or find it [online](https://www.gnu.org/licenses/agpl-3.0.html)."
}
#+end_tool
Good find — it's a real, current project, and it's not a fourth architecture. It's the third one (milter, before-queue) already built, by someone who reached the same conclusions. Roughly a year old: NLnet NGI0 Commons Fund, start 2025-10, milestone 7 in late 2026, Rust + Sequoia PGP, AGPL.

/What it is conceptually/

Two things, and only one of them is about encryption.

1. /A milter./ Same slot as pymilter, same properties: in-process during the SMTP transaction, cleartext never reaches the spool, no re-injection loop, failure is an SMTP code. It also does the MIME surgery you'd otherwise hand-roll: the subject is moved into the encrypted part and replaced with a placeholder (=subject_replacement=, default =...=). That's memory-hole / protected-headers shape, i.e. it's producing real PGP/MIME, not an armored blob. That's precisely the layer =gpg -e= couldn't give you. So this closes the "make gpg be enough" thread by refusing the premise: don't make gpg do MIME — use a library that already knows MIME, and drop the gpg invocation entirely.

2. /A trust-graph query./ This is the actual idea, and it's orthogonal to mail. Whether to encrypt is decided by asking: is there an authenticated certificate for this recipient? Authentication is a path in a WoT graph from a trust root through /introducers/, with edges carrying trust depth, trust amount, and a domain regex. Your input is one line — "trust this CA for =example.com=" — instead of a curated keyring. Certificates are fetched on demand (keyservers, WKD, DANE, optionally via Tor) and refreshed in the background.

/Why that's the wrong policy for you/

Notice what it does in the negative case: if no authenticated cert is found, /it passes the mail through unmodified/. The docs call this "opportunistic encryption." That's a deliberate fail-open choice — availability over confidentiality — and it's the exact opposite of the verdict from earlier in this conversation, where the only correct behavior when gpg can't do its job was refuse/defer. A milter that silently delivers cleartext when confused is not a control, it's a best-effort convenience. If your requirement is "this must be encrypted," opportunistic is not a weaker version of that requirement; it's a different requirement.

You can force it into your shape with =locals= — register your one public key as a local certificate and skip introducers entirely (locals need no authentication). That works, and it's the minimal viable config: no keyservers, no updater, no Tor, no WoT. But then you are running three daemons (milter, updater, monitor) to reach a single-key outcome, and the 80% of the project that organizes distributed trust is solving a problem you don't have. The 20% you do want — milter plumbing plus correct MIME — is real value, and it's the part you cannot reasonably write yourself.

/Other things that bite, specific to your setup/

- /Fail-open again, structurally./ The daemon isn't started by Postfix; if it's down, =milter_default_action= decides, and default is tempfail but many people set accept. Combined with opportunistic policy, there are two independent paths to silently unencrypted mail. Check both.
- /Recipient splitting./ If only some recipients are encryptable, Husk emits a second message for the rest — it re-injects and munges the envelope after all, just internally (=injected_mail= in the counters). Moot for one recipient, but it means the encryption boundary is per-recipient, not per-message. Any future alias or Bcc quietly creates a plaintext twin.
- /v4 vs v6 is a live footgun./ Certificates advertising OpenPGPv6 capabilities yield v6 messages that GnuPG and Thunderbird can't read; =rfc4880_profile = true= forces v4 and is the current default, and the docs themselves call the workaround "problematic... sabotages the upgrade path." With notmuch + GnuPG on the client, you need v4 forced. Leave the default alone, know why it's there.
- /DKIM./ Any body-rewriting milter destroys the sender's signature. Order matters — verify DKIM first, encrypt second. And Husk signing (if you configure a signing key) is not DKIM and doesn't help deliverability; it's a verifiable "this was encrypted by my server" statement.
- /notmuch side./ This is the first proposal in this conversation that actually matches your client: =multipart/encrypted= plus a hidden subject is what =notmuch show --decrypt= was built for. But the placeholder subject lands in your index, so full-text search on subject/body requires =index.decrypt = true= — which stores the cleartext (and stashes session keys) locally, partly undoing the at-rest property you went to all this trouble for. That tension doesn't go away with Husk; it moves to the client.
- /Youth./ One team, grant-funded, AGPL, ~a year of history, single-digit contributors. Fine for a home MTA you can debug; a real consideration if inbound mail must not stop.

/Verdict/

Right architecture, wrong policy. Husk is the honest version of where this conversation was heading — before-queue, MIME-aware, no gpg subprocess, no shell, no temp files, no re-injection dance — and it's the only option discussed that gives GnuPG/notmuch something they can actually decrypt. But it's built to answer "encrypt when I can prove who the recipient is," and you asked "always encrypt to this one key." Those differ in what happens when the machinery is uncertain, and Husk's answer is "send it in the clear."

If you still want a single hardcoded recipient with fail-closed semantics, the small =postfix-gpg-milter= (Go) or a pymilter you own is closer to your stated intent. If you'd rather adopt a maintained implementation and accept opportunistic semantics plus a young codebase, Husk with =locals= and no introducers is the pragmatic choice — just make =milter_default_action = tempfail= and monitor =unencrypted_rcpt= / =encryptable= counters, because those are the numbers that tell you whether your mail is actually being encrypted or just being passed through.

*** so in what case would husk not encrypt mail? When it's already encrypted?


#+begin_reasoning
Let me answer this carefully based on what I actually read from the docs. Let me enumerate the cases from the documentation.

From the theory page and docs:

1. No authenticated certificate for any recipient → opportunistic, pass through unmodified. Actually: "If for all recipients an authenticated certificate is available, the email will be encrypted... If no certificate is found, the email will not be modified." Partial → second email for unencryptable recipients.

2. Already encrypted → e2e_encrypted_mail counter. It doesn't re-encrypt. Presumably detected by Content-Type: multipart/encrypted.

3. Encryption failed due to unsupported certificate → encryption_error counter. Hmm — what happens then? Does it pass through or reject? The docs don't say explicitly. This is an important gap: "encryption_error: Number of attempts an encryption failed due to an unsupported certificate." It's ambiguous whether it then sends unencrypted.

4. No introducer for the domain → no query, no cert, no encryption (the probe output showed "If there are no lines starting with introduced by, then the domain... has no introducer").

5. Throttled — history of failed queries means it won't retry, so no cert found → no encryption.

6. Certificate problems: expired, revoked → not usable (locals/introducer add requires "valid certificate, not expired and not revoked"). A cert that expires later would fail validation.

7. Crypto policy rejects algorithms (deprecated SHA1, weak key) → would count in encryption_error likely.

8. Multiple recipients mixed → split, one message encrypted, one plaintext.

9. Recipients not covered by any introducer/domain regex.

10. Milter down / unavailable → Postfix's milter_default_action decides (this is a genuinely important "not encrypt" case, and it's silent if accept).

11. Config ordering: if Husk isn't in the non_smtpd_milters or the right smtpd service, mail isn't filtered at all. Also note the docs recommend `non_smtpd_milters = $smtpd_milters` — which means locally submitted mail ALSO gets encrypted. Hmm, wait. That's interesting: that means outbound mail you send yourself also passes through Husk. Interesting interaction — for outgoing mail to external recipients, no cert → pass through. But for your own address, it would encrypt.

Actually that's a notable point: Husk's own docs set non_smtpd_milters = $smtpd_milters, which means it filters locally-submitted mail too. That could double-encrypt your own outbound to someone whose key it has, and also could encrypt mail you send to yourself.

12. Also: mail with no recipients / empty recipient list? Edge case — a milter transaction where all RCPTs were... no, Postfix won't accept no RCPT.

13. Split handling: injected_mail counter.

14. Also potential: if message is already signed-only but not encrypted → it'd try to encrypt. Not a non-encrypt case.

15. Header size / timeout? Not documented.

16. Also: subject_replacement — it always replaces subject when encrypting.

The user asks specifically "When it's already encrypted?" — So the answer: yes, that's one case (e2e_encrypted_mail), but it's the least interesting one. The important cases are the fail-open ones. And crucially — I should note that I don't know from the docs what happens after encryption_error, and I should be honest about that uncertainty rather than inventing behavior. - Fail-open (silently plaintext): no cert, no introducer, no WKD/DANE entry, throttled, policy rejection, encryption error(?)
- Not-reached-at-all: milter down, not registered, ordering

And add the conceptual point: the taxonomy is "what counts as 'I can't encrypt?'" — Husk has several distinct answers, and most of them mean "deliver anyway." The counters in the monitor exist precisely because you cannot tell from the outside which happened.

Also worth noting: the split case means mail IS encrypted for some recipients — so "did Husk encrypt?" is per-recipient, not per-message.

#+end_reasoning

Almost, but it's the least interesting case of several. "Already encrypted" is the one case where not encrypting is unambiguously correct. The rest of the list is where the fail-open policy lives.

/The intentional case/

- /Already PGP/MIME encrypted/ (=e2e_encrypted_mail=). It detects =multipart/encrypted= and passes it through. Re-encrypting would nest ciphertext and break the sender's end-to-end property. Correct behavior. It's a counter in =monitor readout= specifically so you can see it happening.

/The fail-open cases — mail delivered in the clear because Husk can't prove who to encrypt to/

- /No authenticated certificate for the recipient./ Either none exists locally, or none was found on WKD/DANE/keyservers. Docs are explicit: "If no certificate is found, the email will not be modified."
- /No introducer configured for that domain./ The =probe= output spells this out: no "introduced by" line means the domain has no introducer, so no query is even attempted. This is the default state for a fresh install — with no introducers and no locals, Husk modifies nothing at all.
- /Queries throttled./ Failed lookups are remembered for =throttle_max_age= (default 3600s), so a recipient whose cert was missing an hour ago won't even be re-queried. The probe reports "currently throttled."
- /Certificate present but unusable/ — expired, revoked, or rejected by the Sequoia crypto policy (deprecated hashes like SHA-1, weak keys). These land in =encryption_error=, and here the docs are genuinely silent: they define the counter ("attempts an encryption failed due to an unsupported certificate") but never state whether the message is then sent in the clear or rejected. I'd assume pass-through given the opportunistic framing, but I don't know that, and you shouldn't either — it's worth testing before you rely on it.

/Partial cases/

- /Mixed recipients./ Only some encryptable → Husk emits a second, cleartext message for the rest (=injected_mail=). So "did Husk encrypt this mail?" is a per-recipient question, not a per-message one — and a Bcc or an alias expansion can silently create a plaintext twin.
- /Outbound, if you follow their config verbatim./ They recommend =non_smtpd_milters = $smtpd_milters=, so locally submitted mail is filtered too. For most outbound there's no cert, so it passes through; but mail you send to a keyed recipient gets encrypted, including mail to yourself.

/Not reached at all — nothing to do with Husk's logic/

- /Daemon down, and =milter_default_action= is =accept=./ Postfix delivers unencrypted and logs a warning. Default is =tempfail=, but plenty of tutorials set =accept= to "fix" milter errors.
- /Not registered for that service./ Filtering is configured per-milter-list per-smtpd-service; mail arriving on a listener you didn't add =-o smtpd_milters= to is simply never offered to Husk.
- /Ordering./ If another body-rewriting milter runs before Husk and trips something, or if the message never reaches the =eom= stage.

/The distinction that matters/

So the taxonomy is really "what does Husk count as /can't/?" and the answer is broad: unknown key, untrusted key, throttled lookup, weak algorithm, expired cert. Only one of those — already encrypted — is a case where delivering is the right call. The others are cases where your preference (deliver) and your stated goal (always encrypt) conflict, and Husk resolves them toward delivery.

Which is why those counters are the actual monitor: =encrypted_rcpt= vs =unencrypted_rcpt= vs =encryption_error=. If =unencrypted_rcpt= is nonzero for legitimate correspondents, your introducers or locals are misconfigured and mail is going out in the clear while everything looks healthy. For your single-key setup with =locals=, that number should be zero for your own address, and any deviation is a bug, not a policy.

The question: "would configuring husk correctly prevent from unencrypted mail reaching the disk?"

Key concept: milter is before-queue, so if Husk encrypts, cleartext never lands in the postfix queue... but there are subtle caveats. Let me think carefully about what actually happens architecturally.

Facts to reason about:
1. Milter = before-queue. smtpd streams DATA to the milter as it arrives; the milter gets header/body chunks. Postfix does NOT write to queue file until after EOM and milter replies. So if milter encrypts at eom via replacebody, the queue file gets the ciphertext. Cleartext was in smtpd's memory (and milter's memory), never on disk... 

BUT: important caveats:
- Postfix may write the message to disk before milter decisions? Actually no — before-queue content inspection happens in smtpd, and the queue file is written by cleanup after. However, if smtpd proxies... no.
- However: if the milter connection times out / errors with tempfail, message is not queued. Good.
- BUT there's the case where the message is rejected after DATA? no.
- Actually key caveat: Postfix's smtpd does buffer the message somewhere? For before-queue milters, smtpd streams to milter and then to cleanup. No disk. But there IS `smtpd_proxy_filter` alternative which writes... no.
- Note: milter protocol has chunked body; pymilter holds in memory. Postfix doesn't spool.

But there are real disk paths:
1. **Swap** — cleartext in smtpd/milter RAM can be swapped to disk. Husk holds plaintext in memory; if the machine swaps, cleartext hits disk. Mitigate: no swap / encrypted swap / mlock.
2. **Crash dumps / core dumps** — same.
3. **Journald/logs** — debug logging could log message content? Husk logs metadata presumably. But careful with debug level.
4. **The client side** — notmuch indexing, mbsync local Maildir: mail downloaded to client is ciphertext, but `index.decrypt=true` writes cleartext index; attachments decrypted to temp; MUAs writing cleartext copies. That's the disk that matters for the earlier threat model.
5. **Husk's own certificate store** — no cleartext mail.
6. **Backups of the queue** — no.
7. **Postfix queue in the delivery chain**: If Husk is configured on smtpd_milters for inbound, cleartext never queued. But if mail is delivered locally, the local mailbox is ciphertext. Good.
8. **BUT** — crucial: husk's fail-open. If it doesn't encrypt, cleartext goes to disk. So "configured correctly" is doing a lot of work: you must ensure unencrypted_rcpt == 0, that means *every* recipient must have a locally trusted cert. Since it's opportunistic per-recipient, and mail arrives from arbitrary senders to arbitrary recipients, "always encrypt" is only achieved by a **catch-all local key** — but Husk encrypts per recipient address. If your domain has multiple addresses (postmaster@, abuse@, MAILER-DAEMON bounces) without certs, those pass through in the clear. That's a real gap.
9. **Bounces/DSNs and internally generated mail**: Postfix generates bounces/notifications internally. Inbound bounces aren't filtered by content filters "Postfix normally does not apply content filters to mail that is generated internally such as bounces or Postmaster notifications" — and those *do* get written to disk (queue) and delivered. Their content includes the original message's headers, and for a DSN of a failed outbound message, potentially content. Also `internal_mail_filter_classes`.
10. **Non-SMTP path**: mail submitted locally via sendmail / cron / local notification systems enters via pickup(8), and is only filtered if non_smtpd_milters is set. Their thread is about inbound though.
11. **Pre-encryption window in memory only** — so "reaching the disk" = essentially no, except swap.
12. **Postfix writes the message to disk in the queue if the milter says tempfail? No, it keeps it in... Actually if milter tempfails mid-DATA, Postfix responds 4xx and drops the message; sender retries. Nothing on disk. Good.
13. **BUT**: Husk splits recipients for partial encryption, injecting a new message. That injected message goes through... it's re-injected via the milter? Docs say "injected_mail: Number of times a new email had to be send because the list of recipients had to be split". It presumably re-injects via sendmail or alters RCPTs. The *plaintext* copy for unencryptable recipients definitely hits disk.
14. Also — does Postfix write DATA to disk before milters when `smtpd_milters`? No. But there's a subtlety: **smtpd has a 300s content timeout**; if Husk times out, milter_default_action applies. Default tempfail → nothing on disk, mail deferred at sender. Good, that's fail-closed... but the message content is held in smtpd memory, and on timeout Postfix... nothing.

Hmm, but also: **Postfix's `cleanup` server writes the queue file after milters return.** So yes ciphertext.

15. **Another important one**: If you use both `smtpd_milters` and something like a mail archiving/local delivery that adds cleartext copies... The IMAP server stores ciphertext. Good.
16. **Sieve/vacation/notmuch-index on server**: If server-side indexing (e.g., Dovecot FTS, or a spam filter before Husk) reads cleartext, that's another copy. Also **spam/virus scanning before Husk** typically writes temp files to disk! E.g., rspamd/amavis/clamsgd processes plaintext and may write temp files. If Husk runs after a scanner that copies to /tmp, cleartext hit disk already. Order matters: Husk first? But then scanners can't scan. Tension.
17. **Logs**: Postfix logs queue ID + sender/recipient; mail headers like Subject are not logged. Fine.
18. **`postcat`/queue backups** no.

Also worth noting: at-rest encryption on the *server* doesn't protect the hop from sender→your MTA: the message was transmitted in plaintext over SMTP (unless TLS/STARTTLS), stored in sender's Sent folder, and sat in the sending MTA's spool. Husk only protects the last stretch and storage from that point onward. So "unencrypted mail reaching *your* disk" — yes prevented (modulo swap/temp), but "the message existed in cleartext elsewhere" — unavoidable.

Also: the **first hop / provider spool**: if you rent a VPS, the mail transits their network.

Let me also consider: does Postfix write the message to the *hold* queue if milter quarantines? Husk doesn't quarantine.

What about **`header_checks`/`body_checks`**? Not relevant.

What about **smtpd's `smtpd_proxy_filter`**? Not used.

One more real one: **The queue file is written by cleanup before delivery**. During Husk's encryption at eom, the message exists in smtpd's memory. But Postfix's smtpd forks a cleanup process and pipes... yes.

Actually — carefully: does smtpd write to a temp file to hand off to cleanup? Postfix smtpd sends the DATA content to cleanup via a socket; cleanup writes the queue file. The milter intercepts before? Order in Postfix: smtpd receives DATA → sends headers to cleanup? Hmm. Milter runs in smtpd, and the content passed to cleanup is what the milter returned. Actually Postfix's smtpd buffers the entire message? No — smtpd streams chunks to cleanup AND to milter? Let me think: In Postfix, smtpd receives the message and forwards it via `cleanup` after milter processing. The milter's replacebody at eom requires the whole body to be buffered by smtpd (or libmilter buffers it). Yes — libmilter buffers the message. So smtpd/libmilter holds the whole message in memory. Then after EOM, the modified content is sent to cleanup.

So: memory-only, plus libmilter temp? No, libmilter keeps in memory.

Important caveat: **Postfix's smtpd may write to disk if the milter buffers overflow?** libmilter has limits but stores in memory.

OK. So the honest answer: Architecturally yes — that's the whole point of before-queue, and it's the one property the pipe/content_filter approach cannot give you. But "correctly configured" has to include a list of things, and there are residual disk paths that aren't Husk's fault:

Residuals:
- swap / hibernation / core dumps
- DSNs and internally-generated mail (not filtered by milters)
- other software in the pipeline that touches the message before/after (spam filter, AV, archiver, server-side FTS, sieve scripts) — many write temp files

- the plaintext twin for unencryptable recipients (partial encryption)
- the pre-Husk hop: sender's MTA spool, transit, your VPS host's network/disk snapshots
